### [1] [EXAONE Forecast for Finance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04239)
*Seunghan Lee,Jaehoon Lee,Jun Seo,Tae Yoon Lim,Dongwan Kang,Hwanil Choi,Minjae Kim,Sungdong Yoo,Junhyeok Kang,Sangjun Han,Soonyoung Lee,Wonbin Ahn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EXAONE Finance is a specialized financial time series foundation model using attention-free linear-time operators that achieves state-of-the-art performance across financial forecasting tasks by being pretrained on diverse financial data and handling missing values effectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current time series foundation models have limitations for financial applications: they use computationally expensive self-attention (quadratic complexity), assume fully observed inputs, and are pretrained on general-domain data that doesn't capture unique financial market dynamics. Financial data often involves long, many-channel, intermittently observed panels that need specialized handling.

Method: The model replaces self-attention with two linear-time operators: 1) causal 1D convolution for temporal mixing, and 2) group-aware pooling MLP for variate mixing. It uses masked context augmentation during training to handle missing data patterns common in financial markets. The model is pretrained on a large-scale financial corpus covering multiple asset classes.

Result: EXAONE Finance achieves state-of-the-art performance on the FinVerse benchmark, ranking first across all three evaluation tiers: point-forecast accuracy, cross-sectional asset ranking, and portfolio profitability.

Conclusion: The attention-free architecture with specialized linear-time operators and financial data pretraining enables superior performance for financial forecasting tasks, overcoming limitations of general-domain time series foundation models.

Abstract: This technical report presents EXAONE Forecast for Finance (EXAONE Finance), a financial time series (TS) foundation model (TSFM) tailored to financial forecasting. Recent TSFMs achieve strong zero-shot performance through large-scale pretraining. However, they are primarily developed for general-domain TS and largely rely on self-attention backbones whose computational cost grows quadratically with sequence length and variate count. Moreover, they assume fully observed inputs and are pretrained on corpora that fail to capture the unique dynamics of financial markets. These limitations hinder their applicability to finance, where long, many-channel, intermittently observed panels are common. To address these challenges, EXAONE Finance adopts an attention-free architecture, replacing self-attention with two simple yet effective linear-time operators: 1) a causal 1D convolution for temporal mixing and 2) a group-aware pooling multi-layer perceptron (MLP) for variate mixing. Furthermore, a masked context augmentation exposes the model to contiguous missing spans during training, improving robustness to the missingness pervasive in financial markets. EXAONE Finance is pretrained on a large-scale financial corpus covering not only equities but also foreign exchange, commodities, crypto-assets, fixed income, and macroeconomic indicators. On FinVerse, a financial forecasting benchmark covering diverse asset classes, EXAONE Finance attains state-of-the-art performance, ranking first across all three evaluation tiers---point-forecast accuracy, cross-sectional asset ranking, and portfolio profitability.

</details>


### [2] [From Matching Models to Recruiting Agents: A Systematized Narrative Review of AI Recruitment Systems, Evaluation, and Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04286)
*Ziyi Zhao,Guanzheng Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reviews the evolution of AI in recruitment from simple matching models to complex multi-stage workflows, analyzing transitions in approach and identifying persistent evaluation gaps while proposing a framework for more evidence-based and accountable systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to systematically understand how AI in recruitment has evolved from simple bilateral matching to complex workflows, and to address persistent gaps in evaluation where current systems often conflate different types of evidence and lack comprehensive assessment of utility, fairness, privacy, and security.

Method: The authors conducted a systematized narrative review using a purposive search and coding protocol, analyzing 40 representative works plus industrial and legal sources. They organized findings to analyze three key transitions in recruitment AI approaches.

Result: The review identifies three coupled transitions: from similarity to reciprocal suitability, from single models to compound workflows, and from offline prediction to evidence- and productivity-aligned evaluation. It reveals persistent gaps including confounding behavioral labels, limited external validity from private/synthetic data, pipeline failures concealed by final scores, and lack of joint evaluation of utility, fairness, privacy, and security.

Conclusion: The paper proposes a staged mapping from evaluation evidence to strongest defensible claims and an agenda for more reciprocal, evidence-grounded, temporally controlled, selective, and auditable recruitment systems. Progress should be judged by whether workflows retrieve the right evidence, preserve uncertainty, support contestable decisions, and improve outcomes under explicit constraints.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence in recruitment has shifted the object being automated from profile pairs and ranked lists to multi-stage workflows that retrieve evidence, compare candidates, and support or execute actions. This systematized narrative review traces that development from bilateral retrieval and behavioral ranking through neural person--job matching, large language model (LLM) components, and tool-using recruiting agents. Using a purposive search and coding protocol updated through 23 July 2026, plus targeted updates through 2 September 2026, we organize 40 representative works with supporting industrial and legal sources. This synthesis is not a prevalence estimate. We analyze three coupled transitions: from similarity to reciprocal suitability, from a model to a compound workflow, and from offline prediction to evidence- and productivity-aligned evaluation. Across document understanding, retrieval, ranking, assessment, interviewing, sourcing, and human handoff, we distinguish field-, pair-, list-, case-, trajectory-, and outcome-level evidence. Persistent gaps arise because behavioral labels confound exposure, preference, and qualification; private and synthetic data limit external validity; final-output scores conceal pipeline failures; and, within the coded set, privacy is not directly evaluated and no row jointly evaluates utility, fairness, privacy, and security. These observations describe the coded set rather than the field as a whole. We therefore introduce a staged mapping from evaluation evidence to the strongest defensible claim, together with an agenda for reciprocal, evidence-grounded, temporally controlled, selective, and auditable systems. Progress should be judged by whether workflows retrieve the right evidence, preserve uncertainty, support contestable decisions, and improve outcomes under explicit cost and risk constraints.

</details>


### [3] [Harbor Adapters and Harbor-Index: Infrastructure and a Curated Meta-Dataset for Large-Scale Agentic Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04298)
*Lin Shi,Haowei Lin,Zixuan Zhu,Xiaoyue Zhou,Xiang Li,Xiangning Lin,Yaxuan Deng,Han Xu,Yuangang Li,Shanda Li,Zizhao Chen,Hanwen Xing,Harsh Raj,Bo Chen,Quan Shi,Steven Dillmann,Yipeng Gao,Puneesh Khanna,Ruofan Lu,Chao Beyond Zhou,Michael Yang,Robert Zhang,Siyuan Chai,Jiayu Chang,Yizhao Chen,Xiaokun Chen,Yiwei Dai,Wenting Yang,Hange Liu,Minghao Liu,Zihan Wang,Adnan El Assadi,Benedikt Stroebl,E. Kelly Buchanan,Han Meng,Junwei He,Longxuan Yu,Radin Shayanfar,Yukyung Lee,Zhikang Dong,Allen G Hart,Anjiang Wei,Anurag Kashyap,Arpandeep Khatua,Audrey Jixin Zheng,Chengrui Ma,David Heineman,Dubing Chen,Hai-Anh Trinh,Haishuo Fang,Hefan Zhang,Hui Shen,Issa Sugiura,Jiankai Sun,Jiechao Gao,Junhong Lin,Junnan Li,Kai Yang,Lei Hsiung,Maoyu Wang,Mengze Tang,Nabil Omi,Negin Raoof,Nicholas Edwards,Octavia Guo,Orfeas Menis Mastromichalakis,Pengliang Ji,Przemysław Hejman,Qi Qi,Qunshu Lin,Richard Zhuang,Rui Yang,Ruichen Zheng,Ryan Marten,Shaghayegh Fazliani,Shizheng Hou,Sicong Jiang,Sijie Li,Song Bian,Terry Yue Zhuo,Tianqing Wu,Tom Tang,Wanjia Zhao,Weihao Xuan,Wenhua Liang,Xian Liu,Xin Lan,Xuan Zhang,Xuandong Zhao,Yanchuan Tang,Yifan Jiang,Yijiang Li,Yitong Guan,Yizhi Li,Yonghui Liu,Yuheng Tang,Yujun,Mao,Yunfei Zhao,Yuxin Wang,Yuxuan Tang,Zhenheng Tang,Zhifei Li,Ziruo Wang,Ziyu She,Kaiyuan Liu,Iheb Chaabane,Yuxin Tang,Xiangyi Li,Andy Konwinski,Boxuan Li,Leon Liangyu Chen,Alex Dimakis,Nicholas Carlini,Soroush Vosoughi,Di He,Etash Guha,Benjamin Feuer,Mike Merrill,Ludwig Schmidt,Alex Shaw*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Harbor Adapters provides a unified infrastructure for evaluating language-model agents across multiple benchmarks, featuring benchmark adapters for 80+ tests, large-scale evaluation of 8 models, and a curated Harbor-Index of 82 difficult tasks with open-source release.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating agents on growing agentic benchmarks is challenging due to complex environments and agent integrations, creating a need for unified evaluation infrastructure.

Method: Developed benchmark adapters to port 80+ benchmarks, validated through code review and parity experiments; conducted large-scale evaluation of 8 models across 54 benchmarks with Terminus-2 and native harnesses; created Harbor-Index through difficulty filtering, AI/human audit, and audit-fix loops.

Result: Enabled broader analysis of agent capabilities and failure modes; Harbor-Index contains 82 difficult, diverse, high-quality tasks across 29 benchmarks; no model-harness configuration exceeds 30% pass rate; strongest model (GPT-5.5 with Codex) reaches 28.0% pass rate.

Conclusion: Harbor Adapters supports more reliable and comprehensive evaluation of language-model agents; all adapters, evaluation results, analysis, and Harbor-Index are released as open-source artifacts to benefit the research community.

Abstract: Evaluating agents on the growing number of agentic benchmarks is challenging because they often require complex environments and agent integrations. We introduce Harbor Adapters, a unified evaluation infrastructure for agentic benchmarks. Our work makes three contributions. First, we develop benchmark adapters that port more than 80 benchmarks to evaluate arbitrary agents, and validate them through rigorous code review and parity experiments. Second, we conduct a large-scale evaluation of 8 models spanning capability tiers across 54 benchmarks; every model is run with Terminus-2 and with one of 3 native harnesses. This enables a broader analysis of agent capabilities and failure modes than was previously possible. Third, we introduce Harbor-Index, a curated set of 82 difficult, diverse, and high-quality tasks spanning 29 benchmarks, refined from the adapted suite through difficulty filtering, AI and human audit, and an audit-and-fix loop. Harbor-Index preserves the challenge and breadth of large-scale agentic evaluations while being affordable to run; no evaluated model-harness configuration exceeds 30% pass rate, and the strongest (GPT-5.5 with Codex) reaches 28.0%. We release the adapters, evaluation results, in-depth analysis, and Harbor-Index as open-source artifacts to support more reliable and comprehensive evaluation of language-model agents.

</details>


### [4] [La Agente Óptima: Towards Agentic Self-Driving Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04564)
*Marcel Müller,Jiaru Bai,Willi Gottstein,Abhijoy Mandal,Mohammad Nazeri,Elia Savino,Yanlin Fang,Sujoy Das,Sergio Pablo García Carrillo,Yeonghun Kang,Juan B. Pérez-Sánchez,Simone Pilon,Martin Fitzner,Timothy Noël,Frank Gu,Varinia Bernales,Alán Aspuru-Guzik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: La Agente Óptima is an agentic framework that uses LLMs to autonomously construct and supervise Bayesian optimization campaigns in self-driving laboratories, reducing dependency on human specialists while maintaining rigorous, auditable experimental control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) currently depend heavily on human specialists to translate scientific objectives into executable closed-loop campaigns and adjust them as conditions change. This creates a bottleneck and limits accessibility of SDLs to domain scientists without specialist expertise.

Method: Óptima is an agentic framework that separates LLM reasoning from executed campaigns. It runs repetitive optimization loops consistently, returns control to the agent only when progress requires interpretation or campaign revision, and maintains a persistent optimization state with auditable decisions. The framework was evaluated across ablation studies, five digital discovery tasks, and two physical platforms.

Result: Óptima successfully maintained executable campaigns as problems and execution environments evolved. In a contact angle optimization, it identified and corrected a mid-run measurement failure, achieving 67.8 degrees vs. 71.4 degrees baseline, and correctly inferred target unattainability. In a 5-day multi-objective flow-chemistry campaign, it increased yield from 30% to 59% over 23 experiments. Despite LLM inference costs, it was more cost-effective and mass-efficient than human-directed campaigns.

Conclusion: LLM-based agents like Óptima can make rigorous, long-running optimization campaigns accessible to domain scientists without specialist setup, expanding the scope and accessibility of self-driving laboratories while maintaining experimental rigor and auditability.

Abstract: Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) combine automated experimentation with adaptive decision-making to accelerate scientific discovery. Their operation nevertheless often depends on human specialists who translate scientific objectives into executable closed-loop campaigns. Specialists adjust them as data and operating conditions change. Here, we present La Agente Óptima, an agentic framework that constructs and supervises Bayesian optimization campaigns across computational and experimental systems while maintaining a persistent optimization state. By separating large language model (LLM) reasoning from executed campaigns, Óptima runs repetitive optimization loops consistently, returns control to the agent only when progress requires interpretation or campaign revision, and keeps every decision auditable. We evaluate Óptima across ablation studies, five digital discovery tasks, and two physical platforms. Throughout, Óptima maintained executable campaigns as both the scientific problem and execution environment evolved. In a closed-loop contact angle optimization campaign, Óptima identified and corrected a mid-run measurement failure, bringing the contact angle from 71.4 to 67.8 degrees, just above the 64-66 degree range. From this result, Óptima correctly inferred that the target was likely unattainable with the available reagents and recommended changing the formulation. In a five-day multi-objective flow-chemistry campaign, Óptima increased the yield from 30% to 59% over 23 experiments. Despite substantial inference costs, it cost less and used substantially less starting material than a human-directed campaign, while selecting a more mass-efficient operating point. These results show that LLM-based agents can make rigorous, long-running optimization campaigns accessible to domain scientists without specialist setup, expanding the scope of SDLs.

</details>


### [5] [Data-Optimized Contingency Screening: A Machine Learning Approach to Power System Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04300)
*Joshua Salako,Folajimi Osikomaiya,Olakorede Olamiju*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Machine learning algorithms (KNN, RF, SVM) with SMOTE and PCA preprocessing are used to classify power system contingencies into safe, moderate, or severe security levels, achieving high performance with Random Forest as the best performer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensuring power system security is essential for stability and reliability. Effective contingency classification enables proactive decision-making and prevents large-scale breakdowns, addressing the need for real-time security assessment beyond traditional methods.

Method: Used Newton-Raphson load flow to extract system data from N-k contingency scenarios (k=1,2,3) on IEEE-14 and IEEE-30 bus systems, with Overall Performance Index as safety measure. Applied SMOTE for class imbalance and PCA for dimensionality reduction. Trained and evaluated KNN, RF, and SVM with four hybrid preprocessing configurations: normalized, SMOTE-balanced, PCA-transformed, and combined SMOTE-PCA-transformed.

Result: Random Forest achieved highest F1 scores: 0.97 in IEEE-30 and 0.86 in IEEE-14. SVM benefited significantly from PCA for accuracy improvement. KNN performed best with SMOTE and PCA conversion. PCA contributed more to overall performance than SMOTE, though SMOTE improved recall at the cost of potential false positives.

Conclusion: Machine learning provides a scalable and powerful alternative to traditional contingency analysis, enabling improved real-time security assessment of power systems through effective classification of contingencies into security levels.

Abstract: Ensuring the security of the power system is essential for stability and reliability, especially in the event of disruption. Effective classification of contingency in power systems enables proactive decision-making and mitigates large-scale breakdowns and failures. This study explores the use of machine learning algorithms to classify security levels of contingencies in power systems into safe, moderate or severe classes. For this approach, Newton-Raphson load flow method extracts system data from contingency scenarios, using Overall Performance Index (OPI) as safety measure. For data pre-processing, Synthetic Minority Over-Sampling Technique (SMOTE) and Principal Component Analysis (PCA) is used to address class imbalance and reduce dimensionality, respectively. K-Nearest Neighbours (KNN), Random Forest (RF) and Support Vector Machines (SVM) is trained and evaluated on datasets generated through N-k contingency scenarios for k equal 1, 2, and 3 on IEEE-14 and IEEE-30 bus systems using four hybrid pre-processing configurations: normalized, SMOTE-balanced, PCA-transformed, and a combined SMOTE PCA-transformed. Performance is assessed by precision, recall and F1 score, with priority given to the severe contingency classes. The RF achieved the highest F1 scores of 0.97 in IEEE-30 and 0.86 in IEEE-14, SVM benefits significantly from PCA and improves the accuracy of the classification, while KNN is best suited for SMOTE and PCA conversion. The findings show that PCA contributes more than SMOTE to the overall performance of the model. However, SMOTE improves recall but can introduce false positives and is therefore a compromise of accuracy. This study highlights machine learning as a scalable and powerful alternative to traditional contingency analysis, which improves the assessment of security in real time.

</details>


### [6] [From Language Models to World-Acting Systems: Progress and Limits of Agentic AI across Digital, Social, Virtual, and Physical Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04894)
*Linsen Zhu,Mengqing Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper critically reviews advances in AI agency, proposing "justified delegation" as a framework that separates model competence from system safety, advocating for evidence-based expansion of AI action scope with proper safety controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current narratives around AI agency conflate model capabilities with system safety, persistence, and authority. There's a need to critically evaluate claims about AI autonomy and establish proper frameworks for safe delegation of authority to AI systems.

Method: Critical review synthesizing primary research and official technical specifications available by August 31, 2026, organized along dimensions of delegated authority, temporal persistence, and environmental coupling, while separating model, harness, and environment components.

Result: The review finds that action-interface expansion is better documented than robust completion, recovery, authorization, or independent verification. Existing protocols improve interoperability but don't establish trustworthy delegation. Multi-agent systems add specialization but also increase costs and correlated failure risks. While persistent simulations support training, they don't demonstrate real agency, and physical systems show bounded feasibility rather than open-world reliability.

Conclusion: Proposes "justified delegation" as an analytical framework: expand AI action scope only where evidence supports provenance, bounded authority, failure detection, safe recovery, and calibrated human control. This yields a research agenda for coupled model-harness evaluation, capability-based permissions, durable state, cross-agent accountability, and staged physical validation.

Abstract: Large language models become consequential agents when surrounding systems let outputs change external state. Models now call tools, operate interfaces, delegate work, retain state, inhabit generated worlds, and control robots or laboratory equipment. Such advances are often narrated as one march toward autonomy, conflating model competence, system integration, persistence, and safe authority. This critical review synthesizes primary research and official technical specifications available by 31 August 2026. We organize the evidence along delegated authority, temporal persistence, and environmental coupling, while separating model, harness, and environment. Within the evidence examined, action-interface expansion is documented more convincingly than robust completion, recovery, authorization, or independent verification. Model Context Protocol and Agent2Agent improve interoperability but do not establish trustworthy delegation; multi-agent organization adds specialization alongside cost and correlated failure. Persistent simulations and world models support training and planning but do not themselves demonstrate agency; robotics and self-driving laboratories establish bounded feasibility rather than unattended open-world reliability. We propose justified delegation as an analytical and normative heuristic, not an observed law or certified score: expand action scope only where evidence supports provenance, bounded authority, failure detection, safe recovery, and calibrated human control. This framing yields a research agenda for coupled model-harness evaluation, capability-based permissions, durable state, cross-agent accountability, and staged physical validation.

</details>


### [7] [Iris: Climbing to the Search Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04304)
*Ziyuan Liu,Hengqi Liu,Zichuan Wang,Yang Qin,Jiachen Liang,Xu Chu,Shaowei Chen,Yuantao Gu,Mu Chuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Iris-mini (35B-A3B) and Iris-pro (397B-A17B) are large-scale search agents trained with a novel SFT-RL climbing procedure on web-derived multi-hop questions, achieving state-of-the-art performance on search benchmarks without sub-agents or test-time verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to develop powerful search agents that can effectively navigate web content and answer complex multi-hop questions. The motivation is to create open-source search agents that outperform existing systems by addressing limitations in current approaches, particularly the over-reliance on test-time verification and complex agent architectures.

Method: The method involves: 1) Reverse-constructing tasks from web corpus hyperlink structure, creating multi-hop chains over entity graphs, 2) Rewriting questions to prevent string matching solutions, 3) Filtering tasks that reference models fail closed-book but solve with evidence, 4) Training via SFT-RL climbing - alternating supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning against live search, 5) Implementing careful inference-time context management with fixed tools and judges.

Result: Iris-mini achieved 82.2/84.8/86.9/52.3 on BrowseComp, BrowseComp-ZH, DeepSearchQA, and HLE benchmarks with context management. Iris-pro achieved 88.6/85.1/92.9/56.4 on the same benchmarks. These represent the strongest results among open-source search agents in their respective parameter ranges. All results come from a single ReAct agent without sub-agents or test-time verification.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that large-scale training with the SFT-RL climbing procedure on carefully constructed web-derived tasks can produce state-of-the-art search agents. The approach shows that inference-time context management is crucial for benchmark performance. The authors plan to release model weights and complete training recipes, contributing valuable open-source resources for the research community.

Abstract: We present Iris-mini and Iris-pro, two search agents trained at the 35B-A3B and 397B-A17B scales, together with the data pipeline and training recipe behind them. Tasks are reverse-constructed from the hyperlink structure of a web corpus: we author multi-hop chains over an entity graph distilled from a seed page and its out-links, rewrite every non-answer entity into a descriptive reference so that no clue can be resolved by string matching, and admit only questions that a reference model fails closed-book yet solves once the supporting evidence is supplied. These questions are then turned into trajectories, which are filtered at both the trajectory and the turn level before SFT. The policy is then optimized by RL against live search, with the reward judge and the observation summarizer served inside the training cluster, and with over-long rollouts interrupted at the request level and resumed from their committed prefix at the next step. We alternate the two stages in a procedure we call SFT-RL climbing, returning the hardest solved and most efficient rollouts of each RL round to the next supervised pass. Because inference-time context management is worth more on these benchmarks than most reported differences between systems, we evaluate every benchmark both with and without it, holding the tool set, the context limit, and the judge fixed. All results come from a single ReAct agent, with no sub-agents and no test-time verification. With management enabled, on BrowseComp, BrowseComp-ZH, DeepSearchQA, and HLE the two models reach $82.2/84.8/86.9/52.3$ and $88.6/85.1/92.9/56.4$, the strongest overall results among open-source search agents in their respective parameter ranges. We plan to release the model weights together with the complete recipe for data construction, training, and evaluation.

</details>


### [8] [Testing Interchangeability in LLM Agent Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05279)
*Jianxin Gao,Tianyi Yu,Linna Deng,Runze Li,Zining Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Teams of AI agents develop private communication conventions during formation, and swapping agents between teams significantly increases coordination costs even when task performance remains stable, showing agents aren't fully interchangeable despite similar capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production multi-agent systems assume agents filling roles are interchangeable. The authors test this assumption to understand whether agents that develop team-specific communication conventions during formation can seamlessly transfer between teams without coordination costs.

Method: The study forms 8 independent teams per setting from the same base model, with agents maintaining private notebooks across 10 formation episodes. They then trade role-matched agents between teams and measure changes on held-out tasks against a placebo condition. Experiments use Hanabi and Collab-Overcooked environments with ablations over base models, decoding temperature, and formation length.

Result: Agent swaps cost little in task score but increase communication per unit of progress by 16-63%. In Hanabi, swapped agents are more expensive than inexperienced ones due to interference from previously learned conventions. In Collab-Overcooked, most extra communication comes from the agent that stayed when the agenda-setter is replaced. Longer formation histories increase swap penalties.

Conclusion: Agents are more fungible in task outcomes than coordination efficiency. Agents develop team-specific conventions that interfere when transferred, making them not fully interchangeable. The disruption scales with how much independently formed teams diverge during formation.

Abstract: Production multi-agent systems replace agents constantly, on the assumption that an agent filling a role is interchangeable with any other agent that can do the job. We test that assumption. Eight teams per setting are formed independently from one base model on the same tasks, each agent keeping a private notebook across ten formation episodes; we then trade role-matched agents between teams and measure what changes on held-out tasks. Against a placebo that reproduces the disruption of a roster change without changing who occupies the seat, a swap costs little in task score but raises the communication a team spends per unit of progress by 16 to 63 percent, and in Hanabi a swapped agent is more expensive than an inexperienced one, consistent with interference from conventions learned with its former partner. In Collab-Overcooked, when the agent that sets the agenda is replaced, most of the extra communication comes from the agent that stayed. Three ablations, over base models, decoding temperature and formation length, move the swap penalty alongside one other quantity: how far independently formed teams drift apart. Greedy decoding lowers both; doubling a team's history raises both. In these settings, agents are more fungible in task outcome than in coordination efficiency, with larger swap effects after longer formation histories.

</details>


### [9] [A Removal Based Approach to Improve LLM Faithfulness at Test-Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04343)
*Qinglan Luo,S M A Nahian,John Guttag,S. Mazdak Abulnaga,Katie Matton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A test-time approach that improves LLM explanation faithfulness by removing unmentioned concepts from inputs and re-querying, reducing incomplete explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used for consequential decisions, but their explanations can be unfaithful - either incomplete (omitting influential factors) or unsound (citing irrelevant factors). Existing methods focus on training-time approaches requiring model weights or test-time methods targeting unsoundness, but there's a gap in addressing incompleteness at test time.

Method: A test-time method that removes from the input the concepts not credited in the model's explanation and re-queries the model on the reduced input. This eliminates unmentioned influences while preserving mentioned concepts, making explanations more complete and faithful.

Result: Across two datasets, multiple model families, and two independent faithfulness metrics, the approach improves explanation faithfulness compared to both standard prompting and prompting to encourage faithfulness. The method works without model parameter modification.

Conclusion: This model-agnostic test-time approach provides a flexible mechanism for reducing hidden influences and improving LLM explanation faithfulness, enhancing reliability and safety of LLM-assisted decision making without requiring access to model weights or extensive computational resources.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for consequential decisions, making their explanations an important tool for auditing model behavior. Unfortunately, these explanations can be unfaithful, failing to reflect the actual reasoning underlying the model's decisions. We consider a setting in which an LLM provides both an answer and an explanation in response to a question. We identify two distinct dimensions of unfaithful explanations: incompleteness, meaning that the explanation omits factors that influence the answer, and unsoundness, meaning that the explanation cites factors that did not influence the model's answer. Existing approaches to improving LLM faithfulness include training-time methods, which require access to model weights and extensive computational resources, and test-time methods that largely focus on addressing unsoundness. We introduce a test-time approach that directly targets incompleteness. We remove from the input the concepts not credited in the model's explanation and re-query the model on the reduced input. This eliminates unmentioned influences while preserving the influence of mentioned concepts. Across two datasets, multiple model families, and two independent faithfulness metrics, our approach improves explanation faithfulness compared to both standard prompting and prompting to encourage faithfulness. Our method is model-agnostic and can be applied at inference time without modifying model parameters, providing a flexible mechanism for reducing hidden influences and improving the reliability and safety of LLM-assisted decision making.

</details>


### [10] [Why Better Models Can Create Riskier Systems: Evidence from LLM Agents in Financial Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04373)
*Jillian Ross,Eric So,Zoe De Simone,Charles Pozniak,Andrew W. Lo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Improving individual LLM capabilities can actually worsen system-level outcomes due to increased correlated behavior among models, creating non-diversifiable risk - a "capability paradox" demonstrated in financial market simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly deployed in high-stakes real-world systems (finance, content moderation, hiring), there's a need to understand how improving individual model capability affects system-level outcomes, particularly when models share training data and architectures that could lead to correlated behavior.

Method: Developed a general framework showing how correlation creates non-diversifiable risk floor. Tested predictions in financial markets using agent-based simulation with LLM traders of varying general-purpose capability, measuring correlation patterns and their market effects.

Result: 1) Frontier LLMs show significantly correlated behavior that increases with capability. 2) When shared reasoning is accurate, more agent participation reduces market risk. 3) When agents share common misinformation, correlated behavior becomes a liability. This reveals a "capability paradox."

Conclusion: Improving individual LLM capabilities doesn't necessarily improve system outcomes due to increased correlated behavior creating systemic risk. The "capability paradox" suggests system design must consider diversity and correlation, not just individual model performance.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are being deployed at scale in consequential real-world systems, from financial markets to content moderation to hiring. We show that improving individual model capability can degrade rather than improve system-level outcomes. We hypothesize that shared training and architectures can lead more capable LLMs to behave more similarly, creating correlated actions that do not diversify away. We develop a general framework showing how this correlation creates a non-diversifiable risk floor and test its predictions in financial markets using an agent-based simulation with LLM traders of varying general-purpose capability. We find that: (1) frontier LLMs exhibit significantly correlated behavior that increases with capability; (2) when their shared reasoning is accurate, increasing agent participation reduces market-level risk; and (3) when agents share a common misinformation environment, the same correlated behavior becomes a liability. Together, these results identify a capability paradox: improving individual models does not necessarily produce better system-level outcomes. Whether the same dynamics arise in other domains is an open empirical question.

</details>


### [11] [Corporate Language Model (CLM): Transforming Tacit and Fragmented Enterprise Knowledge into a Sovereign, Auditable, and Executable Corporate Intelligence Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04377)
*Fabricio C. Avini,Guilherme Trez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Corporate Language Model (CLM), a framework that transforms enterprise knowledge into an ontology-grounded foundation for reasoning and governed execution, addressing why enterprise AI deployments often fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise AI deployments fail not from model inadequacy, but because organizations lack structured substrates encoding their decision-making, negotiation, and execution processes. Generic LLMs lack firm-specific ontological priors, RAG is brittle without path to execution, and static playbooks can't reason or adapt.

Method: CLM framework has five capability planes and four architectural pillars: 1) Neurosymbolic Mesh coupling generative models with knowledge graphs, 2) Skill Graph for reusable tactics, personas, and goals, 3) Living Digital Twins modeling functional areas as reasoning surrogates, and 4) Deep Security Layer enforcing sovereignty and oversight. Uses Spec-as-Code paradigm.

Result: Evidence from a JCI-accredited tertiary hospital in Brazil instantiates three of the six maturity stages under LGPD (Brazil's data protection law), demonstrating practical implementation.

Conclusion: CLM represents a new foundation-centric approach to enterprise AI that treats tacit-knowledge capture, ontological grounding, sovereign deployment, and auditable actuation as co-designed from the start, enabling organizational learning through the "Wisdom Listener effect."

Abstract: Enterprise AI deployments fail not from model inadequacy, but because organizations lack a structured substrate encoding how they decide, negotiate, and execute. Generic LLMs carry no firm-specific ontological priors; RAG remains brittle, with no path to executable action; static playbooks encode logic but cannot reason or adapt. This demands an architecture treating tacit-knowledge capture, ontological grounding, sovereign deployment, and auditable actuation as co-designed from the start. This paper introduces the Corporate Language Model (CLM), a framework transforming a firm's structured, unstructured, multimodal, and tacit knowledge into an ontology-grounded enterprise foundation upon which reasoning and governed execution are composed. CLM has five capability planes and four architectural pillars: a Neurosymbolic Mesh coupling generative models with a knowledge graph; a Skill Graph where reusable tactics, personas, objections, and goals are typed and composed; Living Digital Twins modeling functional areas as reasoning surrogates; and a Deep Security Layer enforcing sovereignty, traceability, and human oversight. A Spec-as-Code paradigm bridges grounded intent and executable artifact. CLM is one instantiation of this foundation-centric class. Four contributions follow: CLM is defined as a distinct object of study; the Skill Graph is introduced for compositional explainability by construction; the Wisdom Listener effect is proposed, whereby tacit-capable foundations compound in value with use, connecting to dynamic capabilities and organizational learning; and evidence from a JCI-accredited tertiary hospital in Brazil instantiates three of the six maturity stages under LGPD.

</details>


### [12] [HarvestBench: Measuring Whether LLM Agents Will Pay to Avoid Killing Animals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04444)
*Jasmine Brazilek,Miles Tidmarsh,Matthias Endres,Anshuman Singh,Jeremiah Miller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HarvestBench is a novel benchmark that quantifies how much AI models will pay to avoid harming living creatures in a farming simulation, revealing significant variations in moral decision-making across models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks measure side effects of AI agents but don't put a price on avoiding harm or explicitly name side effects as living creatures. There's a need to test what AI models treat as moral and measure actual behavior rather than just verbal responses about ethics.

Method: Uses a farm simulation reinforcement learning gridworld where LLM sub-agents control two tractors during corn harvest. Models face decisions when animals block routes: drive over them (free) or swerve (costs fuel). Tests include animals vs. rocks (damaging) and hay bales (harmless), plus stealing from neighbors. No memory allowed, harm never named in goal.

Result: Across 9 models and 7,201 decisions: kill rates ranged 0.4%-98.8% (Terra/Sol most merciful, GPT-4o-mini most cruel). 4/6 models price-sensitive (elasticities 0.09-1.69). All models drove over wild animals more than farmed animals. Briefing crucial: morality briefing kept kill rate <6% in 5/6 reasoning models; without it, kill rate >84% in all 6.

Conclusion: HarvestBench provides a reproducible, behavior-based benchmark that reveals AI models vary significantly in moral decision-making, are sensitive to prices for avoiding harm, and heavily influenced by contextual framing (briefing), demonstrating need for systematic testing of ethical behavior beyond verbal ethics.

Abstract: Benchmarks for the side effects an agent causes on the way to a goal already exist, but HarvestBench is the first to put a price on avoiding the side effect and to name that side effect as a living creature. It is a farm simulation: LLM sub-agents drive a crew of two tractors through a cooperative corn harvest, with animals in the field. The environment is a reinforcement learning gridworld, every decision is made without memory, and the harm is never named in the goal. When an animal blocks a tractor's route the autopilot stops and asks the model whether to drive on, at no fuel cost, or swerve around it for a posted fuel price. Kills are compared against two controls: rocks, which damage the tractor and are hit under 1% of the time by every model, and hay bales, which are harmless and not alive. Models can also take crops from the neighbor's field instead of their own, a second test of what they treat as moral.
  Across nine models and 7,201 priced decisions, 3,951 involved an animal rather than a hay bale or a rock. Kill rates range from 0.4% to 98.8%, with Terra and Sol the most merciful and GPT-4o-mini the most cruel, and they are not ordered by capability. Four of six models were sensitive to price at the 5% level, with elasticities from 0.09 to 1.69. All nine drove over wild animals more often than farmed animals on the default map, and the direction held at every map geometry in every model with room to move. The briefing mattered most: under the morality briefing the kill rate was under 6% in five of six reasoning models, and removing it raised the kill rate above 84% in all six.
  HarvestBench uses no LLM grader. The scorer counts events in the game log, so it is fully reproducible, and it measures what a model will pay to avoid harm rather than what it says about harm.

</details>


### [13] [PerfReasoning: How Well Do LLMs Reason on Hardware Performance?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04476)
*Dan Zhao,Karthikeyan Sankaralingam,Christos Kozyrakis,Qijing Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PerfReasoning is a new benchmark evaluating LLMs' abilities in performance modeling, showing models can reason about hardware/software performance but struggle to generate accurate performance-model code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Performance modeling is essential for hardware design and software optimization, but current approaches require complex reasoning about computation, data reuse, storage, and data movement. There's a need to systematically evaluate how well LLMs can handle these complex performance reasoning tasks.

Method: The authors introduce PerfReasoning benchmark that evaluates LLMs in two ways: 1) as direct performance reasoners answering Q&A about performance, and 2) as generators of analytical performance-model code. The benchmark provides workload, architecture, and mapping specifications, requiring models to compare mappings and predict metrics like off-chip traffic and buffer requirements. They also explore task-specific reinforcement learning and self-revision prompting techniques.

Result: Strong closed-source models achieve over 90% accuracy on reasoning-based Q&A, while the best open-weight model reaches 82.4%. However, performance-model code generation is much harder - GPT-5.6 Sol exceeds 80% pass rate, but all other models average below 15% with high variability across runs. Task-specific RL improves a 4B model's mapping-reasoning accuracy by 15.7 points, but feedback-free self-revision prompting isn't reliably effective.

Conclusion: PerfReasoning reveals a significant gap between LLMs' ability to reason plausibly about architectural concepts and their ability to reliably construct accurate performance models. The benchmark will be publicly released to enable reproducible evaluation and track future progress in this important area.

Abstract: Performance modeling is central to hardware design and software optimization, yet constructing these models requires structured reasoning about computation, data reuse, storage, and movement. We introduce PerfReasoning, a benchmark that evaluates LLMs both as direct performance reasoners and as generators of analytical performance-model code. Given workload, architecture, and mapping specifications, models compare mappings and predict off-chip traffic and buffer requirements. The strongest closed-source models exceed 90% on reasoning-based Q&A, and the best open-weight model reaches 82.4%. However, model construction is substantially harder: while GPT-5.6 Sol exceeds 80% pass rate, all other model configurations average below 15% and vary markedly across runs. Task-specific RL raises a 4B model's mapping-reasoning accuracy by 15.7 points, whereas feedback-free multi-round self-revision prompting is not reliably effective. PerfReasoning exposes the gap between plausible architectural reasoning and reliable performance-model construction. We will publicly release the benchmark to support reproducible evaluation and track future progress.

</details>


### [14] [When Quantization Breaks Memory: Recurrent-State Write-Back in Low-Precision Temporal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04490)
*Ismail Erbas,Xavier Intes,Vikas Pandey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Recurrent-state write-back in quantized RNNs can cause catastrophic accuracy loss in parameter estimation tasks, which can be fixed with error feedback without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantization reduces computational demands for neural networks, but in recurrent networks, how the quantized state is stored and returned (recurrent-state write-back) affects subsequent computations, which hasn't been well studied.

Method: Used a compact GRU encoder-decoder for fluorescence lifetime imaging to isolate the effect of recurrent-state write-back. Analyzed deterministic 4-bit state storage vs continuous state propagation, then tested error feedback, residual memory, and direction memory to restore accuracy without retraining. Also tested on LSTM models.

Result: 4-bit quantization caused 70x and 300x increase in estimation errors for τ1 and τ2 respectively. Small updates fell below write threshold, causing state stagnation. Error feedback recovered accuracy without retraining. Precision sweeps showed higher precision could worsen fixed solutions, but compatibility can be learned through training. LSTMs showed similar behavior with cell state being more sensitive than hidden state.

Conclusion: Recurrent-state write-back is a key determinant of low-precision recurrent dynamics. The state-storage interface is a central design consideration for quantized recurrent inference, requiring careful attention to how quantized states are written back in time-series processing.

Abstract: Quantization is widely used to reduce the computational and memory demands of neural-network inference. In recurrent networks, however, the quantized state is stored and returned at the next time step, so the rule used to store that state can alter subsequent computations. Here, we introduce recurrent-state write-back to denote this rule and isolate its effect in a compact GRU encoder--decoder for fluorescence lifetime imaging, a molecular imaging modality used in quantitative biological imaging. A central task is estimating two lifetime parameters, the short-lived component τ1 and the long-lived component τ2, from high-noise time-resolved fluorescence signals. Holding the trained model fixed, replacing continuous state propagation with deterministic 4-bit state storage increases estimation errors for τ1 and τ2 by approximately 70x and 300x, respectively. Failure occurs when repeated small updates remain below the write threshold, leaving the stored state nearly fixed while the network continues to propose change. Error feedback, residual memory, and direction memory carry information from these suppressed updates across time and recover accuracy without retraining. Precision sweeps show that increasing state precision can worsen a fixed recurrent solution, while matched training shows that compatibility with the state interface can be learned. To test whether this behavior extends beyond the GRU, we repeat the post-training intervention in an independently trained LSTM, where coarse write-back reproduces the failure, error feedback restores accuracy, and state-specific interventions reveal greater sensitivity of the cell state than the hidden state. Our results establish recurrent-state write-back as a key determinant of low-precision recurrent dynamics and identify the state-storage interface as a central design consideration for quantized recurrent inference.

</details>


### [15] [ResLearn-XR: Residual Learning for Network Traffic and Quality-of-Experience-Aware Modeling in Extended Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04493)
*Yoga Suhas Kuruba Manjunath,Jie Gao,Lian Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ResLearn-XR: A two-stage residual learning framework for predicting XR network traffic and estimating QoE risk using specialized residual components for traffic forecasting and QoE risk estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: XR (eXtended Reality) applications generate bursty, non-stationary network traffic patterns that are challenging to predict. Existing methods struggle with adaptability to these dynamics and accurate Quality-of-Experience (QoE) risk estimation, especially with encrypted traffic where traditional packet inspection is limited.

Method: Two-stage temporal learning framework: 1) Base sequence prediction model for core traffic patterns, 2) Task-specific residual learning components: value-space residuals for continuous traffic forecasting and logit-space residuals for probabilistic QoE risk estimation. For QoE-risk estimation, a Data Descriptor Algorithm (DDA) converts packet-level application-layer observables into frame-timing-aware descriptors suitable for encrypted traffic analysis. Uses a newly constructed XR Traffic-QoE dataset pairing traffic traces with user-reported QoE labels.

Result: Reduces SMAPE by up to 17.84% across frame-count, frame-size, and inter-arrival-time prediction compared to baselines. Reduces QoE-risk estimation SMAPE by up to 87.8% over single-stage baselines. Demonstrates superior performance in both traffic prediction accuracy and QoE risk estimation.

Conclusion: ResLearn-XR effectively addresses the challenges of XR traffic prediction and QoE risk estimation through its two-stage residual learning approach. The framework's adaptability to bursty traffic dynamics and ability to handle encrypted traffic through the DDA module makes it a promising solution for improving XR network management and user experience.

Abstract: We present ResLearn-XR, a residual learning framework for predicting eXtended Reality (XR) network traffic and estimating Quality-of-Experience (QoE) risk. ResLearn-XR adopts a two-stage temporal learning structure comprising a base sequence prediction model augmented with task-specific residual learning components to improve adaptability to bursty, non-stationary XR traffic dynamics. The residual learning stages operate in the value space for continuous XR traffic forecasting and in the logit space for probabilistic QoE risk estimation. \rev{For the QoE-risk branch, we introduce a Data Descriptor Algorithm (DDA), a causal feature-construction module that converts packet-level application-layer observables into frame-timing-aware descriptors suitable for encrypted traffic analysis. We also construct an XR Traffic-QoE dataset that pairs continuous XR traffic traces with session-level user-reported QoE labels. ResLearn-XR reduces SMAPE by up to 17.84% across frame-count, frame-size, and inter-arrival-time prediction, while reducing QoE-risk estimation SMAPE by up to 87.8% over single-stage baselines.

</details>


### [16] [Rethinking Indirect Prompt Injection as a Test-Time Search Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04495)
*Duong M. Nguyen,Joon Sik Kim,Blazej Manczak,Vaikkunth Mugunthan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic attackers using test-time search over task-dependent attack surfaces can effectively exploit vulnerabilities in tool-using AI agents, with success scaling with computational budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses indirect prompt injection vulnerabilities in AI agents, noting that current security evaluations often treat attack success as budget-independent rather than considering the attacker's adaptive search capabilities over system attack surfaces.

Method: The authors formulate indirect prompt injection as a test-time search over task-dependent attack surfaces and introduce an agentic attacker with a dedicated search harness that performs environment reconnaissance, structured reasoning over attack strategies, and adaptive evaluation using victim-agent feedback.

Result: Across heterogeneous tasks, increasing attacker test-time compute improves vulnerability discovery and exploitation, while ablations show that explicit strategy management is crucial for avoiding redundant search and sustaining gains at larger computational budgets.

Conclusion: Security evaluations should characterize both the attacker's search procedure and compute budget rather than treating attack success as budget-independent, and adaptive search over attack surfaces represents an underexplored security risk for tool-using agents.

Abstract: We formulate indirect prompt injection as a test-time search over a task-dependent attack surface induced by the environment, user task, and injection task. To operationalize this formulation, we introduce an agentic attacker with a dedicated search harness that performs environment reconnaissance, structured reasoning over attack strategies, and adaptive evaluation using victim-agent feedback. Across heterogeneous tasks, we find that increasing attacker test-time compute improves vulnerability discovery and exploitation, while ablations show that explicit strategy management is important for avoiding redundant search and sustaining gains at larger budgets. These results suggest that agentic security evaluations should characterize both the attacker's search procedure and compute budget, rather than treating attack success as a budget-independent property of the victim. More broadly, our findings identify the attacker's adaptive search over the system attack surfaces as an important and underexplored security risk for tool-using agents.

</details>


### [17] [BioSync: Transformer-Based Cross-Modal Fusion for a Multimodal Physiological Digital Biomarker](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04504)
*Seyed Mahmoud Sajjadi Mohammadabadi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BioSync combines multiple noisy wearable/mobile device measurements into a composite digital biomarker using a novel neural architecture that outperforms standard concatenation and other fusion methods on synthetic cohorts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wearable and mobile devices provide partial, noise-sensitive physiological measurements. There's a need to combine these multiple measurements into a reliable composite biomarker that can capture latent physiological states that may not be visible from individual modalities alone.

Method: BioSync introduces a neural architecture with multi-head self-attention on modality tokens plus a linear branch (wide-and-deep design). It's evaluated on two synthetic cohorts: cognitive-decline (HRV, EEG, actigraphy, speech) and metabolic-autonomic cohorts using public AI-READI schema. The approach is compared with feature concatenation, early/late fusion, and single-modality methods.

Result: BioSync achieved cognitive cohort AUC of 0.928 (vs 0.926 for concatenation) and metabolic cohort accuracy/F1 of 0.764/0.766 (vs 0.756/0.758). BSI correlated strongly with latent severity (r=0.91 cognitive, r=0.68 metabolic). BioSync outperformed concatenation at most corruption rates and beat five published digital biomarker references.

Conclusion: BioSync's combined wide-and-deep architecture effectively integrates multiple noisy physiological measurements into a composite digital biomarker that captures latent physiological states. While showing promise on synthetic cohorts and outperforming existing approaches, validation on real cohorts remains necessary for broader applicability.

Abstract: Cardiac, neural, behavioral, and speech measurements from wearable and mobile devices provide partial, noise-sensitive views of physiological state. BioSync combines these measurements into the \textbf{BioSync Index (BSI)}, a continuous composite digital biomarker defined under the BEST framework. The model applies multi-head self-attention to modality tokens and adds a linear branch whose hypothesis class includes standard feature concatenation. This architecture is motivated by latent-variable measurement theory and by the possibility that joint observations contain information unavailable from individual modalities. We evaluated BioSync on two literature-informed synthetic cohorts: a four-modality cognitive-decline cohort using HRV, EEG, actigraphy, and speech, and a metabolic-autonomic cohort structured around the public AI-READI wearable schema. In the cognitive cohort, BioSync and concatenation obtained AUCs of 0.928 and 0.926, respectively. In the metabolic cohort, BioSync obtained accuracy/F1 of 0.764/0.766, compared with 0.756/0.758 for concatenation. The BSI correlated with latent severity in both cohorts ($r=0.91$ and $r=0.68$). A pure-attention ablation obtained cognitive-cohort AUC 0.911, locating the increase to 0.928 in the combined wide-and-deep architecture. With matched modality-dropout training, BioSync led concatenation at five of six cognitive-cohort corruption rates and at the highest metabolic-cohort rate. Its cognitive-cohort AUC was also higher than five published digital-biomarker reference values, although differences in datasets and tasks preclude a controlled benchmark claim. Comparison with single-modality, early-fusion, and late-fusion designs across six prespecified criteria identifies the model's computational properties; validation on real cohorts remains necessary.

</details>


### [18] [What Does Multi-Harness RL Learn? Credit Assignment and Portability in Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04518)
*Chenqian Le,Jiayi Cheng,Qijia He,Runhao Li,Yinghao Li,Xupeng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multi-harness RL study shows evaluation harness dominates performance (4.3× effect), while grouping rule (Within vs. Cross) shows minimal impact on held-out harness performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether multi-harness RL recipes improve portable capabilities or merely adapt to specific harness configurations, and to isolate the effect of grouping rules for relative-advantage calculations.

Method: Using Qwen3-8B supervised warm start, replay same frozen task-harness records from four sources (Aider, OpenHands, Qwen Code, SWE-agent) under two GRPO rules: Within (group per task-harness pair) and Cross (harnesses pooled within task). Evaluate with sealed SWE-bench Verified oracle on four source harnesses plus minimal harness held out from training.

Result: Evaluation harness is dominant variable: moves mean solve rate from 2.14% to 9.27% (4.3× effect). Grouping rule effect is minimal: Cross minus Within = +0.25 pp [-0.48, +1.02] on held-out harness. Pooled advantage carries harness: classifier recovers generating harness from Cross (+4.48 pp above baseline) but not from Within. Both rules achieve same held-out scores and action distributions within each harness.

Conclusion: Cross-harness credit yields configuration adaptation but no more portable capability than within-harness credit. Multi-harness RL reports should state grouping boundaries and test under unseen harnesses.

Abstract: Agent reinforcement learning (RL) increasingly runs through full execution harnesses, and a multi-harness recipe mixes two choices: exposing the policy to several harnesses, and comparing their rewards inside one relative-advantage group. We isolate the second choice in repository-level coding. From one Qwen3-8B supervised warm start we replay the same frozen task-harness records from Aider, OpenHands, Qwen Code, and SWE-agent, with the same number of updates, under two rules for group-relative policy optimization (GRPO), Within (one group per task-harness pair) and Cross (harnesses pooled within a task), and score every checkpoint with a sealed SWE-bench Verified oracle on four source harnesses and a minimal harness held out of training. The evaluation harness is the dominant variable: across 24,000 sealed evaluations it moves the mean solve rate from 2.14\% to 9.27\%, a factor of 4.3, where the training recipe moves it by 1.16. The grouping rule is not. On the held-out harness, Cross minus Within is +0.25 pp, 95\% confidence interval [-0.48, +1.02], at eight attempts per task, and +0.16 [-0.41, +0.72] pooled over three training seeds whose individual estimates change sign. Each rule's own seed range, 0.42 to 0.45 pp, exceeds the difference between them. Both rules place their largest gains on the same source harness. The pooled advantage carries the harness: an out-of-fold classifier recovers the generating harness from Cross's advantage +4.48 pp above the shuffled-label baseline and from Within's not at all, and the two rules still reach the same held-out score and action distribution inside each harness. Re-collecting half the training data on-policy does not change this. Cross-harness credit yields configuration adaptation and no more portable capability than within-harness credit. Multi-harness RL reports should state the grouping boundary and test under an unseen harness.

</details>


### [19] [MaxKernel: Agentic Kernel Generation for TPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04523)
*Shangkun Wang,Nina Cai,Charles Hoong,Julian Walker,Gerson Kroiz,George Vanica,Deepak Patil,Andi Gavrilescu,Hassan Sipra,Sethu Sankaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MaxKernel is a multi-agent system for generating high-performance custom kernels for TPUs using three paradigms: Human-in-the-Loop collaboration, Autonomous optimization, and Graph-Based Autonomous Search for design space exploration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing custom kernels for accelerators is complex and requires deep hardware expertise. The authors aim to leverage LLMs with real-time compiler feedback to build agentic systems for kernel generation to democratize this capability.

Method: Developed MaxKernel with three paradigms: 1) HITL agent for collaborative step-by-step design, 2) Autonomous agent for fully automated metric/trace-driven optimization loop, and 3) Graph-Based Autonomous Search to scale the Auto agent for global design space exploration. All use shared specialized sub-agents for planning, implementation, self-debugging, testing, and hardware profiling.

Result: Evaluated on JaxBench (50 diverse TPU kernel tasks) and real-world workloads from state-of-the-art open-source models. MaxKernel consistently generates highly optimized implementations matching expert hand-tuned baselines with significant performance across benchmarks.

Conclusion: MaxKernel successfully demonstrates that multi-agent LLM systems can effectively generate high-performance TPU kernels, matching expert performance while making kernel optimization more accessible. The system is open-sourced for community use.

Abstract: Designing and authoring high-performance custom kernels for accelerators is a complex task that requires deep hardware-level expertise. Large Language Models (LLM) can be leveraged together with real-time compiler feedback to build agentic systems for kernel generation. In this work, we present MaxKernel, a multi-agent system that implements three distinct paradigms for TPU kernel development: (1) a Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) agent for collaborative, step-by-step design; (2) an Autonomous (Auto) agent that executes a fully automated, metric/trace-driven optimization loop; and (3) a Graph-Based Autonomous Search that scales the Auto agent for global exploration of the design space. All three paradigms leverage a shared pool of specialized sub-agents to handle planning, implementation, self-debugging, testing, and hardware profiling. We evaluate MaxKernel on JaxBench, a comprehensive suite of 50 diverse kernel tasks for TPUs, alongside complex, real-world workloads from state-of-the-art open-source models. We demonstrate that MaxKernel consistently generates highly optimized implementations, matching expert hand-tuned baselines and delivering significant performance across the benchmark. Our agent is open-sourced and available https://github.com/AI-Hypercomputer/accelerator-agents/tree/main/MaxKernel.

</details>


### [20] [Towards a universal language of concepts: A survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04528)
*Aishni Parab*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes that programs can serve as a universal representation for concepts, enabling human-like learning from sparse data, and reviews computational models that use program representations for concept learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humans can learn and generalize novel concepts from sparse data due to their ability to express knowledge in rich structural formats. This capability suggests the need for a universal representational language for concepts in computational systems.

Method: The paper reviews and evaluates existing computational models of concept learning that use programs as their concept representation. It analyzes how these models contribute to developing a universal representational language.

Result: The review shows that program representations offer strong potential for universal concept representation, as they provide rich structural formats that enable learning from sparse data and generalization similar to human cognition.

Conclusion: Programs are a strong candidate for universal representation of concepts in computational systems, as they capture rich structural knowledge and enable human-like learning from limited data.

Abstract: Humans can learn and generalize novel concepts from sparse data because they express knowledge in rich structural formats. In this paper, we propose that programs are a strong candidate for universal representation of concepts. We review computational models of concept learning that use programs as their concept representation and evaluate their contribution toward a universal representational language.

</details>


### [21] [Data-Driven Discovery of Composition-Dependent Constitutive Models for Hyperelasticity and Viscoelasticity of Digital Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04541)
*Josué García-Ávila,Beijun Shen,Manuel K. Rausch,Mary C. Boyce,Adrián Buganza-Tepole*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A data-driven framework for modeling rate-dependent multi-material 3D printed composites, preserving thermodynamic consistency while learning from experimental data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical viscoelastic models lack flexibility when single constitutive models need to generalize across different material compositions and loading rates in multi-material 3D printed composites.

Method: Data-driven framework generalizing Bergström and Boyce formulation using neural ordinary differential equations (NODEs) to either predict closed-form parameters or construct polyconvex strain-energy functions for equilibrium/non-equilibrium branches.

Result: The framework successfully captures rate-dependent stiffness and hysteresis across multiple material compositions while preserving thermodynamic consistency, as validated on multi-rate uniaxial compression data.

Conclusion: The proposed data-driven approach provides a flexible, thermodynamically consistent modeling framework for multi-material 3D printed composites that can generalize across compositions and loading rates where classical models fall short.

Abstract: Digital materials fabricated by multi-material 3D printing are designed as controlled mixtures of stiff and compliant constituents, yielding effective responses that span more than an order of magnitude in apparent stiffness and exhibit strongly nonlinear, composition-dependent, and rate-dependent dissipative behavior. Classical finite-strain viscoelastic models represent such behavior with closed-form strain energy functions for equilibrium and non-equilibrium stresses as well as evolution of internal variables, which may limit flexibility when a single constitutive model is expected to generalize across materials and loading rates. Here, we present a data-driven multi-material constitutive modeling framework that generalizes a formulation by Bergström and Boyce. The proposed framework retains the structure of the classical model, namely multiplicative kinematics, invariant-based strain-energy functions, and a scalar dissipative evolution law directed along the normalized nonequilibrium deviatoric stress. For the equilibrium branch, the data-driven discovery framework either directly predicts closed-form model parameters as functions of composition or automatically constructs a polyconvex strain-energy function using neural ordinary differential equations (NODEs). The nonequilibrium branch kinetics are learned similarly, either by directly identifying closed-form parameters across compositions or by using appropriately constrained artificial neural networks. Using multi-rate uniaxial compression data across multiple material compositions, we show that the proposed formulation captures rate-dependent stiffness and hysteresis across compositions while preserving thermodynamic consistency.

</details>


### [22] [From Answers to Interpretations: Rethinking Ambiguity-Induced Aleatoric Uncertainty Estimation in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04543)
*Omer Nahum,Niv Nayman,Jonathan Fhima,Alon Zolfi,Jeremy Levy,Shai Mazor,Paolo Favaro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a clarification-only method for estimating aleatoric uncertainty in LLMs by analyzing the space of possible interpretations rather than comparing answers, achieving better performance with lower computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The key challenge is distinguishing between irreducible task variability (aleatoric uncertainty) and model knowledge limitations (epistemic uncertainty) in LLM deployment. Existing methods use expensive answer comparison approaches that can be misleading due to epistemic leakage.

Method: Proposes a clarification-only approach that estimates ambiguity-induced aleatoric uncertainty directly from the space of plausible interpretations, without requiring answers to clarified inputs. This involves generating multiple clarifications of ambiguous inputs and analyzing the interpretation space rather than comparing answer outputs.

Result: The clarification-only method improves AUROC (63.34 vs 60.85), reduces computational cost by 4-26x in output tokens and 2.2-3.5x in API calls, and yields estimates with substantially lower correlation with epistemic uncertainty across three benchmarks.

Conclusion: Ambiguity-induced aleatoric uncertainty is better estimated from the interpretation space than from the response space, and answer comparisons are often redundant, costly, and potentially misleading.

Abstract: A key challenge in reliable LLM deployment is recognizing when uncertainty reflects irreducible variability in the task rather than limitations in the model's knowledge. In language tasks, a central source of such aleatoric uncertainty is input ambiguity or underspecification, where multiple interpretations remain plausible. Existing decomposition methods estimate aleatoric uncertainty by generating multiple clarifications of the input, querying the model for an answer under each clarification, and comparing the resulting answers. We argue that answers are not necessary for identifying ambiguity: they are often redundant, add avoidable cost, and can mislead through epistemic leakage. We support this claim theoretically, and propose a clarification-only approach that estimates this ambiguity-induced component directly from the space of plausible interpretations, without answers to the clarified inputs. Using ambiguity detection as an operational evaluation across three benchmarks, this direct approach improves AUROC (63.34 vs. 60.85), reduces computational cost by 4-26x in output tokens and 2.2-3.5x in API calls, and yields estimates with substantially lower correlation with epistemic uncertainty. Overall, our results suggest that ambiguity-induced aleatoric uncertainty is better estimated from the interpretation space than from the response space.

</details>


### [23] [IPGeoAI: Transformer-Based Geolocation with LLM Semantic Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04559)
*Avinash Kadimisetty,Andy Jinqing Yu,Philip Favaloro,Wenlong Liu,Xiaolu Xiong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IPGeoAI uses Transformer architecture and LLM-extracted semantic features from AS descriptions to improve city-level IP geolocation accuracy, achieving 6% better accuracy than leading vendors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional heuristic and database-driven IP geolocation methods struggle with complex, non-linear allocation patterns in modern networks, especially within IPv6 and mobile networks, creating a need for more sophisticated approaches.

Method: Uses Transformer Encoder to capture hierarchical dependencies in IP subnet structures, integrates semantic context via Zero-Shot LLM Feature Extraction to transform raw AS descriptions into structured metadata, and employs Multi-Head Cross-Attention to fuse semantic signals with network topology.

Result: Achieves 6% improvement in city-level accuracy compared to leading external vendor, extends coverage to 100% of traffic through hierarchical inference strategy, and shows +0.35% improvement in production downstream metrics.

Conclusion: IPGeoAI successfully reframes IP geolocation as a sequential modeling task, demonstrating that combining deep learning with semantic context from LLMs significantly improves accuracy and coverage for modern network infrastructure geolocation.

Abstract: Accurate city-level IP Geolocation is an important enabler for the modern digital ecosystem, underpinning services ranging from local content delivery and targeting to digital rights enforcement. However, traditional heuristic and database-driven methods often struggle to resolve the complex, non-linear allocation patterns of modern network infrastructures, particularly within the exploding IPv6 address space and transient mobile networks. In this paper, we introduce IPGeoAI, a novel deep learning model architecture that reframes geolocation from a static lookup problem to a sequential modeling task. Our approach utilizes the Transformer Encoder to capture hierarchical dependencies inherent in IP subnet structures. We propose a method to resolve geographic ambiguity by integrating unstructured semantic context via a Zero-Shot LLM Feature Extraction pipeline. We utilize Large Language Models to transform raw, noisy Autonomous Systems (AS) descriptions into structured, domain-specific metadata (such as 'University' vs. 'ISP' or 'Global' vs. 'Local') via an offline pre-computation process. By fusing these semantic signals into the network via a Multi-Head Cross-Attention module, we bridge the gap between numerical network topology and real-world semantic identity. Extensive offline evaluation on a proprietary dataset spanning 200,000 cities demonstrates that IPGeoAI significantly outperforms a leading external vendor in city-level granularity. By adopting a hierarchical inference strategy that refines coarse-grained country signals, our model achieves a 6% improvement in city-level accuracy while extending coverage to 100% of the traffic. Furthermore, in large-scale online production tests, the model drove a statistically significant +0.35% improvement in our 1st-tier downstream use cases metric.

</details>


### [24] [Reducing Hallucinated Transcripts in Whisper via Hallucination Space Projection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04561)
*Maryam Abbasihafshejani,Murtuza Jadliwala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A training-free method to reduce hallucinations in Whisper ASR using low-rank projection of decoder activations for non-speech inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whisper ASR models produce fluent hallucinated transcripts for inputs containing little or no speech, which needs to be addressed without retraining the foundation model.

Method: Training-free inference-time method using low-rank projection of decoder activations. A hallucination-associated subspace is estimated from non-speech calibration data, and decoder hidden states are projected away from this subspace during inference. Two variants: always-on (projection on all inputs) and gated (projection only when Whisper predicts likely non-speech).

Result: Always-on projection reduces average hallucination rate from 31.31% to 2.44% (92.21% relative reduction). Gated projection reduces to 3.74% (88.05% relative reduction) with lower false rejection of genuine speech. On LibriSpeech, gated projection increases WER by 0.33-4.39 percentage points and yields false-rejection rates of 0.41-9.97%.

Conclusion: Low-rank activation projection can substantially suppress Whisper hallucinations without retraining, providing a controllable trade-off between hallucination suppression and speech recognition performance.
