Each study classified across five application families and three LLM method families, assessing evidence realism, deployment readiness, and responsibility boundaries between LLMs and physical HVAC decisions.

Result: 1) Research concentrated in building energy modeling (BEM - 32/66 papers), 2) Only 4 studies reach pilot-level evidence, none report sustained operational deployment, 3) No study ready-now for industry adoption (3 near-term, 63 research-only), 4) LLMs show promise for bounded, human-in-the-loop uses: point-name normalization, document-grounded operator support, BEM workflow assistance, advisory interfaces around physics-based controllers, 5) Conventional ML, MPC, RL, and ontology-based tools remain more adopted for high-frequency control and forecasting.

Conclusion: Current evidence supports LLMs primarily as semantic and workflow layers rather than autonomous HVAC controllers. Future work should prioritize field-validated benchmarks, orchestration evaluation under operational constraints, and LLM-MPC/RL architectures with bounded latency and verifiable safety properties.

Abstract: Building automation systems generate rich sensor data yet remain insight-poor because heterogeneous point naming, missing metadata, and fragmented documentation obstruct their operational use. This systematic review analyses and codes 66 peer-reviewed studies on large language models (LLMs) for HVAC operations published between 2023 and March 2026. Each study is classified across five application families and three LLM method families and assessed for evidence realism, deployment readiness, and the responsibility boundary between the LLM and physical HVAC decisions. The corpus is concentrated in building energy modelling (BEM, 32 of 66 papers), while load forecasting remains too sparse for subfield-level conclusions. Only four studies reach pilot-level evidence, and none reports sustained operational deployment. No study was classified as ready-now for industry adoption; three were near-term and 63 research-only. Nevertheless, several bounded, human-in-the-loop uses merit near-term trials, including point-name normalisation, document-grounded operator support, BEM workflow assistance, and advisory interfaces around physics-based controllers. Conventional machine learning (ML), model predictive control (MPC), reinforcement learning (RL) and ontology-based tools remain more adopted for high-frequency control, short-horizon numerical forecasting, and well-posed ontology mapping, while autonomous agentic operation and unvalidated occupant proxies remain research-stage. Current evidence therefore supports LLMs primarily as semantic and workflow layers rather than autonomous HVAC controllers. Future work should prioritise field-validated benchmarks, orchestration evaluation under operational constraints, and LLM-MPC/RL architectures with bounded latency and verifiable safety properties.

</details>


### [99] [LLM-Driven Algorithm Design for Quantum Circuit Synthesis based on Binary Decision Diagrams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05327)
*Yoonju Sim,Federico Berto,Chuanbo Hua,Jinkyoo Park,Changhyun Kwon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QuantumEvo uses evolutionary algorithms with LLMs to optimize BDD variable ordering for quantum circuit cost, outperforming traditional heuristics


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reversible circuit synthesis methods using BDDs rely on variable ordering heuristics that minimize BDD size, but BDD size is an imperfect proxy for actual quantum circuit cost (QCC), leading to suboptimal quantum circuit implementations

Method: Proposes QuantumEvo evolutionary framework that uses LLM as heuristic generator for QCC-aware BDD variable ordering; searches over ordering heuristics initialized from multiple families; candidate heuristics manipulate variable orderings using standard BDD operations, selected by downstream QCC; discovered heuristic HGA-QE modifies sifting step inside genetic algorithm to better align with QCC

Result: HGA-QE achieves 70.9% tie-or-win rate against per-function best baseline; strictly best on 13.5% of functions; shows clearer relative advantage in strict wins on benchmark suites from different sources than training data

Conclusion: Evolutionary search over BDD variable ordering heuristics with QCC-aware evaluation can discover better heuristics than traditional size-minimization approaches, demonstrating competitive performance and good generalization to unseen functions

Abstract: Quantum circuits are central to implementing quantum algorithms on quantum devices, where quantum gates must be reversible. Many quantum algorithms rely on Boolean functions, which must therefore be implemented reversibly within quantum circuits. Reversible circuit synthesis provides a way to translate such Boolean functions into reversible circuits. Binary decision diagrams (BDDs) offer a scalable approach to this task, but the resulting BDDs and circuits depend heavily on variable ordering. Existing ordering heuristics commonly minimize BDD size because it is closely tied to the circuit size. However, BDD size is an imperfect proxy for the quantum cost of the synthesized circuit (QCC). We propose \texttt{QuantumEvo}, an evolutionary framework that uses an LLM as a heuristic generator for QCC-aware BDD variable ordering. Instead of predicting orderings directly, \texttt{QuantumEvo} searches over ordering heuristics initialized from multiple heuristic families. Candidate heuristics directly manipulate variable orderings using standard BDD operations and are selected by downstream QCC. The discovered heuristic, HGA-QE, modifies the sifting step inside a genetic algorithm so that the procedure is better aligned with QCC. Across the benchmark set, HGA-QE achieves a 70.9\% tie-or-win rate against the per-function best baseline and is strictly best on 13.5\% of the functions. The results demonstrate broadly competitive QCC performance, with HGA-QE showing a clearer relative advantage in strict wins on the two benchmark suites drawn from sources different from the data used for heuristic discovery.

</details>


### [100] [Technical Manual for a Toolkit for Measuring Contextual Individuation in Transformer Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05333)
*José Luciano Verçosa Marques,Frederico Jorge Heitmann,Daniel Omar Perez,Marcelo Vinicius de Paula,Tárcio André dos Santos Barros*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Authors present a toolkit for measuring how transformer language models separate word meanings across different contexts using "bridge forms" - words with different meanings in different domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether transformer language models truly individuate word meanings by context in later layers, researchers need a controlled construct that holds word form constant while varying context and sense. Existing approaches have methodological flaws like sense contamination and multi-group biases.

Method: Created a toolkit using "bridge forms" - single written words that recur unchanged across different subject domains with different meanings. The pipeline includes: declarative specification of bridge forms, corpus acquisition from Wikipedia, occurrence localization, layer-wise representation extraction, domain-pairwise silhouette measurement of separation, and paired visualization protocols.

Result: This is a methodological paper presenting the toolkit design, not reporting empirical results. The toolkit addresses various methodological failure modes like sense contamination from broad labels, multi-group bias of silhouette coefficient, subword-tokenization misalignment, and visualization artifacts.

Conclusion: The authors present a validated toolkit for studying how transformer models separate word meanings by context. The toolkit is archived with persistent identifier and intended to be cited as an instrument by future studies that use it to produce empirical results about contextual word meaning separation in language models.

Abstract: A transformer language model assigns a single, context-independent vector to a word type at its embedding layer, yet is widely believed to individuate that word's occurrences by context in its later layers. Testing this belief cleanly requires a construct that holds the word form fixed while its context and intended sense vary in a controlled, labeled way. This manual documents an open toolkit built around such a construct, which we call a bridge form: a single written word that recurs, unchanged, across two or more subject domains with a different sense in each. We describe, and justify, every stage of the pipeline: the declarative specification of bridge forms and their source domains, corpus acquisition from Wikipedia, occurrence localization, layer-wise representation extraction, a domain-pairwise silhouette measurement of separation in the model's representation space, and a paired visualization protocol. Each design choice is presented together with the methodological failure mode it is meant to avoid (sense contamination from overly broad category labels, the multi-group bias of the silhouette coefficient, subword-tokenization misalignment, and axis-comparability artifacts in dimensionality-reduced plots, among others). This manuscript is a methodological and implementation reference: it does not report or interpret empirical outcomes of running the toolkit on any particular model or bridge-form set. The toolkit, its full source, and the corpora used to exercise it are archived separately (Section 9) under a persistent identifier, and are intended to be cited as an instrument by studies that use it to produce and interpret empirical results.

</details>


### [101] [Does Your Agent's Memory Survive a Model Upgrade? A Controlled Study of Memory Portability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05339)
*Ankit Goyal,Jaideep Ray*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Memory formats (raw text, RAG chunks, compressed notes, knowledge graphs) show different reliability when AI models are upgraded, with fixed-schema knowledge graphs being most stable and compressed notes suffering significant performance changes during model migration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI model upgrades are common, but memory migrations are problematic because new models may interpret old memories differently, mixed embedding versions can break retrieval, and repair may fail without original evidence. The paper aims to understand how different memory representation formats transfer when models change.

Method: Used 48 synthetic histories with randomized answer codes and exact scoring. Tested four memory formats: 1) verbatim long-context reading (LC-RAW), 2) chunked retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), 3) compressed natural-language notes (NOTES), 4) normalized fixed-schema knowledge graph (KG-fixed). Evaluated using two open-weight models with <10B parameters, measuring accuracy changes during model migration.

Result: Fixed-schema KG transferred most reliably with near-zero accuracy change (+0.0004 ± 0.0020). Compressed NOTES showed high model coupling with asymmetric accuracy shifts (+9.91 or -13.28 percentage points). RAG with mixed embeddings captured only 4.96-point improvement vs 11.90-point gain from full re-embedding. Diagnostic analysis showed 80% of NOTES deficit from information loss during construction, 81% of RAG deficit from retrieval failures. Store-only repair of NOTES failed in all cases, while keeping raw history enabled successful recovery in 34/48 cases.

Conclusion: Memory migration requires direction-specific testing, strict embedding space isolation, and retention of source histories for effective repair. Fixed-schema knowledge graphs offer reliable model-agnostic memory, while compressed formats risk significant performance degradation during model upgrades.

Abstract: Model upgrades are routine; memory migrations are not. An agent can keep the same memory store and still forget: a new model may interpret old notes differently, mixed embedding versions may break retrieval, and repair may fail without the original evidence. We compare memory as the same history is preserved verbatim for long-context reading (LC-RAW), divided into chunks for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), compressed by a model into natural-language notes (NOTES), or normalized into a fixed-schema knowledge graph (KG-fixed). The study uses 48 synthetic histories with randomized answer codes, exact scoring, and two open-weight models with sub 10 billion parameters.
  Our measurements show that fixed-schema structures transfer reliably, with KG-fixed accuracy changing by only $+0.0004 \pm 0.0020$ following a writer swap. Conversely, compressed NOTES exhibit high model coupling, with accuracy shifting asymmetrically by $+9.91$ or $-13.28$ percentage points depending on the specific migration direction. In RAG systems, partial embedding migrations using a 50/50 mixed index capture only a 4.96-point accuracy improvement, forfeiting the majority of the 11.90-point gain achieved through full re-embedding. Diagnostic decomposition attributes 80% ($0.467 \pm 0.014$) of the NOTES accuracy deficit to information lost during initial construction, whereas retrieval failures drive 81% ($0.364 \pm 0.012$) of the RAG deficit. Finally, store-only repair of NOTES fails to reach a 90% performance recovery target in all 48 test cases, whereas retaining the raw source history enables successful recovery in 34 of 48 cases for one tested direction. These findings highlight the necessity of direction-specific migration testing, strict embedding space isolation, and the retention of source histories for memory repair.

</details>


### [102] [Who Should Grade My Work? Student Perspectives on Transparent AI-Assisted Writing Assessment in Higher Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05346)
*Rayed AlGhamdi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Students accept GenAI feedback as useful for surface-level revision but view human instructors as the true grading authority, distinguishing between feedback utility and evaluative authority.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While prior research has examined GenAI feedback's impact on writing performance and student perceptions, little is known about how students interpret AI-mediated evaluation when explicitly informed that feedback comes from AI rather than a human instructor.

Method: Qualitative pedagogical inquiry with 13 male computing students in a Saudi university technical communication course. Students completed a handwritten writing task, received ChatGPT-generated scores and feedback using a rubric-based prompt, were explicitly told the evaluation came from AI, and then wrote reflections analyzed through inductive thematic analysis.

Result: Analysis revealed four themes: perceived usefulness of feedback; awareness of AI's contextual and pedagogical limitations; conditional trust (distinguishing feedback utility from evaluative authority); and reflection on the human instructor's institutional and pedagogical role. Students accepted AI feedback for surface-level revision but positioned human instructors as the appropriate grading authority.

Conclusion: Students treat feedback utility and evaluative authority as analytically separate judgments rather than opposite ends of a single approval scale, preferring to use AI feedback for revision while reserving final grading decisions for human instructors.

Abstract: The integration of GenAI tools into higher education assessment raises important questions about how students understand, interpret, and respond to AI-mediated evaluation. As instructors increasingly explore AI tools for providing feedback, prior research has examined whether GenAI-generated feedback improves writing performance and how students perceive its usefulness; comparatively little is known, however, about how students interpret such evaluation when they are explicitly informed that an AI system, rather than a human instructor, produced the feedback and the score. This study reports findings from a qualitative pedagogical inquiry conducted in an undergraduate technical communication course for computing students at a Saudi public university. Thirteen male undergraduate computing students completed an in-class handwritten writing task; the scanned submissions were evaluated by ChatGPT using a rubric-based prompt aligned with the task objectives. Students were then explicitly informed that ChatGPT had generated the score and feedback and were invited to reflect on the evaluation in writing. Inductive thematic analysis of these reflections identified four themes: perceived usefulness of feedback; awareness of AI's contextual and pedagogical limitations; conditional trust, distinguishing feedback utility from evaluative authority; and reflection on the institutional and pedagogical role of the human instructor. Participants accepted GenAI feedback as useful for surface-level revision but consistently positioned the human instructor as the appropriate authority over grading decisions. The study identifies this as a distinction between feedback utility and evaluative authority, two judgments that students treat as analytically separate rather than as opposite ends of a single approval scale...

</details>


### [103] [CUA-Universe: A Scalable and Dynamic Environment for Hybrid GUI+CLI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05374)
*Haoting Shi,Wenhao Wang,Weicheng Fang,Yaozhong Liang,Tian Jin,Pengxiang Zhao,Guangyi Liu,Siheng Chen,Yanfeng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CUA-Universe is a scalable pipeline that transforms real desktop software into hybrid GUI+CLI environments, enabling training of computer agents that effectively coordinate both interfaces for more efficient task execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current computer-use agents rely heavily on inefficient GUI interactions, ignoring the hybrid nature of real-world computer work that combines visual inspection with command-line operations. Existing approaches lack scalable hybrid environments and agents struggle to use GUI and CLI interfaces complementarily.

Method: Three-component pipeline: App-Forge adapts real applications into reproducible VMs with command-line surfaces; Task-Weave synthesizes diverse hybrid tasks of varying difficulty from reusable operations; Path-Steer steers rollouts along efficient hybrid paths and collects verified trajectories for training.

Result: Training on CUA-Universe data significantly improves agent performance: 9B model shows +39.3 pts score improvement with 37% fewer steps and 60% fewer tokens on CUA-Verse; +16.8 pts success rate improvement on OSWorld with 57% fewer steps; +7.84 pts score on OSWorld-MCP with 27% fewer steps.

Conclusion: CUA-Universe provides a scalable approach to developing more capable and efficient computer-use agents by enabling effective GUI+CLI orchestration, addressing the hybrid nature of real-world computer tasks through automated environment generation and trajectory collection.

Abstract: Computer-use agents have advanced on benchmarks like OSWorld and AndroidWorld, but still act mostly through the GUI, often producing inefficient trajectories. Real-world computer work is hybrid, combining visual-state inspection with precise, high-throughput command-line operations, so capable agents must coordinate both modalities over shared application state. Yet scalable hybrid environments remain scarce because supporting both GUI and CLI over real applications typically requires substantial manual engineering for each application. Existing agents also struggle to use the two interfaces complementarily: CLI-native agents lack visual perception for tasks involving interface state or layout, while GUI-native agents are inefficient for operations better executed through commands. We introduce CUA-Universe, a scalable environment-to-data pipeline that turns real desktop software into hybrid GUI+CLI environments. App-Forge adapts applications into reproducible VMs and command-line surfaces it discovers, wraps, or generates, scaling to 16 applications; Task-Weave synthesizes diverse hybrid tasks of controllable difficulty from reusable operations over seed files; and Path-Steer steers rollouts along efficient hybrid paths and harvests verified trajectories for post-training. Training on this data shifts behavior from inefficient GUI interaction and brittle CLI scripting toward effective GUI+CLI orchestration. Our 9B model improves both success and efficiency on CUA-Verse (Score +39.3 pts; -37% steps, -60% tokens), OSWorld (SR +16.8 pts; -57% steps, -44% tokens), and OSWorld-MCP (Score +7.84 pts; -27% steps, -30% tokens). CUA-Universe provides a scalable path toward more capable and efficient computer-use agents.

</details>


### [104] [Molecular Déjà Vu: Digit-Level Retrieval of Published Values in Frontier Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05381)
*Matthias Busch,Marius Tacke,Sviatlana V. Lamaka,Mikhail L. Zheludkevich,Christian J. Cyron,Roland C. Aydin,Christian Feiler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs exhibit widespread verbatim retrieval in molecular property benchmarks, with retrieval varying by dataset and reasoning level, and suppressing retrieval reduces performance differences between models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of LLMs on molecular property benchmarks cannot distinguish between genuine prediction capability and simple verbatim retrieval of published values, which raises concerns about the validity of these benchmarks for assessing true predictive power.

Method: The researchers audited 22 frontier models on 12 regression benchmarks to detect verbatim retrieval. They conducted experiments at two different reasoning levels, tested methods to interrupt retrieval using transformed SMILES strings, and analyzed how suppression of retrieval affects prediction errors.

Result: 1) Verbatim retrieval is widespread but benchmark-specific - over 50% of LLMs show retrieval on 5 datasets, while minimal on others. 2) Higher reasoning levels increase retrieval detection by 89%. 3) Even with transformed inputs, strong models can still recognize original labels. 4) Suppressing retrieval reduces performance differences between models.

Conclusion: Verbatim retrieval significantly contaminates molecular regression benchmarks for LLMs, but general predictive capability isn't determined solely by memorized values. The study provides important insights into retrieval patterns and suggests that benchmark design needs to account for this phenomenon to properly evaluate true predictive abilities.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated on molecular property benchmarks, but accuracy cannot distinguish a model that predicts a property from one that retrieves a published number. We audit 22 frontier models on 12 regression benchmarks for verbatim retrieval and find that it is widespread but relatively benchmark-specific: on five datasets more than $50\%$ of the LLMs show verbatim retrieval, while on the remaining datasets it appears only in isolated cells. We run our experiments at two reasoning levels and find that reasoning changes retrieval. The same experiments, on the same molecules and with the same prompt, are flagged $89\%$ more often at the higher reasoning level than at the lowest one. Finally, we test a way to interrupt retrieval in our most contaminated cases, and find that the strongest models in some cases still recognise a combination of transformed SMILES strings and original labels. Furthermore, suppressing retrieval moves the prediction errors of the different models closer together in relative terms, while their differing use of verbatim retrieval spreads them apart. This indicates that the general predictive capability of an LLM is not determined solely by the amount of memorised values. This work provides an overview of the amount and depth of verbatim retrieval in molecular regression benchmarks using LLMs.

</details>


### [105] [Necessary or Sufficient? Evaluating LLM Explanations With Behavioural Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05385)
*Urja Pawar,Rajitha Ramanayake,Nabeel Kemal,Ashwin Kandath,Owen O'Neill,Guillaume Bourgeon,Houssem Chatbri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM decision explanations often don't reliably identify the most influential factors - necessity (changing factor changes output) and sufficiency (retaining factor preserves output) - according to black-box testing on synthetic use cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM decision components in agent workflows produce action-relevant recommendations with explanations. Users assume these explanations agree with observable decision behavior, but this assumption needs empirical validation.

Method: Test two interpretations of named factors in explanations: necessity (changing factor changes output) and sufficiency (retaining factor while removing other information preserves output). Evaluate in synthetic use cases: advisor recommendations and prompt harmfulness judgments. Use controlled black-box interventions to estimate necessity and sufficiency scores by measuring how often changing/retaining factors affects outputs across eight LLMs (Claude, GPT, Gemini families).

Result: Mean Spearman correlations between cited ranking and necessity/sufficiency scores: 0.349/0.354 for advisor recommendation, 0.431/0.580 for prompt monitoring. Uncited factors score above lowest-scoring cited factors in 57.6%/58.1% of advisor responses (necessity/sufficiency) and 25.8%/8.9% for prompt monitoring. Cited top-three contain useful information but don't reliably identify the three most influential factors.

Conclusion: LLM decision explanations have moderate agreement with measured factor influence but are not reliable indicators of the most influential factors. The black-box testing framework provides a practical reliability check for explanations used in agent oversight while remaining scoped to individual LLM decisions.

Abstract: LLM decision components that can operate within agent workflows often produce action-relevant recommendations or judgements together with explanations. Operators may use the named factors to monitor a system, diagnose errors, or decide when to escalate an output. Such use assumes that the explanations agree with the component's observable decision behaviour. We test two interpretations of the named factors: necessity, meaning that changing a factor would change the output, and sufficiency, meaning that retaining it while removing other changeable information would preserve the output. We evaluate these interpretations in two synthetic use cases: recommending advisors to clients and judging prompts for harmfulness or risk. Models return an output and the top three factors that most influenced it. Controlled black-box interventions estimate a necessity score for each factor by measuring how often changing it changes the output, and a sufficiency score by measuring how often retaining it preserves the output. Across eight models from the Claude, GPT, and Gemini families, the mean Spearman correlations between the cited ranking and the necessity and sufficiency scores are 0.349 and 0.354 for advisor recommendation, and 0.431 and 0.580 for prompt monitoring. Furthermore, an uncited factor scores above the lowest-scoring cited factor in 57.6% of advisor responses under necessity and 58.1% under sufficiency; the corresponding prompt-monitoring rates are 25.8% and 8.9%. The cited top three contain useful information but do not reliably identify the three factors with the strongest measured influence under necessity or sufficiency. The framework provides a black-box reliability check for explanations used in agent oversight while remaining scoped to individual LLM decisions.

</details>


### [106] [Multi-Step Tool-Calling over Korean Open Public APIs: A Benchmark and a Data-Synthesis Recipe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05395)
*Dain Kim,Eungi Cho,Kyumin Kim,Shinyeong Noh,Kyuseong Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EDGE (Execution-grounded Dynamic Graph) is a method for synthesizing multi-step tool-calling data from live APIs, used to fine-tune a 9B model that nearly matches untuned 27B performance on government API benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-sovereignty regulations require public institutions to use open-source, on-premise LLM agents that chain multiple tool calls across live government APIs, but open-source models underperform in multi-step settings with no existing benchmark to measure the gap.

Method: EDGE builds a graph showing how each tool's output can feed another's input, verifies links by actually calling them against live APIs, and traverses verified links to synthesize executable multi-step trajectories. The resulting dataset is used to fine-tune a 9B model via GRPO.

Result: The fine-tuned 9B model nearly matches the untuned 27B model from the same family, showing substantial improvement on both the introduced KOPA-Bench (Korean Open Public API Benchmark) and the BFCL benchmark.

Conclusion: EDGE effectively addresses the performance gap of open-source models in multi-step tool-calling scenarios for government APIs, enabling smaller models to achieve performance comparable to much larger models through targeted data synthesis and fine-tuning.

Abstract: Data-sovereignty regulations increasingly require public institutions to deploy open-source, on-premise LLM agents that chain multiple tool-calls across live government APIs. However, open-source models consistently underperform in this multi-step setting, and no existing benchmark measures the gap. We introduce the Korean Open Public API Benchmark (KOPA-Bench), comprising 145 real-world tasks. To close this gap, we present EDGE, an Execution-grounded Dynamic Graph for tool-calling data synthEsis driven by live execution. EDGE builds a graph of how each tool's output can feed another's input, keeps only the links that succeed when actually called against the live APIs, and traverses these verified links to synthesize executable multi-step trajectories. Fine-tuned via GRPO on the resulting dataset, our 9B model nearly matches the untuned 27B model from the same family, improving substantially not only on KOPA-Bench but also on the BFCL benchmark.

</details>


### [107] [A Deep Generative Model for Synthesizing Labeled Wireless Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05396)
*Yuxiao Li,Keke Hu,Santiago Mazuelas,Yuan Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IIns-GAN: A deep learning-based method to generate realistic labeled wireless signals that adapt to different environments, improving model training for wireless sensing tasks like distance estimation and environment identification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Acquiring real-world wireless signal datasets with position labels is expensive and challenging due to measurement and labeling costs. Traditional synthesis methods based on environmental models require extensive hyper-parameter tuning and lack realism for comprehensive model training.

Method: Proposed Inter-Instance Generative Adversarial Networks (IIns-GAN), a deep learning-based approach to generate realistic labeled wireless signals that adapt to different environment scenarios.

Result: Extensive experiments on public Ultra-Wideband datasets show that IIns-GAN-generated signals mirror physical characteristics of real-world measurements and significantly improve model training performance for wireless sensing tasks like distance estimation and environment identification.

Conclusion: IIns-GAN successfully addresses the limitations of traditional wireless signal synthesis methods by generating realistic, adaptive labeled signals that enhance model training in diverse wireless sensing applications.

Abstract: Wireless signals with position-related labels are pivotal for both performance evaluation and model training in the realm of wireless sensing. However, acquiring real-world datasets is often challenged by significant measurement and labeling costs. Traditional methods for synthesizing labeled wireless signals typically rely on environmental models, leading to extensive hyper-parameter tuning and inadequate realism for comprehensive model training purposes. To address these limitations, we introduce a novel deep learning (DL)-based method, namely Inter-Instance Generative Adversarial Networks (IIns-GAN), to generate realistic labeled wireless signals. The generated signals are particularly adaptive to different environment scenarios and well-suited for various model training tasks, including distance estimation and environment identification. We have conducted extensive experiments on public Ultra-Wideband (UWB) datasets to evaluate the realism and utility of the generated signals. The results demonstrate that the signals generated by IIns-GAN mirror the physical characteristics of real-world measurements, and significantly contribute to the improvement of model training in diverse wireless sensing tasks.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [108] [Spectral-Target Physical Latent Structuring for JEPA-Style World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04264)
*Penghao Zhu,Salvatore Penachio,Kaustav Mukherjee,Aneesh Jonelagadda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adding Fourier auxiliary head during training eliminates physical representation laziness in latent world models, improving planning success in dynamic environments without inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Even with regularization preventing representation collapse, latent world models exhibit "physical representation laziness" where latent states fail to represent key physical properties, causing planning failures in dynamic environments.

Method: Propose training-time auxiliary supervision with a lightweight "Fourier auxiliary head" that enforces physically-informed structuring of latent space without additional inference-time cost. This can be generalized to any environment.

Result: Auxiliary head substantially improves planning success rates in dynamic environments where baseline LeWM exhibits laziness, shows modest improvements elsewhere, increases latent space correlations with physical properties, and is particularly effective in low-data regimes.

Conclusion: Fourier auxiliary head method improves overall success rate and data efficiency while avoiding representation laziness in latent world models, enabling better physical structuring of latent representations.

Abstract: Latent world models have become increasingly popular as a method to predict and plan in latent space rather than pixel space. Recent architectures, such as LeWorldModel (LeWM), jointly train the encoder and predictor using regularization techniques like SIGReg to prevent representation collapse. Even with such regularization preventing representation collapse, we identify a new world model failure mode of \textit{physical representation laziness}, particularly noted in highly dynamic environments. For these lazy cases, the learned latent states do not collapse but nonetheless fail to represent key physical properties, causing ubiquitous downstream planning failure. To resolve this issue, we propose training-time auxiliary supervision with a lightweight "Fourier auxiliary head", which enforces physically-informed structuring of the latent space with no additional inference-time cost and can be generalized to any environment. Experimentally, we show that the auxiliary head substantially improves planning success rates in dynamic environments where the baseline LeWM exhibits physical representation laziness. It also leads to modest improvements in other environments, even when the baseline does not exhibit physical representation laziness. We further observe superior planning performance being accompanied by higher latent space correlations with key physical properties, indicating both the ability of our method to physically structure latent states and the potential planning-side benefit to the learned representation being physically structured. We also see in low-data regimes, auxiliary supervision is particularly impactful in increasing success rate. These findings support the use of our Fourier auxiliary head method to improve both overall success rate and data efficiency, while avoiding representation laziness in latent world models.

</details>


### [109] [ProToMEx: Rapid, Interpretable Explanations via Structured Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04265)
*Athina Georgara,Adarsh Valoor,Sarvapali D. Ramchurn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProToMEx introduces a novel explainability paradigm using Probabilistic Topic Models to learn latent topics as classification reasons, providing both global and local explanations while being dramatically faster than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current post-hoc explainers focus on feature attribution which struggles to articulate complex combinatorial patterns that drive model decisions. Feature importance approaches lack the ability to reveal underlying semantic structures and multiple co-existing reasons for predictions.

Method: ProToMEx leverages Probabilistic Topic Models (PTMs) to learn latent "topics" that represent distinct, high-level reasons for classification. It's a model-agnostic framework that moves beyond feature importance to reveal semantic structures and provides both global and local explanations.

Result: ProToMEx produces explanations with comparable fidelity to SHAP and LIME while drastically reducing computational cost (~30-40x faster) over tabular and synthetic datasets. It enables both global model behavior explanations and local explanations that disentangle multiple co-existing reasons for predictions.

Conclusion: ProToMEx offers a superior explainability paradigm that reveals underlying semantic patterns beyond simple feature attribution, provides comprehensive global and local explanations, and is highly suitable for real-time applications due to its dramatically improved computational efficiency.

Abstract: Existing post-hoc explainers for machine learning classifiers primarily focus on feature attribution, assigning importance scores to individual features. While valuable, this approach struggles to articulate the complex, combinatorial patterns that often drive a model's decision-making process. To overcome this limitation, we introduce ProToMEx, a new paradigm for explainability that leverages Probabilistic Topic Models (PTMs). Our model-agnostic framework learns latent ''topics'' that represent distinct, high-level reasons for a classification, moving beyond simple feature importance to reveal underlying semantic structures. ProToMEx naturally provides both global explanations of a model's overall behaviour and local explanations that can disentangle multiple co-existing reasons for a specific prediction. We demonstrate empirically that ProToMEx not only produces explanations of comparable fidelity to popular methods like SHAP and LIME but also drastically reduces the amortised computational cost of generating local explanations, making it highly suitable for real-time applications. Specifically, we show that ProToMEx is ~30-40x faster than SHAP and LIME over standardised tabular datasets and synthetic datasets.

</details>


### [110] [A Data Fusion Framework for Grounding Aerospace Surrogate Model via Experimental Wind-Tunnel Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04267)
*Nitin Nagesh Kulkarni,Dheeraj Vemula,Yin Yu,Peter Lyu,Juan J. Alonso*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework that adapts CFD-trained aerodynamic surrogate models to experimental data using a correction network trained on wind-tunnel PSP measurements, improving agreement with experiments while preserving generalization from simulation data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aerodynamic surrogate models trained on high-fidelity CFD data accurately reproduce numerical predictions but have systematic discrepancies with experimental observations, limiting their predictive fidelity for real-world applications.

Method: Developed an experimentally grounded correction framework using a Geotransolver surrogate trained on 2,300 CFD simulations of NASA CRM wing-body configuration. A correction network is trained on spatially registered PSP measurements at two Mach numbers (0.70 and 0.85) across angle-of-attack range 0-4°, learning the discrepancy between surrogate-predicted and experimentally measured surface-pressure distributions without retraining the base surrogate.

Result: The correction substantially improves agreement with PSP measurements, particularly at wing suction peak, shock location, and pressure recovery regions. At Mach 0.85, it reduces prediction error magnitude and area with error >0.05 in Cp. On held-out angles of attack, the grounded surrogate agrees within 2.3-2.7% of measured Cp range and outperforms direct interpolation between measured conditions.

Conclusion: Experimental measurements can ground large-scale simulation-trained surrogates by learning systematic CFD-to-experiment discrepancies while preserving their generalization capability and computational efficiency, enabling more accurate aerodynamic predictions.

Abstract: Aerodynamic surrogate models trained on high-fidelity CFD data reproduce numerical predictions of both scalar outputs and entire fields accurately, yet their predictive fidelity is limited by systematic discrepancies between CFD and experimental observations. We present an experimentally grounded correction framework that adapts a CFD-trained deep learning surrogate using wind-tunnel PSP measurements. A Geotransolver surrogate trained on 2,300 high-fidelity CFD simulations of the NASA CRM wing-body configuration, spanning geometric variation, Mach 0.70-0.85, and angles of attack 0 to 4 degrees, reproduces the CFD integrated aerodynamic forces and pitching moment to R2 > 0.99 but does not match the experimental data. To incorporate experimental information without retraining the surrogate, a correction network is trained on spatially registered PSP measurements at two freestream Mach numbers (0.70 and 0.85) across the same angle-of-attack range, learning the discrepancy between the surrogate-predicted and experimentally measured surface-pressure distributions. At Mach 0.85 the correction substantially improves agreement with PSP, particularly at the wing suction peak, shock location, and subsequent pressure recovery, reducing both the magnitude of the prediction error and the fraction of wetted surface on which it exceeds 0.05 in Cp, and it does so from a limited experimental dataset without modifying the pretrained surrogate parameters. On held-out angles of attack the grounded surrogate agrees with measurement to within 2.3-2.7% of the measured Cp range, and outperforms direct interpolation between the measured conditions at every state tested. Experimental measurements can therefore ground a large-scale simulation-trained surrogate by learning systematic CFD-to-experiment discrepancies while preserving its generalization capability and computational efficiency.

</details>


### [111] [Quantum-Assisted Memory-Efficient Training for Parameter-Intensive Wi-Fi-Based Human Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04271)
*To Truong An,Jie Zhang,Guolin Yin,Junqing Zhang,Yanjiao Li,Trung Q. Duong,Simon L. Cotton*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Q-MET: A quantum-assisted framework that reduces Wi-Fi HAR model parameters by 90-95% in training and achieves 75-85% sparsity in inference with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Wi-Fi-based human activity recognition systems rely on computationally intensive deep learning models that are challenging to deploy in real-world scenarios due to high memory consumption in both training (simultaneous updates of millions of parameters) and inference phases.

Method: Q-MET uses a hybrid quantum-classical neural network to indirectly generate parameters for HAR models instead of directly optimizing them, significantly reducing trainable parameters. It also integrates structured pruning during training to further support deployment on resource-constrained devices.

Result: Achieves 90-95% reduction in trainable parameters compared to conventional DL training while maintaining or exceeding classical classification accuracy. Supports lightweight inference with 75-85% model sparsity while losing less than 2% classification accuracy.

Conclusion: Q-MET represents the first quantum-assisted approach to simultaneously address memory inefficiencies in both training and inference stages of Wi-Fi HAR systems, enabling more practical deployment on resource-constrained devices.

Abstract: Wi-Fi-based human activity recognition (HAR) has become an important part of integrated sensing and communications, paving the way for a range of context-aware services. However, most existing Wi-Fi-based HAR systems rely on deep learning (DL) models that are computationally and memory intensive in both training and inference, which poses significant challenges for real-world deployment. Conventional training requires simultaneous updates of millions of parameters, leading to prohibitive memory consumption. In this paper, we propose a novel quantum-assisted memory-efficient training framework (Q-MET) designed to improve efficiency in both training and inference. Q-MET utilizes a hybrid quantum classical neural network to indirectly generate parameters for HAR models, significantly reducing the trainable parameter count compared to direct optimization. To further support the deployment on resource-constrained devices, we integrate structured pruning during the training phase. Experimental results demonstrate that Q-MET achieves a 90% to 95% reduction in trainable parameters compared with conventional backpropagation-based DL training while maintaining or even exceeding classical classification accuracy. Additionally, Q-MET supports lightweight inference through structured pruning, achieving 75% to 85% model sparsity with less than 2% loss in classification accuracy. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents the first quantum-assisted approach to simultaneously tackle memory inefficiencies in both the training and inference stages of HAR systems.

</details>


### [112] [Evaluating Large Language Models for Forced Outage Risk Prediction: Benefits and Comparison to Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04272)
*Christos Petridis,Zoran Obradovic,Mladen Kezunovic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs can predict weather-related grid outages in zero-shot setting without labeled data, achieving competitive performance to supervised models with added benefits in reasoning and scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need to assess whether large language models can effectively predict forced outages in distribution grids from weather data without requiring labeled training data, which is often expensive or unavailable.

Method: Formulated as binary severity classification task using 6 years of outage records and high-resolution weather data for central Texas utility. Tested 4 zero-shot LLMs vs 2 supervised classifiers across 3 forecast horizons (3h, 6h, 12h) and two input configurations (current observations and forecast data).

Result: Supervised models outperform LLMs on macro-F1 and precision metrics, but newer LLM generations achieve competitive scores. LLMs offer complementary strengths in actionable reasoning and geographic scalability beyond just accuracy metrics.

Conclusion: While supervised models remain superior in pure accuracy, LLMs show promising zero-shot capabilities for outage prediction. The best practice likely involves combining supervised models with LLMs to leverage both accuracy and the reasoning/scalability benefits of LLMs.

Abstract: This study examines the ability of large language models (LLMs) to predict the risk of weather-related forced outages in the distribution grid in a zero-shot framework, without labeled training data. The problem is formulated as a binary severity classification task across three forecast horizons (3h, 6h, 12h), using six years of outage records and high-resolution weather data for a utility service area in central Texas. Four zero-shot LLMs are benchmarked against two supervised classifiers across two input configurations: one using current weather observations and the other using weather forecast data. Results show that supervised models outperform LLMs on macro-F1 and precision, while newer LLM generations achieve competitive scores. Beyond accuracy, LLMs offer complementary strengths in actionable reasoning and geographic scalability, suggesting that combining them with supervised models may be the best practice.

</details>


### [113] [BER-PEF: Unified Human Mobility Predictability Evaluation via Bayes Error Rate Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04292)
*En Xu,Jingtao Ding,Zhiwen Yu,Yong Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BER-PEF is a framework that estimates mobility predictability using Bayes error rate when ground truth predictability is unavailable, providing a unified protocol to evaluate estimators across different data types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human mobility predictability ground truth is not directly observable in real mobility data, making it difficult to evaluate and compare different predictability estimators without a common reference.

Method: Converts BER estimation into mobility predictability estimation by mapping various data types (symbolic sequences, numeric trajectories, contextual features, learned representations) into a common feature-label space, then evaluates estimators against a shared predictability reference interval using controlled perturbation curves.

Result: BER-based estimators achieve lower reference discrepancy than existing methods on Foursquare NYC/TKY, GeoLife, and T-Drive datasets; estimates track changes in empirical prediction performance under perturbation; contextual inputs and multiple representations can be evaluated under same protocol.

Conclusion: BER-PEF offers a unified and verifiable path for evaluating predictability estimators on heterogeneous mobility data when ground-truth predictability is unavailable, providing more reliable estimator selection through evidence aggregation across perturbation levels.

Abstract: Human mobility predictability concerns the best prediction performance attainable from a given target and input information, but its ground truth is not directly observable on real mobility data. We present BER-PEF, a Bayes-error-rate-based framework that converts BER estimation into mobility predictability estimation and provides a unified protocol for comparing estimators without observable ground truth. The framework maps symbolic sequences, numeric trajectories, contextual features, and learned representations into a common feature--label space, then evaluates estimator outputs along controlled perturbation curves against a shared predictability reference interval by measuring deviations below the interval, above the interval, and across the full interval. Experiments on Foursquare NYC and TKY, GeoLife, and T-Drive show that several BER-based estimators achieve lower reference discrepancy than existing predictability methods on symbolic sequences and numeric trajectories, while their estimates track changes in empirical prediction performance under perturbation. Additional analyses show that contextual inputs and multiple structured representations can be evaluated under the same protocol, and that aggregating evidence across multiple perturbation levels provides a more reliable basis for estimator selection than relying on a single unperturbed observation. BER-PEF therefore offers a unified and verifiable path for evaluating predictability estimators on heterogeneous mobility data when ground-truth predictability is unavailable.

</details>


### [114] [Data-Driven Learning of Unknown Nonlinear Differential Equations Using Functional Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04329)
*Seyyed Shaho Alaviani,Yongzhi Qu,Gregory W. Vogl*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new interpretable ML method for discovering nonlinear ODEs from single trajectory data using functional analysis and continuous cost functions, with online learning capability for autonomous/non-autonomous systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for data-driven discovery of nonlinear ODEs without prior physics knowledge, addressing limitations of existing ML methods that use discrete-sum error formulations and lack interpretability.

Method: Proposes an interpretable ML method based on Functional Analysis and Operator Theory, using a continuous integral-based cost function (distance between functions) instead of discrete error sums. Includes an incremental learning algorithm for online handling of new training samples from single trajectory data.

Result: The method successfully discovers unknown vector fields from both forced/unforced autonomous and non-autonomous systems, simultaneously identifies unknown external forces as time functions, and numerical examples demonstrate advantages over existing approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed functional analysis-based approach provides a mathematically rigorous, interpretable framework for nonlinear dynamics discovery from limited data, with superior performance demonstrated through numerical examples and capability for online learning.

Abstract: In this paper, the problem of data-driven discovery of nonlinear ordinary differential equations (ODEs) is recast, and a new interpretable machine learning (ML) method is proposed. The proposed method aims to learn the unknown vector field of nonlinear dynamics without prior knowledge of the system's physics from only one single state trajectory's data. The proposed method has two fundamental differences with existing methods: 1) the formulation presented in this method is derived based on Functional Analysis and Operator Theory, and 2) the cost function is constructed in the function space as a distance between two functions as an integral, instead of the discrete-sum of errors used in existing ML approaches. An incremental learning algorithm is proposed to learn the unknown vector field to handle new training samples in an online manner. The proposed method can discover the unknown vector field from both forced and unforced autonomous and non-autonomous (or time-varying) dynamical systems. The proposed method is able to simultaneously discover unknown external forces as a function of time and unknown underlying dynamics. Finally, numerical examples are given to demonstrate the advantages of the proposed method.

</details>


### [115] [Modular Deep Recurrent Neural Network: Application to Quadrotors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04339)
*Nima Mohajerin,Steven L. Waslander*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A modular RNN framework with feedforward inter-layer connections improves learning of high-order dynamics and alleviates vanishing gradient issues, demonstrated on quadrotor altitude modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need to facilitate deployment of various RNN architectures and automatically compute derivatives for gradient-based learning. Existing RNNs struggle with learning high-order dynamics and nonlinearities, and suffer from vanishing/exploding gradient problems in multilayer configurations.

Method: Introduces a modular deep RNN framework that enables easy deployment of different architectures. Proposes adding feedforward inter-layer connections to multi-layer RNNs to create new network structures. The framework automatically computes derivatives for gradient-based learning methods.

Result: Feedforward inter-layer connections significantly improve RNN's capability to learn and model high-order dynamics and nonlinearities. The approach alleviates vanishing/exploding gradient problems in space for multilayer RNNs. Demonstrated superior performance on quadrotor altitude dynamics modeling where existing methods couldn't generalize as quickly or at all.

Conclusion: The modular RNN with feedforward inter-layer connections provides an effective solution for learning complex dynamical systems, offering better generalization and faster learning than existing methods while addressing fundamental RNN limitations.

Abstract: A modular deep Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) is introduced to facilitate the process of deploying various architectures of RNNs, and to automatically compute derivatives for gradient-based learning methods. The modularity leads to a set of new architectures, one of which includes feedforward inter-layer connections. By adding feedforward inter-layer connections in a multi-layer RNN, it is observed that the capability of the RNN to learn and model high-order dynamics and nonlinearities is significantly improved. The problem of vanishing/exploding gradient in space for a multilayer RNN is also alleviated using feedforward connections. These results are demonstrated using a quadrotor case study, for which a model of the altitude dynamics is learned with our particular network structure, while existing methods are unable to generalize as quickly or at all.

</details>


### [116] [SharedSAE: One Feature Dictionary Across Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04344)
*Daniil Ognev,Célian Vasson,Lijie Hu,Kentaro Inui,Benjamin Heinzerling*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SharedSAE enables using a single sparse autoencoder across multiple language models instead of training separate SAEs for each model, while maintaining high reconstruction quality and enabling cross-model latent description transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Currently, sparse autoencoders (SAEs) for interpreting language model activations require training separate SAEs for each individual model, which is inefficient and doesn't allow for cross-model comparison or knowledge transfer.

Method: SharedSAE uses a shared dictionary across models combined with model-specific encoder-decoder pairs. It normalizes only selection scores while preserving activation magnitudes, and uses model dropout for single-model inference. The approach was trained on four 1B-scale base language models from distinct families with different tokenizers.

Result: SharedSAE retains 96.6% of dedicated SAEs' mean explained variance, shows cross-model latent correlations 1.8x higher than post-hoc aligned separate SAEs, and enables latent description transfer across models. New models can be efficiently adapted to the frozen dictionary, achieving near-dedicated-SAE reconstruction quality.

Conclusion: A single shared sparse autoencoder can effectively serve multiple language models, enabling efficient cross-model interpretability analysis while maintaining high reconstruction performance, offering a more scalable approach to language model interpretability.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are widely used to interpret language model activations, but SAE training and latent labelling are typically repeated for every model. Here, we show that a single shared SAE can replace a collection of dedicated per-model SAEs. Our method, SharedSAE, combines a shared dictionary with model-specific encoder-decoder pairs. Unlike the closest prior method, which discards activation magnitudes and requires all models at inference, SharedSAE instead normalizes only selection scores, preserving magnitudes, and uses model dropout for single-model inference. We train SharedSAE on four 1B-scale base language models spanning distinct families and tokenizers. Despite sharing its latents across models, SharedSAE retains 96.6% of dedicated SAEs' mean explained variance; its latent activations exhibit cross-model correlations 1.8 times as high as separate SAEs aligned post-hoc, and its latent descriptions transfer across models. After the dictionary is frozen, new models can be efficiently adapted to it, achieving near-dedicated-SAE reconstruction quality while reusing the shared latent descriptions.

</details>


### [117] [A Quantum Variational Approach to Prototypical Recurrent Unit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04354)
*Mahyar Sadeghi Garjan,Tommaso Cesari,Michel Barbeau*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QPRU is a lightweight quantum recurrent unit with fewer parameters than classical and quantum alternatives, achieving competitive forecasting performance with enhanced scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical recurrent architectures (LSTM, GRU) and their quantum variants (QLSTM, QGRU) typically require many parameters, which limits scalability and efficiency. There's a need for more compact quantum recurrent units that maintain performance while reducing computational overhead.

Method: Introduces a Quantum Prototypical Recurrent Unit (QPRU) that uses a lightweight design requiring significantly fewer parameters than existing classical and quantum recurrent architectures.

Result: QPRU achieves competitive forecasting performance matching state-of-the-art baselines while offering structural advantages: enhanced scalability and reduced number of trainable parameters compared to both classical (LSTM, GRU) and quantum (QLSTM, QGRU) alternatives.

Conclusion: The QPRU provides an efficient quantum recurrent architecture that balances performance with parameter efficiency, offering a practical solution for quantum machine learning applications where computational resources are limited.

Abstract: We introduce a lightweight Quantum Prototypical Recurrent Unit (QPRU) that requires significantly fewer parameters than both classical recurrent architectures, such as Long Short- Term Memory (LSTM) and Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU), and quantum variants, including Quantum LSTM (QLSTM) and Quantum GRU (QGRU). Despite its compact design, the QPRU achieves competitive forecasting performance, matching state-of-the-art baselines while offering important structural and practical advantages, including enhanced scalability and a reduced number of trainable parameters.

</details>


### [118] [On the Abundance of Critical Points of the t-SNE Energy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04379)
*Nakul Haridas,Ryan Murray*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the complex energy landscape of t-SNE algorithms, showing that for symmetric data distributions, there exist infinite families of critical points that explain observed problematic behaviors like topology breaking and spurious clustering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: t-SNE is widely used but has a non-convex energy function that makes it difficult to rigorously understand what the algorithm captures. Well-known numerical examples show complicated energy landscapes with local minimizers that don't respect underlying data structure, which needs theoretical explanation.

Method: The authors analyze a general family of energies including t-SNE and its large data limits. For feature space densities with continuous symmetry, they construct infinite families of distinct critical points by identifying pairs of discrete symmetries in both feature and target spaces that are preserved under gradient dynamics.

Result: The constructed critical points exhibit characteristics often observed empirically, such as topology breaking and spurious clustering. The approach is illustrated with numerical and analytical examples throughout the paper.

Conclusion: The work provides first steps toward rigorous explanation of t-SNE's complex behaviors by showing how symmetries lead to infinite families of critical points that manifest as undesirable phenomena like topology distortion and artificial clustering.

Abstract: This paper considers the energy landscape of the t-SNE algorithm. While this algorithm has enjoyed broad adoption, the non-convexity of the associated energy has made it difficult to rigorously understand what the algorithm captures in many settings. In particular, a number of well-known numerical examples, several of which are reproduced in this article, suggest a complicated energy landscape with many local minimizers that do not respect the topology or clustering structure of the underlying data. This work seeks to provide first steps towards a rigorous explanation of these phenomena. Specifically, for a general family of energies, which include both the original t-SNE algorithm and recently identified large data limits, and for densities in feature space which obey a continuous symmetry, we construct infinite families of distinct critical points. These critical points are based upon identifying pairs of discrete symmetries, one in the original feature space and the other in the target embedding space, which are preserved under gradient dynamics. These critical configurations exhibit many characteristics, such as topology breaking and spurious clustering, which are often observed empirically. Finally, numerical and analytical examples are given throughout as a means of illustrating the approach.

</details>


### [119] [Disentangling Attention in Deep Operator Learning: A Controlled Study of Data-Driven and Physics-Informed Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04407)
*Amar Alem Koric,Qibang Liu,Seid Koric*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematic study shows that cross-attention mechanisms consistently improve DeepONet accuracy across various PDE problems, while self-attention benefits are more situational and dependent on input complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need to isolate and understand the specific effects of different attention mechanisms in neural operators, as previous studies often introduced multiple architectural changes simultaneously, making it unclear which components actually improve accuracy.

Method: Conducted controlled study of five DeepONet variants with distinct attention mechanisms, testing under both data-driven and physics-informed regimes. Evaluated cross-attention, self-attention, tokenization strategies, and attention depth on three benchmark problems: 1D nonlinear diffusion-reaction, 1D viscous Burgers with variable initial conditions, and 2D Poisson heat-conduction with heterogeneous source fields.

Result: Per-sensor tokenization with cross-attention reduced mean relative L₂ error by factors of 2.4-28.0 compared to classical DeepONet. Best attention configurations achieved 3.5-32.3x error reduction. Branch self-attention showed inconsistent performance - helpful for complex 2D problems but degraded 1D problems. Global pre-mixing provided no consistent benefits. Increased cross-attention depth improved accuracy but with diminishing returns and higher computational cost.

Conclusion: Query-dependent cross-attention is the most reliable attention mechanism for neural operators, while branch self-attention is most beneficial for large, spatially complex functional inputs. Careful attention design can substantially improve neural operator accuracy across different training regimes and problem complexities.

Abstract: Deep neural operators learn mappings between input functions and complete PDE solution fields, enabling forward evaluations of new problem instances orders of magnitude faster than conventional numerical solvers. Attention mechanisms have recently been introduced into neural operators, but most studies change several architectural components at once, making it difficult to identify what actually improves accuracy. This work presents a controlled and systematic study of five deep operator network (DeepONet) variants with distinct attention mechanisms, trained under both data-driven and physics-informed regimes, to isolate the effects of cross-attention, self-attention, tokenization, and attention depth. We evaluate them on a source-driven transient one-dimensional nonlinear diffusion-reaction equation, a transient one-dimensional viscous Burgers equation with variable initial conditions, and a two-dimensional Poisson heat-conduction problem with heterogeneous source fields. Per-sensor tokenization with cross-attention reduces the mean relative L_2 error of the classical DeepONet in all benchmark-training combinations by factors of 2.4-28.0, while the best attention configurations reach 3.5-32.3. Branch self-attention paired only with dot-product fusion is inconsistent, degrading the one-dimensional problems while helping the more complex two-dimensional source field; added on top of cross-attention it improves all six cases, though by less than cross-attention fusion alone. Global pre-mixing provides no consistent benefit. Increasing cross-attention depth further improves accuracy, but with diminishing returns and a substantially higher cost under physics-informed training. Overall, query-dependent cross-attention is the most reliable mechanism, whereas branch self-attention is most useful for large, spatially complex functional inputs.

</details>


### [120] [REFINE: LLM Refinement over Budgeted Text-Attributed Graphs for Personalized Medical Concept Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04415)
*Mohsen Nayebi Kerdabadi,Arya Hadizadeh Moghaddam,Dongjie Wang,Zijun Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: REFINE is a KG-aware budgeted LLM graph refinement framework that creates patient-personalized medical concept representations by selectively expanding knowledge graphs per patient and aligning semantic info with patient-specific relational structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing medical concept encoders process concepts uniformly across patients, but a code's meaning and predictive value depends on patient-specific clinical context and trajectory. Learning patient-personalized concept representations from text-attributed knowledge graphs (TKGs) introduces challenges: deciding how much KG context to incorporate per code, and aligning semantic information with patient-specific relational structure.

Method: REFINE constructs patient-specific temporal graphs from a global TKG. A sequential reinforcement learning policy selects personalized KG expansion budget for each observed code. The resulting patient graph is processed by a heterogeneous GNN to capture relation-aware structural dependencies, while a frozen LLM uses graph-aware soft prompts to semantically refine concept representations.

Result: Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV show REFINE consistently improves diverse EHR backbones, outperforms strong baselines, and demonstrates robust gains across component ablation, KG selection, and data insufficiency scenarios.

Conclusion: REFINE effectively addresses the challenges of patient-personalized medical concept encoding by integrating KG refinement with LLM-based semantic enhancement, leading to improved EHR prediction performance.

Abstract: Learning rich medical concept representations is essential for EHR prediction. Text-attributed knowledge graphs (TKGs) provide a natural foundation by organizing heterogeneous medical relations together with textual semantics. However, most existing encoders process concepts uniformly across patients, despite the fact that a code's meaning and predictive value depend on patient-specific clinical context and trajectory. Learning patient-personalized concept representations from TKGs introduces two key challenges: (1) deciding how much KG context to incorporate for each observed code, and (2) aligning semantic information with the patient-specific relational structure. We propose REFINE, a KG-aware budgeted LLM graph refinement framework for patient-personalized medical concept encoding. Starting from a global TKG, REFINE constructs patient-specific temporal graphs. A sequential reinforcement learning policy selects a personalized KG expansion budget for each observed code. The resulting patient graph is processed by a heterogeneous GNN to capture relation-aware structural dependencies, while a frozen LLM uses graph-aware soft prompts to semantically refine concept representations. Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV show that REFINE consistently improves diverse EHR backbones, outperforms strong baselines, and demonstrates robust gains across component ablation, KG selection, and data insufficiency.

</details>


### [121] [Beyond a Universal Forecasting Selector: Demand-Conditioned Model Selection across Demand Patterns and Horizons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04425)
*Adolfo González*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: No single model selection mechanism dominates all conditions; the choice of forecasting model selector should be context-dependent based on demand patterns, data availability, and forecasting horizons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecasting model selection is challenging in heterogeneous demand environments because the optimal decision rule varies with demand structure, data availability, and forecasting horizon. There's uncertainty about whether the selector itself should be treated as a context-dependent component rather than using a universal approach.

Method: Compared five selection mechanisms (RMSSE, ERA, OWA, CCG-AHSC, and CCG-AHSCD) across 24 optimized forecasting models, nine datasets, three training-testing partitions, and horizons from 1 to 12 cycles. Performance was evaluated ex post using Global Relative Accuracy (GRA), statistical tests, and a best-attainable-model reference.

Result: No single selector dominates across all conditions. CCG-AHSC and CCG-AHSCD perform better for Smooth demand and some Erratic configurations, while OWA and ERA excel in Intermittent and Lumpy demand settings. Selector suitability also varies with historical data availability and forecasting horizon.

Conclusion: The forecasting model selector should be treated as a context-dependent component rather than using a universal approach. The choice depends on demand patterns, available historical data, and forecasting horizon, supporting adaptive selection strategies for heterogeneous demand environments.

Abstract: Forecasting-model selection remains difficult in heterogeneous demand because the most suitable decision rule may vary with demand structure, data availability, and forecasting horizon. This study examines whether the selector itself should be treated as a context-dependent component of the forecasting process. Five selection mechanisms - RMSSE, ERA, OWA, CCG-AHSC, and CCG-AHSCD - are compared across 24 optimized forecasting models, nine datasets, three training-testing partitions, and horizons from 1 to 12 cycles. Selector performance is evaluated ex post using Global Relative Accuracy (GRA), statistical tests, and a best-attainable-model reference. No selector dominates across all conditions. CCG-AHSC and CCG-AHSCD are more competitive for Smooth demand and several Erratic configurations, whereas OWA and ERA perform better in Intermittent and Lumpy settings. Selector suitability also changes with historical data availability and horizon, supporting a context-dependent rather than universal approach to forecasting-model selection.

</details>


### [122] [A Repeated-Measurement Study for Cultural Analytics of English Song Lyrics Using Five Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04428)
*E. Cho Smith,Samuel Ho,Dawn Laux*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM-based cultural text annotation shows varying reliability across social constructs; self-esteem has strongest repeated-measurement reliability while seeking recognition is least stable, highlighting need to report measurement properties before treating LLM outputs as valid measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used to annotate cultural texts at scale, but before treating their outputs as measurements of latent social constructs, it's necessary to establish whether these measurements are reliable. Need to evaluate LLMs as zero-shot annotators for cultural analysis tasks.

Method: Evaluated five LLMs as zero-shot annotators of four social constructs in English song lyrics (self-esteem, self-control, seeking belonging, seeking recognition). Used repeated annotations of a large lyric corpus to examine: 1) consistency across repeated runs, 2) convergence across models, and 3) transferability of consensus labels to supervised classification.

Result: LLM-based measurement reliability varies significantly across constructs: self-esteem shows strongest repeated-measurement reliability across models; seeking recognition is generally less stable; self-control and seeking belonging show intermediate but model-dependent reliability. Consensus LLM labels contain learnable signal for downstream classification, though transferability doesn't establish construct validity.

Conclusion: LLM-based cultural text annotation is not uniformly reliable across different social constructs. Researchers must report repeated-measurement stability and cross-model convergence before treating LLM annotations as scalable measurements in cultural analytics. The reliability varies by construct, highlighting the importance of construct-specific validation.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to annotate cultural texts at scales that are impractical for human coders. However, before their outputs are treated as measurements of latent social constructs, it is necessary to establish whether those measurements are reliable. This study evaluates five LLMs as zero-shot annotators of four social constructs expressed in English song lyrics: self-esteem, self-control, seeking belonging, and seeking recognition. Using repeated annotations of a large lyric corpus, we examine three properties of LLM-based measurement: consistency across repeated runs, convergence across models, and transferability of consensus labels to supervised classification. The findings show that LLM-based measurement is not uniformly reliable across constructs. Self-esteem exhibits the strongest repeated-measurement reliability across models, while seeking recognition is generally less stable; self-control and seeking belonging show intermediate but model-dependent reliability. Downstream classification further indicates that consensus LLM labels contain learnable signal, although transferability does not itself establish construct validity. Repeated-measurement stability and cross-model convergence should therefore be reported before LLM annotations are treated as scalable measurements in cultural analytics.

</details>


### [123] [Conformity Breaks Conformal Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04445)
*Yibo Hu,Hanyu Su*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Conformal certificates can be invalidated when LLMs are exposed to peer pressure from unanimous wrong answers, causing coverage failures even when question distribution remains unchanged.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper identifies a vulnerability in conformal prediction for multi-agent LLM systems where peer pressure from unanimous wrong answers can cause score-mechanism shifts, breaking calibration without changing the underlying question distribution.

Method: The authors demonstrate this vulnerability through experiments with open-weight models on multiple-choice QA tasks, showing how coverage falls under unanimous-wrong peers. They analyze both average coverage failures and targeted attacks on low-confidence items, and examine decision layer impacts.
