### [148] [Learning-Augmented Algorithms: Guarantees, Construction Mechanisms, and System-Level Implications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04787)
*Hailiang Zhao,Peng Chen,Xueyan Tang,Jianwei Yin,Shuiguang Deng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Survey synthesizes learning-augmented algorithms across multiple domains, examining prediction interfaces, error measures, consistency-robustness trade-offs, and construction mechanisms, while separating formal guarantees from empirical evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning-augmented algorithms use fallible predictions while retaining formal performance guarantees, but there's a need to synthesize the field's development across different problem domains and establish a unified understanding of the theoretical framework.

Method: The survey synthesizes prediction interfaces, error measures, consistency-robustness trade-offs, and five representative construction mechanisms across online optimization, caching, learned data structures, graph problems, and mechanism design. It distinguishes achieved upper bounds from matched asymptotic dependence and separates formal guarantees from empirical systems evidence.

Result: The synthesis establishes sufficient conditions for limited end-to-end reasoning and delineates open problems in cost-aware prediction, endogenous error, semantic predictors, and benchmarking within the learning-augmented algorithms framework.

Conclusion: The survey provides a comprehensive synthesis of the learning-augmented algorithms field, organizing existing knowledge while identifying key open problems that need to be addressed for further advancement, particularly around prediction costs and semantic predictors.

Abstract: Learning-augmented algorithms use fallible predictions while retaining formal performance guarantees. This survey synthesizes prediction interfaces, error measures, consistency--robustness trade-offs, and five representative construction mechanisms across online optimization, caching, learned data structures, graph problems, and mechanism design. An orthogonal theorem-level axis distinguishes achieved upper bounds from matched asymptotic dependence. Formal guarantees are separated from empirical systems evidence, with explicit treatment of prediction cost, feedback, and composition. The resulting synthesis states sufficient conditions for limited end-to-end reasoning and delineates open problems in cost-aware prediction, endogenous error, semantic predictors, and benchmarking.

</details>


### [149] [How Faithful Is Attribution for Sales Forecasting? A Counterfactual Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04797)
*Glib Kechyn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adds a post-hoc counterfactual interpretability layer to multi-series WaveNet forecasters that decomposes forecasts into exact contributions, with rigorous faithfulness evaluation and honest assessment of interpretability limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep models like WaveNet for sales forecasting are accurate but opaque - they offer no explanation for why specific predictions are made, which limits trust and understanding of model behavior.

Method: Develops a post-hoc, architecture-agnostic counterfactual interpretability layer for multi-series WaveNet forecasters. Uses deletion/insertion protocol for faithfulness evaluation and avoids additive SHAP-style attribution artifacts.

Result: Method decomposes forecasts into exact contributions summing to predicted values. Shows statistically significant faithfulness (deletion gap 0.22, p<0.001; insertion gap 0.27, p<0.01). Reveals heterogeneous reliance on promotion signals and captures weekly sales cycle shape while underestimating amplitude.

Conclusion: The main contribution is an interpretability layer with rigorous faithfulness evaluation and candid acknowledgment of its limitations, rather than improved forecasting accuracy. Provides transparent insights into model behavior while honestly characterizing where attribution is and isn't informative.

Abstract: Deep models for sales forecasting, such as WaveNet-style dilated convolutional networks, are accurate but opaque: when a single model predicts sales for one of many series, it offers no account of why. We add a post-hoc, architecture-agnostic counterfactual interpretability layer to a multi-series WaveNet forecaster trained on the full Corporacion Favorita grocery dataset (174,685 series over 1,688 days). The method decomposes each forecast into contributions that sum exactly to the predicted value, avoiding the allocation artifacts we observed with additive SHAP-style attribution. We evaluate faithfulness with a deletion/insertion protocol and find a statistically significant effect on both tests (deletion gap 0.22, p<0.001; insertion gap 0.27, p<0.01; robust across five background-sampling seeds), establishing that the attributions reflect genuine model behavior rather than plausible-looking artifacts. We then characterize, honestly, where attribution is and is not informative: reliance on the promotion signal is heterogeneous across series (median ratio approximately 1.0, with roughly 20% of series showing a strong effect), and the model captures the shape of the weekly sales cycle (day-of-week r=0.78) while systematically under-predicting its amplitude. Our contribution is not improved accuracy but an interpretability layer with a rigorous faithfulness evaluation and a candid account of its limits.

</details>


### [150] [Federated Attack Campaign Detection via Contrastive Encoding of Threat Indicators in Gradient Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04815)
*Manuel Röder,Bibin Babu,Frank-Michael Schleif*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedIoC is a federated learning framework for detecting orchestrated cyberattack campaigns across organizations without sharing sensitive threat indicators, using contrastive learning on local IoC-matched flows and clustering gradient similarities at the server.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional cyberattack campaign detection requires sharing sensitive telemetry and threat intelligence across organizational boundaries, which creates privacy and security barriers. Federated Learning can overcome this by training shared threat detectors directly on local data without data sharing.

Method: FedIoC uses a modular framework where clients incorporate local structured threat indicators (IoCs) into gradient updates. Clients employ supervised contrastive loss over IoC-matched network flows - flows matching known indicators form positive sets, pulling their embeddings together while pushing non-IoC embeddings apart. The server then clusters clients' gradient updates by cosine similarity to recover global campaign patterns without direct IoC transmission.

Result: Evaluation on two public threat-detection benchmarks shows FedIoC successfully recovers cross-organizational campaign cohorts directly from gradient geometry. The framework works even when each client observes only fragments of active campaigns and holds disjoint indicator sets from local telemetry.

Conclusion: FedIoC demonstrates that non-IID gradient structure is the main driver for campaign recovery in federated settings. The paper contributes a modular framework for this problem domain and identifies the open challenge of designing encoders that can improve upon the gradient geometry approach.

Abstract: Detecting orchestrated cyberattack campaigns that span multiple organizations traditionally requires sharing sensitive telemetry and threat intelligence across institutional boundaries and country borders, a barrier that Federated Learning removes by training shared threat detectors directly on local data. We propose FedIoC, a modular framework in which clients fold locally available structured threat indicators into their gradient updates; we instantiate the client-side encoder with a supervised contrastive loss over IoC-matched flows. Within each training batch, flows that match any known indicator pattern form the positive set; the contrastive objective pulls their learned embeddings together and pushes non-IoC embeddings away, so that campaign-relevant structure is, by design, expressed in the gradient direction. Clients sharing indicators for the same attack campaign then produce aligned gradient components, which the server clusters by the cosine similarity of their updates to recover global campaign patterns without any direct IoC transmission. We evaluate FedIoC on two public threat-detection benchmarks distributed across FL clients that each observe only a fragment of every active campaign and hold disjoint indicator sets derived from their local telemetry. In this regime the FL server recovers cross-organizational campaign cohorts directly from gradient geometry. We contribute FedIoC as a modular framework for this setting, and use it to pinpoint the non-IID gradient structure as the main driver of recovery and to define the open problem of designing encoders that improve on it.

</details>


### [151] [Communication-Efficient Personalized Federated Learning via Layer-Wise Multi-Threshold Random Sketching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04830)
*Xu Zhang,Xingyu Hou,Jiacheng Cheng,Kaiyuan Feng,Maoguo Gong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel communication-efficient personalized federated learning framework using layer-wise multi-threshold random sketching to reduce communication costs while maintaining accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized federated learning (PFL) addresses data heterogeneity but suffers from high communication costs in bandwidth-constrained systems. Existing one-bit compression methods use a single threshold for the entire model, which overlooks layer-wise differences in parameter distributions and provides only coarse binary information.

Method: Proposes a communication-efficient PFL framework using layer-wise multi-threshold random sketching. Each layer gets its own set of quantization thresholds, allowing compressed representations to adapt to layer-specific statistics while using multiple intervals for finer low-bit description of sketched parameters.

Result: The method supports bidirectional communication using compact low-bit sketches and improves the communication-accuracy tradeoff compared to existing one-bit compression approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed layer-wise multi-threshold approach effectively addresses limitations of single-threshold methods by adapting to layer-specific characteristics, resulting in better communication efficiency while maintaining learning performance in personalized federated learning settings.

Abstract: Personalized federated learning (PFL) is a promising paradigm for collaborative learning over distributed devices, where edge nodes collaboratively train personalized models without sharing raw data. Although PFL addresses data heterogeneity by learning client-specific models, it still suffers from substantial uplink and downlink communication costs when exchanging high-dimensional parameters in bandwidth-constrained systems. Recent one-bit methods achieve extreme compression, but they usually rely on a single thresholding rule applied to the whole model. This design has two limitations. First, it overlooks layer-wise differences in parameter distributions and quantization sensitivities. Second, a single threshold provides only coarse binary information and cannot capture fine-grained variations in parameter distributions. To address these issues, we propose a communication-efficient PFL framework via layer-wise multi-threshold random sketching. In the proposed method, each layer is assigned its own set of quantization thresholds, so that the compressed representation can adapt to layer-specific statistics while using multiple intervals to provide a finer low-bit description of sketched parameters. The proposed method supports bidirectional communication using compact low-bit sketches and improves the communication-accuracy tradeoff compared with existing one-bit compression approaches.

</details>


### [152] [PACE: Propagation-Aware Collaborative Correction for One-Shot Personalized Federated Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04832)
*Ruizhe Huang,Chengran Li,Xiaochuan Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PACE is a one-shot federated graph learning method that treats collaborative knowledge as a compact correction to local models rather than replacement, using rank-r updates and convex calibration to selectively augment predictions when beneficial while preserving local predictions otherwise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Client heterogeneity in federated graph learning creates a tension: knowledge from other subgraphs can complement local models, but incompatible transfers can override reliable predictions. One-shot communication exacerbates this risk since unsuitable server returns cannot be corrected later.

Method: PACE uses rank-r update carriers and diagonal sketches of propagated message moments uploaded by clients. The server constructs propagation-aware, receiver-anchored corrections while clients retain full local models. Convex negative-log-likelihood calibration (CNLL) selects coefficients between local and external logits using validation nodes, keeping model parameters fixed with no feedback.

Result: At Rank-6, personalized returns occupy only 9.6-17.6% of dense tensor bytes across six datasets. The correction receives nonzero weight and improves both Accuracy and weighted-F1 over local models on five datasets; on ogbn-arxiv, CNLL assigns zero weight to correction, preserving local predictions exactly. Gains are not explained by CNLL alone on baseline models.

Conclusion: Small transported corrections can augment complete local models when receiver evidence supports them while leaving local predictions unchanged otherwise, demonstrating that collaborative knowledge can be effectively used as targeted corrections rather than replacements in personalized federated graph learning.

Abstract: Client heterogeneity creates both an opportunity and a risk in personalized federated graph learning. Knowledge held by other subgraphs may complement a receiver's Local model, but an incompatible transfer can override reliable predictions. One-shot communication sharpens this tension because an unsuitable server return cannot be corrected later. We introduce PACE, which treats collaborative knowledge as a compact correction to a complete Local predictor rather than as its replacement. Each client uploads a rank-r update carrier and a diagonal sketch of propagated message moments. The server uses them to construct a propagation-aware, receiver-anchored correction, while the receiver retains its full Local model. Convex negative-log-likelihood calibration (CNLL) then selects one coefficient between Local and External logits using validation nodes; model parameters remain fixed and no feedback is sent. At Rank-6, personalized returns occupy 9.6-17.6% of dense tensor bytes across the six evaluated datasets. The correction receives nonzero weight and improves both Accuracy and weighted-F1 over Local on five datasets; on ogbn-arxiv, CNLL assigns zero predictive weight to the correction and preserves Local predictions exactly. Applying the same CNLL rule to matched baselines on three citation datasets does not account for these gains. The central result is therefore that a small transported correction can augment a complete Local model when receiver evidence supports it while leaving the Local prediction unchanged otherwise.

</details>


### [153] [KVMem: Virtualizing Million-Token Agent Workspaces on a Consumer GPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04852)
*Di Chai,Leye Wang,Zeshen Su,Zhiguo Xia,Zhihang Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KVMem is a KV-context virtualization system that enables LLM agents to work with persistent workspaces exceeding GPU KV capacity and native context windows by paging historical KV states across GPU, host memory, and NVMe storage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern LLM agents operate in persistent workspaces with accumulated histories that exceed both GPU KV capacity and models' native context windows. Existing approaches either compact older context into summaries (losing execution evidence) or retrieve text later (requiring repeated prefilling), both causing inefficiencies.

Method: KVMem uses lightweight, model-native attention-space indexes to select relevant historical blocks and materializes query-dependent execution views bounded by the model's native context window. It preserves overflowed workspace history as paged KV state across GPU memory, host memory, and NVMe storage.

Result: Extensive evaluations on long-context agent benchmarks (LongMemEval, MemoryAgentBench, AgentLongBench) with histories up to 1M tokens show KVMem achieves higher task utility and greater inference efficiency than compaction-based approaches. On DeepSWE test with Qwen3.8-27B, KVMem improved task success from 43.8% to 48.4%. In local deployment, KVMem ran Qwen3.6/3.8-27B on a laptop with 24GB GPU, virtualizing 1M token workspaces (4x native context) while maintaining ~50 tokens/s generation speed.

Conclusion: KVMem decouples addressable workspace size from the LLM's native context window, providing a practical path toward long-running agents with workspaces that can grow beyond the model's inherent context limitations while maintaining interactive responsiveness.

Abstract: Modern LLM agents operate in persistent workspaces whose accumulated history can exceed both GPU KV capacity and the model's native context window. Existing systems typically compact older context into summaries or retrieve it later as text, either losing fine-grained execution evidence or repeatedly prefilling content that the model has already processed. We present KVMem, a KV-context virtualization system that preserves overflowed workspace history as paged KV state across GPU memory, host memory, and NVMe. KVMem uses lightweight, model-native attention-space indexes to select relevant historical blocks and materializes a query-dependent execution view bounded by the model's native context window. Extensive evaluations on long-context agent benchmarks spanning histories up to one million tokens, including LongMemEval, MemoryAgentBench, and AgentLongBench, show that KVMem generally achieves higher task utility and greater inference efficiency than compaction-based approaches, the de facto standard for handling context overflow. In the DeepSWE long-context test with Qwen3.8-27B, KVMem improves task success from 43.8% with compaction-only context management to 48.4%.
  In our local-deployment evaluation, KVMem runs Qwen3.6/3.8-27B NVFP4 with MTP on an off-the-shelf laptop equipped with a 24\,GB RTX 5090 Laptop GPU, virtualizing agent workspaces of up to 1M tokens-four times the model's native 256K-token context window. In a single-session setting, KVMem generates $\sim$50 tokens/s, providing interactive responsiveness for local agent execution. More broadly, by decoupling addressable workspace size from the LLM's native context window, KVMem provides a practical path toward long-running agents whose workspaces can grow beyond that window.

</details>


### [154] [When Genomic Masking Priors Fail to Transfer: Strong Variant Prediction, Weak Functional Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04861)
*Susu Hu,Preetam Gattogi,Jens Lehmann,Sahar Vahdati,Stefanie Speidel,Julien Vibert*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A bidirectional diffusion model for genomic modeling (GenDA) with entropy-guided span placement shows mixed results: it improves variant-effect prediction after fine-tuning but fails functional inpainting tests, revealing distinct requirements for prediction vs generation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bidirectional discrete diffusion models are naturally suited for genomic modeling because they can reconstruct missing sequences from both flanks. The authors hypothesize that entropy-guided span placement would concentrate reconstruction pressure on compositionally complex regions, improving variant-effect prediction and functional sequence generation.

Method: Developed GenDA (Genomic Density-optimized Absorbing Diffusion) with entropy-guided span placement. The 202M-parameter model was evaluated on variant-effect prediction (ClinVar SNV dataset) and functional inpainting tests across promoters, enhancers, exon boundaries, and intron boundaries.

Result: 1) After supervised fine-tuning, GenDA achieves 0.774 AUROC on ClinVar SNV prediction, beating autoregressive baseline by 0.103. 2) However, a random-span variant achieves 0.777 AUROC, showing entropy guidance isn't the cause of improvement. 3) GenDA fails zero-shot functional inpainting tests across genomic elements, not outperforming a control that preserves 3-mer composition. 4) Diagnostics reveal boundary conditions: entropy measures local complexity not functional importance, tokenization limits, training span caps, and high absolute fidelity with negative control-normalized restoration.

Conclusion: Strong fine-tuned variant prediction, plausible corruption prior, and functional generation are distinct claims requiring separate validation. The study shows that while diffusion models can perform well on supervised tasks, they may not generalize to functional inpainting, highlighting the need for careful evaluation of different genomic modeling objectives.

Abstract: Bidirectional discrete diffusion model appears naturally suited to genomic modeling because it can reconstruct missing sequence from both flanks. We developed GenDA (Genomic Density-optimized Absorbing Diffusion) under the additional hypothesis that entropy-guided span placement would concentrate reconstruction pressure on compositionally complex regions, improving both downstream variant-effect prediction and functional sequence generation. Our results only partially support this premise. After supervised fine-tuning, the 202M-parameter GenDA model reaches a pooled ClinVar SNV AUROC of 0.774, exceeding a similarly scaled autoregressive model by 0.103. However, a matched random-span variant reaches 0.777, providing no evidence that entropy guidance causes the ClinVar improvement. More unexpectedly, GenDA fails a zero-shot functional inpainting stress test: across promoters, enhancers, exon boundaries, and intron boundaries, it does not consistently outperform a control that shuffles the native gap while exactly preserving 3-mer composition. Failure is already present for 50--500-bp gaps, although enhancer degradation worsens at longer gaps. Diagnostics identify several boundary conditions: entropy measures local sequence complexity rather than functional importance; 1-mer tokenization limits physical context; training spans are capped at 300 bp; and high absolute AlphaGenome fidelity can coexist with negative control-normalized restoration. These results show that strong fine-tuned variant prediction, a plausible corruption prior, and functional generation are distinct claims that require separate validation.

</details>


### [155] [From Deep to Shallow: Unconstrained and Efficient Layer Merging Strategy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04881)
*Petro Shulzhenko,Gabriele Spadaro,Enzo Tartaglione*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes an efficient depth compression method for neural networks that overcomes limitations of existing approaches, enabling layer merging without increasing kernel size and handling convolutions with padding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep Neural Networks have high computational demands that limit their application in resource-constrained environments. Existing depth compression methods have two key limitations: they cannot handle convolutions with padding (no analytical solution) and they increase kernel size of merged layers, limiting speed-up gains.

Method: The authors propose an efficient strategy that enables merging of layers without requiring an analytical solution for convolutions with padding, and without increasing kernel size during the merging process.

Result: The approach was validated across multiple architectures and datasets, with inference speed-up gains measured on real embedded platforms. The code was publicly released on GitHub.

Conclusion: The proposed method successfully overcomes the limitations of existing depth compression techniques, providing an efficient solution for deploying neural networks in resource-constrained environments by enabling layer merging without kernel size increase and handling previously problematic cases like convolutions with padding.

Abstract: Although Deep Neural Networks have become foundational in many areas of Machine Learning, high computational demands limit their application in resource-constrained environments. To address this issue, depth compression methods have been proposed to identify and linearize redundant activation functions, thereby allowing for the merging of layers without intermediate non-linearities. However, these methods face two key challenges: they cannot be directly applied to convolutions with padding due to the absence of an analytical solution for merging these layers, and they typically increase the kernel size of merged layers, thus limiting speed-up gains. To overcome these limitations, we propose an efficient strategy that enables merging of layers without an existing analytical solution, and also without increasing kernel size. We validate our approach across multiple architectures and datasets, and measure inference speed-up gains on real embedded platforms. We publicly released the code at https://github.com/ShulzhenkoPetr/deep-to-shallow.

</details>


### [156] [Adaptation Interfaces for In-Context Tabular Foundation Models in Time-to-Event Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04901)
*Minh-Khoi Pham,Luca Cotugno,Dan Cernei,Alina Sirbu,Stefano Masi,Giuseppe Prencipe,Alessandro Pingitore,Patrizia Landi,Working Group on Uric Acid,Cardiovascular Risk of the Italian Society of Hypertension,Tai Tan Mai,Martin Crane,Marija Bezbradica*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tabular foundation models can be effectively adapted for survival analysis through various methods, with performance depending on data size and adaptation approach - CoxPH interface works best for larger datasets while zero-shot works on smaller ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tabular foundation models perform well on standard classification/regression but face challenges in censored time-to-event prediction due to censoring handling and event-time dynamics. The paper aims to extend TabFMs to survival analysis.

Method: Links TabFMs with CoxPH and DeepHit, revises context-resampled training procedure. Evaluates three approaches: temporal zero-shot reformulation, classification-based fine-tuning, and survival-head adaptation using frozen TabFM backbones on 74 single-risk and 4 competing-risk datasets.

Result: Zero-shot inference works well on smaller single-risk datasets; supervised adaptation becomes better as datasets scale. CoxPH provides strongest interface especially for IBS on larger datasets. DeepHit stronger for time-dependent Concordance Index. Classification fine-tuning competitive with zero-shot as datasets grow but weaker for probabilistic prediction.

Conclusion: Effective TabFM transfer to survival analysis depends on data regime and statistical structure of adaptation interface. Implementation provides practical guidance for applying foundation models to survival prediction tasks.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TabFMs) achieve strong performance on structured data, particularly for standard classification and regression problems. Yet, extending them to censored time-to-event prediction is challenging because it requires properly handling censoring and event-time dynamics. Building on our prior work, we further link TabFMs with CoxPH and DeepHit and revise the context-resampled training procedure. We evaluate temporal zero-shot reformulation, classification-based fine-tuning, and survival-head adaptation using frozen TabFM backbones on 74 single-risk data sets, and we additionally study 4 competing-risk data sets. Zero-shot inference is effective on smaller single-risk data sets, whereas supervised adaptation becomes increasingly advantageous as data sets scale. Cox provides the most reliably strong interface, especially for Integrated Brier Score (IBS) on larger data sets. DeepHit is relatively stronger for the time-dependent Concordance Index than for IBS, while cause-specific MTLR ranks highest among the TabFM survival heads in the four-data-set competing-risk analysis. Classification fine-tuning becomes more competitive with zero-shot inference as data sets grow but remains weaker for probabilistic prediction. Overall, our results indicate that effective TabFM transfer depends on the data regime and on the statistical structure represented by the chosen adaptation interface. The implementation scripts used for this work are available at https://github.com/kaylode/survival-fm.

</details>


### [157] [Fast Gauss Sums via Flash Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04910)
*Nicolaj Rux,Sebastian Neumayer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Flash attention can compute Gaussian kernel sums via small input augmentations, achieving better speed, memory efficiency, and accuracy than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gaussian kernel sums are fundamental to many kernel methods (MMDs, kernel flows, SVGD), but softmax attention has received extensive hardware-aware optimization through flash attention. The paper aims to leverage this highly optimized infrastructure for Gaussian kernel computations.

Method: Show that Gaussian kernel sums with arbitrary signed weights can be evaluated using flash attention by making two small input augmentations. This transforms the normalized softmax reduction into an unnormalized Gauss sum without writing custom GPU code.

Result: For feature dimension D>8 in fp16, this approach beats compiled PyTorch code and PyKeOps kernels (often significantly) in speed, memory-overhead, and accuracy. Most importantly, it maintains linear memory scaling.

Conclusion: Flash attention provides an efficient, hardware-optimized solution for Gaussian kernel sums, enabling better performance than existing specialized implementations while maintaining favorable computational properties like linear memory scaling.

Abstract: Gaussian kernel sums are the computational core of maximum mean discrepancies (MMDs), kernel gradient flows, Stein variational gradient descent (SVGD), and many other kernel methods. At the same time, softmax attention has received an extraordinary amount of hardware-aware code engineering, culminating in flash attention. We show that Gauss kernel sums with arbitrary, signed weights can be evaluated via flash attention: two small input augmentations turn the normalized softmax reduction into the unnormalized Gauss sum, without writing a single line of custom GPU code. For feature dimension D>8 in fp16, this approach beats compiled PyTorch code as well as PyKeOps kernels (often significantly) in speed, memory-overhead and accuracy. Indeed, its memory scaling remains linear.

</details>


### [158] [Physics-Aware Random Walk Fingerprints for Scalable Power Grid Graph Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04943)
*Adnan Anwar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MC-PA-RWF introduces physical edge states into random walk fingerprints for power-grid cascading-failure classification, achieving competitive accuracy with GNNs while being more interpretable and lightweight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current graph neural networks (GNNs) for power-grid cascading-failure classification require end-to-end training and model-specific tuning, and their representations are difficult to relate to physically meaningful propagation patterns. Existing Random Walk Fingerprints (RWF) variants emphasize topology and node-level information but neglect grid-relevant operational edge states.

Method: Multi-Channel Physics-Aware Random Walk Fingerprints (MC-PA-RWF) introduces physical edge states into random-walk propagation through three steps: 1) constructs multiple edge-weighted channels from domain-relevant attributes, 2) extracts a channel-specific fingerprint from each weighted graph, and 3) concatenates the resulting vectors into a compact graph-level representation. The node-edge extension MC-PA-RWF+ adds node-level information.

Result: On three PowerGraph benchmark systems, MC-PA-RWF shows substantial improvements over topology-only RWF and competitive balanced accuracy against strong GNN baselines (GCN, GAT, GINE, TransformerConv). At largest settings, MC-PA-RWF+ achieves 98.04%-99.32% balanced accuracy and improves failure-class F1 over the strongest GNN baseline by 1.60-5.84 percentage points, with statistically significant gains across all three systems.

Conclusion: MC-PA-RWF provides a lightweight, scalable, and interpretable alternative to GNNs for power-grid cascading-failure classification, incorporating physical edge states into random walk fingerprints while achieving competitive or superior performance to state-of-the-art GNN baselines.

Abstract: Recent benchmarks such as PowerGraph provide large collections of power-grid graphs for cascading-failure classification. Graph neural networks (GNNs) achieve strong predictive performance on this task, but typically require end-to-end training and model-specific tuning, while their latent representations can be difficult to relate to physically meaningful propagation patterns. Random Walk Fingerprints (RWF) offer a scalable and interpretable alternative, but existing variants primarily emphasise topology and node-level information, leaving grid-relevant operational edge states in the walk dynamics. We propose Multi-Channel Physics-Aware Random Walk Fingerprints (MC-PA-RWF) for power systems, a lightweight graph-level representation framework that introduces physical edge states into random-walk propagation. The method constructs multiple edge-weighted channels from domain-relevant attributes, extracts a channel-specific fingerprint from each weighted graph, and concatenates the resulting vectors into a compact representation. Experiments on three \textit{PowerGraph} benchmark systems show substantial improvements over topology-only RWF and competitive balanced accuracy against strong GNN baselines, including Graph Convolutional Networks (GCN), Graph Attention Networks (GAT), Graph Isomorphism Networks with edge features (GINE), and Transformer-based Graph Convolutional Networks (TransformerConv). At the largest evaluated settings, the node-edge extension MC-PA-RWF+ achieves around 98.04% - 99.32% balanced accuracy and improves failure-class F1 over the strongest GNN baseline by 1.60 -- 5.84 percentage points, with statistically significant gains across all three systems.

</details>


### [159] [Fractal basins trap latent reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04963)
*Jeffrey Lai,Anthony Bao,John Quinn,William Gilpin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper reveals that reasoning slowdowns in AI models are caused by transient chaos emerging from computational complexity, with models showing fractal basins and getting trapped near saddle points corresponding to nearly-correct solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning models exhibit longer reasoning times on harder tasks, but the underlying mechanism for these slowdowns is unknown. The paper aims to uncover the physical/dynamical system principles behind why AI reasoning slows down with increasing task difficulty.

Method: Analyzed diverse leading reasoning models as dynamical systems, examining their behavior across multiple tasks including Sudoku, maze solving, visual puzzles, and mathematical logic. Studied how fractality of basins changes with task difficulty and identified transient chaos emerging from reasoning getting trapped near saddle points.

Result: Found that reasoning models exhibit transient chaos, with fractal basins whose fractality increases with task difficulty. Showed that slowdowns occur because reasoning gets trapped near saddle points, which correspond to nearly-correct attempted solutions. Demonstrated this pattern across diverse tasks and models.

Conclusion: Reasoning slowdowns are an inevitable consequence of problem hardness in AI models, emerging from transient chaos in dynamical systems. The study establishes reasoning traces as a new class of dynamical system and provides a physical explanation for observed reasoning behavior patterns.

Abstract: Reasoning allows artificial intelligence models to revisit and correct their mistakes, enabling recent frontier advances in mathematical theorem solving, software engineering, and autonomous task planning. Reasoning models are widely observed to reason for longer on harder tasks, but the general mechanism responsible for these slowdowns is unknown. Here, we show that reasoning models exhibit transient chaos, a physical consequence of the computational complexity of difficult tasks. As a consequence, we show that diverse leading reasoning models are dynamical systems with fractal basins, with fractality increasing with task difficulty across diverse tasks like Sudoku and maze solving, visual puzzles, and mathematical logic. We show that transient chaos emerges due to reasoning becoming trapped for extended durations near saddle points, which we show correspond to nearly-correct attempted solutions of the underlying problem. Our results show that reasoning slowdowns are an inevitable consequence of problem hardness in modern artificial intelligence models, and establish reasoning traces as a rich new class of dynamical system.

</details>


### [160] [BeaconKV: Key-Value Cache Compression Guided by Beacon Queries for Efficient Large Reasoning Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04971)
*Janghyeon Kim,Minsoo Kim,Kyuhong Shim,Jungwook Choi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BeaconKV: A training-free KV cache compression method for Large Reasoning Models that maintains beacon queries to anticipate revisit patterns, achieving 5.8× memory reduction while preserving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Reasoning Models suffer from severe memory bottlenecks due to linearly growing KV caches during extended Chain-of-Thought reasoning. Existing compression methods fail for long-horizon reasoning because they incorrectly assume recent queries reliably predict future attention patterns, but Thought Revisiting Tokens require access to distant context.

Method: Systematic analysis reveals queries for Thought Revisiting Tokens cluster into similarity groups. BeaconKV maintains compact beacon queries as representatives for each global query cluster to anticipate which KV pairs will be revisited, eliminating the need to store entire query history.

Result: Across four open-source LRMs and diverse reasoning benchmarks, BeaconKV generally outperforms existing compression methods, achieving up to 5.8× memory reduction while nearly preserving full cache accuracy and improving throughput by over 4.3×.

Conclusion: BeaconKV provides an effective training-free solution to the KV cache memory bottleneck in long-horizon reasoning by leveraging the clustering property of Thought Revisiting Tokens, enabling efficient deployment of Large Reasoning Models without sacrificing reasoning quality.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) achieve superior problem-solving through extended Chain-of-Thought (CoT) generation, but the resulting key-value (KV) cache grows linearly with sequence length and creates severe memory bottlenecks, often exceeding GPU capacity for long reasoning traces. Existing KV cache compression methods rely on recent queries to estimate future token importance, implicitly assuming these serve as reliable proxies for future attention patterns. We demonstrate that this assumption fails in long-horizon reasoning: certain decoding steps generate Thought Revisiting Tokens (TRT) that re-attend to distant previous context, such as task-solving plans formulated early in the trace. Through systematic analysis, we discover that queries corresponding to the TRT cluster into a small number of similarity groups in the embedding space. Based on this insight, we propose BeaconKV, a training-free KV cache compression method that maintains beacon queries, compact representatives for each global query cluster, to anticipate which KV pairs will be revisited without storing the entire query history. Across four open-source LRMs and diverse reasoning benchmarks, BeaconKV generally outperforms existing compression methods, achieving up to $5.8\times$ memory reduction while nearly preserving full cache accuracy and improving throughput by over $4.3\times$.

</details>


### [161] [Beyond Homoscedasticity: Decoupled Uncertainty Optimization for Deep Imbalanced Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04995)
*Juncheng Zhou,Jiaxi Lu,Weijing Zeng,Zhong Li,Hao Qi,Jingsong Cui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DUO is a deep imbalanced regression framework that addresses heteroscedastic uncertainty in long-tailed continuous data through decoupled mean-variance optimization and distribution-guided contrastive learning, outperforming existing methods on DIR benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep Imbalanced Regression (DIR) is common in continuous prediction tasks where tail samples (label-scarce) have high practical value. Existing methods use deterministic point mappings assuming uniform uncertainty, overlooking instance-wise heteroscedasticity in long-tailed data. Even heteroscedastic NLL suffers from gradient coupling that weakens learning signal for hard tail samples, causing optimization inertia and underfitting.

Method: Proposes DUO framework: 1) Models regression target as conditional Gaussian distribution to characterize instance-level predictive uncertainty, 2) Transforms uncertainty into dynamic enhancement signal for tail samples through decoupled mean-variance optimization, 3) Designs distribution-guided contrastive learning that adaptively constructs positive/negative pairs based on overlap between sample distributions to alleviate feature looseness and semantic entanglement.

Result: DUO achieves best few-shot bMAE and GM on IMDB-WIKI-DIR, AgeDB-DIR, and AAV2-DIR benchmarks while remaining competitive on few-shot MAE across visual and biological DIR tasks.

Conclusion: The DUO framework effectively addresses uncertainty-aware learning in deep imbalanced regression by decoupling mean-variance optimization and incorporating distribution-aware contrastive learning, demonstrating superior performance on multiple DIR benchmarks and providing a robust solution for handling heteroscedastic uncertainty in long-tailed continuous data.

Abstract: Deep Imbalanced Regression (DIR) is pervasive in continuous prediction tasks across diverse modalities, such as age estimation, depth prediction, and protein mutation activity prediction, where label-scarce tail samples often carry higher practical value. However, most existing methods still learn deterministic point mappings under mean squared error or its simple variants, implicitly assuming a uniform uncertainty level across all samples and thereby overlooking the instance-wise heteroscedasticity that is widespread in long-tailed data. We further point out that even heteroscedastic negative log-likelihood suffers from a gradient coupling issue, which, under DIR scenarios, weakens the learning signal of hard tail samples and leads to optimization inertia as well as tail underfitting. To address this, we propose DUO, an uncertainty-aware long-tailed regression framework. Specifically, the proposed method models the regression target as a conditional Gaussian distribution to explicitly characterize instance-level predictive uncertainty, and transforms uncertainty into a dynamic enhancement signal for tail samples through decoupled mean-variance optimization. Furthermore, we design a distribution-guided contrastive learning mechanism that adaptively constructs positive and negative pairs based on the overlap between sample distributions, thereby alleviating feature looseness and cross-label semantic entanglement. Across visual and biological DIR benchmarks, DUO achieves the best few-shot bMAE and GM on IMDB-WIKI-DIR, AgeDB-DIR, and AAV2-DIR while remaining competitive on few-shot MAE.

</details>


### [162] [Solution-space heterogeneity shapes federated learning dynamics across partial differential equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05012)
*Ping Luo,Jiahuan Wang,Ziqing Wen,Tao Sun,Dongsheng Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new federated learning protocol for PDEs that quantifies non-IID data separation through optimal transport over solution-space bins, showing how heterogeneity affects training dynamics and task-dependent generalization outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current federated learning approaches for PDEs lack a transferable, consistent way to define and quantify non-IID data distributions across institutions. Existing methods use equation-specific partitioning rules that aren't generalizable.

Method: Proposed solution-space PDE-Dirichlet protocol that converts continuous PDE solutions into reusable bins, quantifies client separation via optimal transport over bin geometry, and relates heterogeneity to Dirichlet concentration parameter.

Result: Lower Dirichlet concentration increases solution distance and optimization heterogeneity consistently across 7 PDE tasks, 3 neural operator families, and 5 random seeds. Final error degradation is task-dependent, with largest effect (4.157 percentage points) for low-viscosity Burgers.

Conclusion: Establishes a reproducible geometric mechanism for quantifying non-IID federated PDE learning that separates geometric effects from task-dependent generalization outcomes, providing a common evaluation framework.

Abstract: Federated scientific machine learning enables institutions to train neural surrogates without centralizing local physical data, yet studies of partial differential equations (PDEs) lack a transferable definition of non-independent and identically distributed data. Existing protocols partition coordinates, coefficients, boundary conditions, or geometries according to equation-specific rules. Here, we introduce solution-space PDE-Dirichlet, a protocol that converts continuous supervised responses into reusable solution bins and quantifies the realized separation between clients through optimal transport over the geometry of these bins. We derive an exact inverse relation between population allocation heterogeneity and the Dirichlet concentration, and we establish conditions under which response heterogeneity induces gradient disagreement, local-update dispersion, and parameter divergence. Across seven controlled and public PDE tasks, three neural-operator families, and five random seeds, a lower concentration consistently increases the realized solution distance and optimization heterogeneity. The degradation in final error is task dependent: the largest effect occurs for low-viscosity Burgers, reaching 4.157 percentage points under the most heterogeneous setting, whereas additional communication or smoother dynamics can reduce the final gap despite persistent parameter separation. These results distinguish a reproducible geometric mechanism from task-dependent generalization outcomes and provide a common basis for evaluating non-IID federated PDE learning.

</details>


### [163] [Amortizing Scaling Law Construction Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05016)
*Abhash Kumar Jha,Diana Alexandra Onuţu,Neeratyoy Mallik,Swagatam Haldar,Sam Laing,Niccolò Ajroldi,Shiwei Liu,Joaquin Vanschoren,Aaron Klein*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Bayesian optimization framework for efficient scaling law construction that reduces computational costs 10-100x by focusing on optimal configurations and using surrogate models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional scaling law derivation requires exhaustive grid training over hyperparameters, token budgets, and parameter counts, which is computationally expensive. Most trained configurations are discarded when only the best-loss frontier is needed for fitting scaling laws.

Method: Proposes a framework using Bayesian optimization for data collection, progressive compute budget expansion during acquisition, and augmentation of observed configurations with surrogate-fantasized evaluations to recover the broader experimental grid.

Result: The method achieves computational savings of 10-100× compared to full dense grid training, while closely matching scaling law fits. Progressive compute budget expansion improves recovery efficiency, and surrogate evaluations enable accurate scaling law fitting without training every configuration.

Conclusion: Scaling laws can be efficiently constructed without exhaustive grid training by using Bayesian optimization with progressive compute budget expansion and surrogate-fantasized evaluations, achieving substantial computational savings while maintaining accuracy.

Abstract: Scaling laws guide the design choices for training large foundation models, but deriving them involves training an exhaustive grid over hyperparameters, token budgets, and parameter counts, which is computationally expensive. Fitting a scaling law, however, only requires the best-loss frontier across compute scales, discarding most of the trained configurations. We propose a framework for efficient scaling law construction that formulates data collection as a Bayesian optimization problem, and introduce metrics for comparing scaling law fitting methods under constrained compute budgets. We find that progressively expanding the compute budget during acquisition, mirroring the compute-ordered evaluation of configurations in practice, substantially improves recovery efficiency. Augmenting the observed configurations with surrogate-fantasized evaluations then recovers the broader experimental grid, allowing accurate scaling law fitting without training every configuration. Together, these can closely match scaling law fits over a full dense grid at computational savings of up to $10\text{--}100\times$.

</details>


### [164] [Confounding-Valid Conformal Inference for Counterfactual KPIs in Wireless Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05073)
*Abdessamed Qchohi,Jessica Moysen Cortes,Matteo Zecchin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CV-CCI combines observational and randomized telemetry data to provide reliable counterfactual predictions for network KPIs while maintaining statistical guarantees under hidden confounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Network operators need to answer 'what-if' questions using logged telemetry data, but hidden confounding (when logged data omits controller variables) invalidates statistical guarantees. Randomized telemetry solves this but is scarce and disruptive to normal operation, leading to uninformative predictions.

Method: Proposes Confounding-Valid Counterfactual Conformal Inference (CV-CCI) that combines abundant observational telemetry (potentially confounded) with limited randomized data using the General Synthetic-Powered Inference (GESPI) principle. This leverages observational data for efficiency while using randomized data for coverage guarantees.

Result: Experiments on two radio access network control tasks show CV-CCI maintains validity under hidden confounding while producing more efficient prediction sets than state-of-the-art confounding-valid baselines.

Conclusion: CV-CCI successfully addresses the trade-off between data availability and statistical validity in counterfactual inference for network operations, providing reliable 'what-if' answers even with hidden confounding.

Abstract: Conformal counterfactual inference enables network operators to use logged telemetry to reliably answer 'what-if' questions about network operation. These answers typically take the form of prediction sets that contain, with a user-defined probability, the key performance indicators (KPIs) that would have been observed under alternative control actions. A key challenge is that logged telemetry may omit variables used by the controller, resulting in hidden confounding and invalidating the statistical guarantees of counterfactual analysis. In principle, this issue can be addressed using randomized telemetry, collected by assigning control actions independently of the network state. However, because such randomization may disrupt normal operation, randomized telemetry is typically scarce, causing counterfactual analysis based solely on it to produce uninformative prediction sets. To address these challenges, we propose Confounding-Valid Counterfactual Conformal Inference (CV-CCI), which combines abundant, potentially confounded observational telemetry with limited randomized data through the General Synthetic-Powered Inference (GESPI) principle. CV-CCI leverages observational data to improve efficiency while using randomized data to retain finite-sample coverage guarantees under arbitrary hidden confounding. Experiments on two representative radio access network (RAN) control tasks show that CV-CCI remains valid under hidden confounding while producing more efficient prediction sets than state-of-the-art confounding-valid baselines.

</details>


### [165] [Deep Microcompression: Structured Pruning and Bit-packed Quantization for Microcontrollers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05081)
*Opegbemi Matthias Busoye,Tolulope Matthew Busoye,Eghonghon-aye Eigbe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep Microcompression (DMC) is a hardware-aware pipeline that achieves extreme compression (55.8×) for deep learning models on microcontrollers, enabling CNN deployment on severely constrained devices like the ATmega328P with only 2KB SRAM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need to deploy deep learning models on extremely resource-constrained microcontrollers with limited memory and computational capabilities, where traditional frameworks like TensorFlow Lite are too heavy. Specifically, enabling CNN inference on devices like the ATmega328P (2KB SRAM) was previously considered infeasible.

Method: DMC combines structured pruning, quantization-aware training, and fixed-length bit-packing in an integrated pipeline to achieve extreme weight compression. The approach generates dependency-free C libraries with deterministic latency specifically designed for bare-metal microcontrollers.

Result: Achieves 55.8× weight compression ratio on LeNet-5 while maintaining 98.77% accuracy. On RP2040 (Cortex-M0+), reduces binary size by 3× compared to TensorFlow Lite with comparable accuracy. Most notably, enables the first documented deployment of a standard CNN on ATmega328P (2KB SRAM device).

Conclusion: DMC successfully bridges the gap between deep learning and ultra-constrained microcontroller environments, making CNN inference feasible on devices previously considered too resource-limited for such applications, opening new possibilities for edge AI deployment.

Abstract: This paper introduces Deep Microcompression (DMC), a hardware-aware pipeline for deep learning inference on bare-metal microcontrollers. DMC integrates structured pruning, quantization-aware training, and fixed-length bit-packing to achieve a 55.8$\times$ weight compression ratio on LeNet-5 (98.77\% accuracy), generating a dependency-free C library with deterministic latency. On the RP2040 (Cortex-M0+), DMC reduces binary size by 3$\times$ versus TensorFlow Lite while matching its accuracy. Critically, DMC enables the first documented deployment of a standard CNN on the ATmega328P, a device constrained to 2KB SRAM, previously considered infeasible for CNN inference.

</details>


### [166] [NEAT-POCKET: Pocket-Conditioned Autoregressive 3D Molecular Generation with a Neighborhood-Guided Set Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05097)
*Roxane Axel Jacob,Daniel Rose,Thierry Langer,Johannes Kirchmair*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NEAT-POCKET is a pocket-conditioned autoregressive model for 3D molecular generation that creates novel ligands directly within target protein binding pockets atom by atom, achieving competitive performance with faster sampling than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-driven de novo molecular design can accelerate early-stage drug discovery by generating novel ligands directly in target protein binding pockets, addressing the need for more efficient structure-based drug design approaches.

Method: NEAT-POCKET extends the autoregressive NEAT model for 3D molecular generation by conditioning on protein pocket environments, generating molecules atom by atom while preserving atom permutation invariance and explicitly modeling hydrogen atoms.

Result: Benchmarks on CrossDocked and SPINDR datasets show NEAT-POCKET achieves competitive structure-based generation performance while sampling substantially faster than existing baselines, and naturally enables pocket-conditioned fragment completion tasks.

Conclusion: NEAT-POCKET positions itself as a fast, flexible, and practical framework for structure-based drug design with applications in both full-molecule generation and lead optimization through fragment completion.

Abstract: AI-driven de novo molecular design offers a promising route to accelerate early-stage drug discovery by generating novel ligands directly within target protein binding pockets. We present NEAT-POCKET, a pocket-conditioned extension of the autoregressive NEAT model for 3D molecular generation. NEAT-POCKET generates molecules atom by atom in protein pocket environments while preserving atom permutation invariance and explicitly modeling hydrogen atoms. Benchmarks on the CrossDocked and SPINDR datasets show that NEAT-POCKET achieves competitive structure-based generation performance while sampling substantially faster than existing baselines. Beyond full-molecule generation, NEAT-POCKET naturally enables pocket-conditioned fragment completion, a task directly relevant to lead optimization and scaffold elaboration. These results position NEAT-POCKET as a fast, flexible, and practical framework for structure-based drug design.

</details>


### [167] [A Comparative Study of Counterfactual Explainers for Graph Neural Networks Enabling Multiple Types of Graph Edit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05113)
*Maria Myrto Villia,Filippos Gouidis,Theodore Patkos,Panos Trahanias*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comprehensive comparative study of six state-of-the-art counterfactual explanation methods for graph-structured data across diverse datasets and tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a lack of general and efficient counterfactual explanation methods for graph data that can modify both edges and nodes. Existing methods trade off between explanation size, coverage, and quality, making it important to systematically compare their strengths and weaknesses to guide future research.

Method: Comparative analysis of six SOTA counterfactual explanation models on diverse real-world and synthetic datasets covering both binary and multi-class graph and node classification tasks, using various quantitative and qualitative evaluation metrics.

Result: The study provides comprehensive comparison results identifying where each method performs well and where they fall short, revealing trade-offs between explanation size, coverage, and quality across different approaches.

Conclusion: Systematic comparison of counterfactual explainers for graphs reveals their relative strengths and weaknesses, providing valuable guidance for future research in developing better methods that balance explanation quality, size, and coverage.

Abstract: Counterfactual explanations for graph-structured data seek to determine minimal and realistic modifications required in an input graph to alter a model's prediction to a predefined output. Although counterfactual explainers that support modifying the graph by both adding and removing edges have recently emerged, there is still a lack of general and efficient methods, especially when considering the quality of the generated explanations. Moreover, the problem remains far from solved, as existing methods exhibit different strengths and weaknesses, often trading off between explanation size, coverage and quality. For this reason, it is important to identify where each method performs well and where it falls short, so as to guide future research in the field. Thus, our study compares six state-of-the-art (SOTA) models on a diverse set of real-world and synthetic datasets, covering both binary and multi-class graph and node classification tasks, and evaluates their performance using diverse quantitative and qualitative metrics.

</details>


### [168] [Single-Query Black-Box Calibration Auditing via Logit Bias](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05125)
*Roman Plaud,Antoine Saillenfest,Matthieu Labeau,Thomas Bonald,Willem Waegeman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers develop a method to evaluate LLM calibration using only logit_bias API calls, enabling calibration assessment of black-box commercial models with one query per sample.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Commercial LLM APIs are increasingly hiding continuous output probabilities needed for standard calibration metrics, making it difficult to evaluate model reliability for safe deployment as zero-shot classifiers.

Method: The paper demonstrates that any LLM API exposing a logit_bias parameter can be mathematically manipulated to evaluate exact probability thresholds using just one query per sample. They leverage this to create a provably consistent estimator of True Calibration Error.

Result: The approach provides an efficient framework for auditing black-box foundation models without needing access to internal probability distributions, bypassing API opacity limitations.

Conclusion: This novel method enables calibration assessment of commercial LLMs through API manipulation, offering a practical solution for safe deployment evaluation despite restricted access to model internals.

Abstract: Evaluating the calibration of Large Language Models (LLMs) is critical for their safe deployment as zero-shot classifiers. Yet, commercial API providers increasingly hide the continuous output probabilities required by standard calibration metrics. To bypass this opacity, we demonstrate that any LLM API exposing a logit\_bias parameter can be mathematically manipulated to evaluate exact probability thresholds using strictly one query per sample. Leveraging this mechanism, we introduce a novel and provably consistent estimator of the True Calibration Error for binary tasks. Our approach therefore provides an efficient framework for auditing black-box foundation models.

</details>


### [169] [Coarse-Graining Hidden Representations: Unsupervised Neuron Selection via Mapping Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05126)
*Margherita Mele,Andrea Castagna,Roberto Menichetti,Raffaello Potestio,Alessandro Ingrosso*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes using mapping entropy (ME) as an unsupervised method to select essential neurons in overparameterized neural networks by identifying subsets that retain maximal discriminatory power based only on activation statistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overparameterized neural networks contain many more neurons than tasks require, making it unclear which neurons are truly essential. There's a need for methods to identify important neurons without relying on labels or gradients.

Method: The authors frame neuron selection as coarse-graining the hidden layer, scoring potential neuron subsets using mapping entropy (ME) - a measure of discriminatory power loss when discarding neurons. ME optimization identifies the most informative subset based solely on hidden activation statistics.

Result: In teacher-student networks, ME optimization recovers minimal teacher-consistent representations. On non-linear Gaussian process tasks and translation-augmented MNIST, ME-selected subnetworks outperform random subsets, especially under strong compression, linking configurational distinguishability to predictive performance.

Conclusion: Mapping entropy provides an effective unsupervised criterion for neuron selection in overparameterized networks, identifying essential neurons based purely on activation statistics and improving performance in compressed subnetworks.

Abstract: Overparameterized neural networks carry far more hidden units than a task nominally requires, raising the question of which neurons are essential and whether that distinction is legible in the representation itself, without labels or gradients. We cast neuron selection as the problem of coarse-graining the hidden layer by retaining a subset of its neurons, and score each putative selection by the mapping entropy (ME). This quantity measures the loss of discriminatory power inherent in discarding part of the network neurons, and the selection that minimises the ME is taken as particularly informative. This criterion is fully unsupervised, in that it depends only on hidden-activation statistics. In teacher-student networks, ME optimisation recovers the minimal teacher-consistent representation and retains extra units in proportion to the hidden layer's residual variability; in a non-linear Gaussian process task, it selects coherent functional-class mappings whose preferred class shifts across training. On this task and on translation-augmented MNIST, ME-selected subnetworks outperform random subsets of equal size, most clearly under strong compression - linking configurational distinguishability to predictive performance.

</details>


### [170] [MomentQuant: an even more minimalist interval method with linear time complexity for time series classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05136)
*Johann Faouzi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two optimizations for the Quant time series classification algorithm: better implementation and approximate quantiles via Cornish-Fisher expansion, creating faster MomentQuant algorithm with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series classification is important but faces trade-off between predictive performance and computational cost. Quant algorithm achieves good accuracy but can be optimized for even faster inference, which is crucial for real-world applications where inference is performed much more frequently than training.

Method: Two approaches: 1) Better optimized implementation of the existing Quant algorithm; 2) Replacing exact quantiles with approximate quantiles using Cornish-Fisher expansion (creating MomentQuant), which eliminates the need to sort time series and reduces computational complexity.

Result: The new implementation of Quant is faster than original Quant, and MomentQuant is even faster than the optimized Quant implementation, with only a tiny decrease in predictive performance. The improvements are particularly valuable for real-life applications with frequent inference needs.

Conclusion: MomentQuant offers significant speed improvements over Quant with minimal accuracy trade-off, making it especially suitable for practical applications where computational efficiency during inference is critical. The Cornish-Fisher expansion provides an effective way to approximate quantiles without sorting operations.

Abstract: Time series data is very common in many real-world applications and in numerous domains, with increasing interest for automated information extraction using machine learning. One of these subfields is time series classification, which consists in assigning a label to each new, unseen time series. Many algorithms have been developed over the past decades, with the trade-off between predictive performance and computational cost being consistently discussed. Quant, an interval-based algorithm extracting quantiles from recursive, fixed, dyadic intervals, was shown to achieve high accuracy, while being very fast. We propose two changes to make this algorithm even faster. The first one is a better optimized implementation of the exact same algorithm. The second one is to derive approximate quantiles, using the Cornish-Fisher expansion, instead of exact quantiles. This change removes the necessity to sort the time series, leading to a smaller computational complexity. We call this novel algorithm MomentQuant. We provide evidence that our implementation of Quant is faster than the original one, and that MomentQuant is even faster than our implementation of Quant, at the cost of a tiny decrease in predictive performance. These improvements are especially relevant for real-life applications, where inference is performed much more often than training.

</details>


### [171] [From 80x to 385x: A Best-Matching-Unit Search at the L2 Roof, Measured Against a Symmetrically Tuned Baseline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05138)
*Andrew James Amos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A study showing that fair GPU algorithm comparisons require tuning both sides equally, demonstrating dramatic performance improvements (up to 385x) when both novel SparseBin SOM algorithm and baseline cuSPARSE are properly optimized.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GPU implementation comparisons are typically asymmetric - one algorithm gets tuned by its author while the other runs as-is. This creates unfair performance evaluations that don't reflect true algorithmic advantages.

Method: Systematically tuned both a novel sparse binary self-organizing map algorithm (SparseBin) and the baseline cuSPARSE algorithm using four optimization levers: tile size, tile-membership clustering, neuron-axis chunking, and vectorized loads.

Result: SparseBin achieved 5.6-10.1x speedup per epoch over previously published configurations across map sizes (32x32 to 512x512). When both algorithms were tuned, SparseBin's advantage over the baseline increased from ~80x to ~385x. cuSPARSE also improved 2-3x with tuning. The optimized kernel reached 77% of L2 bandwidth peak, indicating near-optimal performance.

Conclusion: Fair GPU algorithm comparisons require equal tuning effort on both sides. The performance ceiling was reached at 77% of theoretical bandwidth, establishing these results as terminal rather than intermediate improvements. The study provides a methodology for meaningful GPU algorithm evaluation.

Abstract: Comparisons between GPU implementations are usually asymmetric: one side is tuned by its author, the other is run as found. I report a programme that tuned both a novel SOM algorithm (SparseBin) and the baseline algorithm it was being compared to (cuSPARSE). The best-matching-unit search that dominates self-organizing map training was tuned through four levers - tile size, tile-membership clustering, neuron-axis chunking and vectorised loads - reaching 5.6-10.1x per epoch over the previously published configuration at map sizes from 32x32 to 512x512, and lifting the margin over the CUDA implementation behind our earlier MEDLINE atlases from ~80x to ~385x. cuSPARSE, the implementation SparseBin is compared against, received every lever with an analogue on its side, and became 2-3x faster in the process. The tuned kernel pressed the L2 bandwidth roof at 77% of peak with every other unit at 40-65%, bounding any further lever at ~1.3x - a terminal result rather than a waypoint, and every untested lever was either capped by that bound by construction or measured null.

</details>


### [172] [Beyond Stationarity in Time Series: Discovering Causal Structures and Latent Regimes via Markov Blankets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05150)
*Lei Zan,Charles K. Assaad,Emilie Devijver,Eric Gaussier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel causal discovery algorithm for time series that identifies latent causal regimes and their associated causal structures, relaxing the assumption of static causal relationships and addressing non-stationarity in dynamic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series often exhibit regime changes that challenge the assumption of a static causal structure, which is a limitation in real-world dynamic systems where causal relationships may change over different time periods or operating conditions.

Method: RCBNB-MB (Regime-aware Constraint-Based and Noise-Based causal discovery with Markov Blankets) uses an iterative strategy to segment time series into latent causal regimes and discover causal graphs within each regime by leveraging Markov blankets for robustness against discovery errors.

Result: The algorithm systematically outperforms baseline approaches in accurately detecting regime changes and their associated causal graphs, validated through extensive experiments on simulated datasets with known ground truth and real-world IT monitoring data.

Conclusion: RCBNB-MB provides a robust and versatile framework for non-stationary time series analysis with theoretical guarantees for recovering both regime transitions and causal graphs under reasonable assumptions.

Abstract: This paper introduces Regime-aware Constraint-Based and Noise-Based causal discovery with Markov Blankets (RCBNB-MB), a novel causal discovery algorithm for time series that relaxes the common assumption of a single, time-consistent causal structure. Time series are typically observed at discrete time points and often exhibit regime changes that challenge the assumption of a static causal structure, a limitation in many real-world dynamic systems. To address this challenge, RCBNB-MB identifies latent causal regimes, defined as subsets of time points within which a stable causal structure holds. The algorithm follows an iterative strategy that segments the time series into regimes and discovers the causal graph within each regime. By leveraging the Markov blanket rather than direct parents, RCBNB-MB gains robustness to errors in causal discovery and preserves predictive information. We provide theoretical guarantees for RCBNB-MB's ability to recover both regime transitions and causal graphs under reasonable assumptions. Furthermore, we validate its effectiveness through extensive experiments on simulated datasets with known ground truth and real-world IT monitoring data, where taking into account regime shifts is critical. Empirical results show that RCBNB-MB systematically outperforms baseline approaches in accurately detecting regime changes and their associated causal graphs, positioning it as a robust and versatile framework for non-stationary time series analysis.

</details>


### [173] [Phase Transition Frequency as a Training Time Predictor of Test Accuracy in ResNets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05194)
*Arunan J*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The number of class-separability jumps during ResNet finetuning negatively correlates with final test accuracy on standard i.i.d. benchmarks, but this correlation weakens under distributional stress.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether the number of discrete class-separability jumps observed during neural network finetuning can serve as a predictor of final test accuracy, providing an inexpensive training-quality probe.

Method: Empirical examination across 75 experiments spanning four benchmarks (CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, TinyImageNet, CIFAR-10-C) and three ResNet architectures (18, 50, 101), with 5-10 seeds per configuration. Used correlation analysis and compared against six alternative training-curve signals.

Result: Strong negative correlations on standard i.i.d. benchmarks: r = -0.84 on CIFAR-10 and r = -0.87 on CIFAR-100. Weaker correlations under distributional stress: r = -0.45 on TinyImageNet and r = -0.19 on CIFAR-10-C. Transition count was among strongest predictors on clean datasets but dominated by other signals on stressed benchmarks.

Conclusion: Class-separability jump count serves as an effective in-distribution training-quality probe, with strong negative correlation to final accuracy on standard benchmarks. It provides an inexpensive detection procedure suitable for logging during training, though its predictive power attenuates under distributional stress.

Abstract: The number of discrete class-separability jumps observed during ResNet finetuning is examined empirically as a predictor of final test accuracy. Across 75 experiments spanning four benchmarks (CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, TinyImageNet, and CIFAR-10-C) and three architectures (ResNet-18, ResNet-50, and ResNet-101), with five to ten seeds per configuration, a strong within-dataset negative correlation is obtained on standard i.i.d. classification benchmarks: \(r = -0.84\) on CIFAR-10 (\(p < 10^{-8}\), \(n = 30\)) and \(r = -0.87\) on CIFAR-100 (\(p < 10^{-5}\), \(n = 15\)). Under distributional stress, the relationship attenuates: TinyImageNet yields \(r = -0.45\), and the CIFAR-10-C corruption benchmark yields \(r = -0.19\). Two additional analyses discipline the empirical claim. A partial correlation controlling for architecture depth, treated as a linear covariate, shows that on CIFAR-100 the transition count retains statistically significant predictive power (\(r_{\mathrm{partial}} = -0.69\), \(p = 0.007\)); the corresponding result under the stricter categorical conditioning is not established at \(n = 15\). A comparison against six alternative training-curve signals shows that transition count achieved the strongest correlation among the evaluated signals on CIFAR-100 and one of the strongest on CIFAR-10, but is dominated by other signals on the two stressed benchmarks. The comparison is restricted to training-curve-level signals; comparisons against effective rank, Hessian sharpness, Fisher information, margin, and neural-collapse measures, which are the strongest competitors in the current literature, are not part of the present study and remain open. The observation is presented as an in-distribution training-quality probe among a family of candidate probes, and an inexpensive detection procedure suitable for logging alongside a standard training loop is provided.

</details>


### [174] [Dimension-Adaptive Batched Lipschitz Narrowing Without Knowing the Zooming Dimension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05214)
*Yasong Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ACE sequence in A-BLiN algorithm no longer depends on zooming dimension dz, making it fully adaptive to unknown smoothness parameters while maintaining optimal regret and batch complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original A-BLiN algorithm's ACE sequence depended on the zooming dimension dz, which requires knowledge of this smoothness parameter. This limits practical applicability since dz is typically unknown in real-world problems.

Method: Developed Count-Adaptive BLiN algorithm that selects the next edge length based on the number of cubes that survive preceding elimination steps, rather than using dz or the zooming constant Cz. This creates a parameter-free adaptive method.

Result: Achieves Õ_d(T^{(dz+1)/(dz+2)}) regret with O_d(log log T) batches without requiring knowledge of dz or Cz. Combined with lower bound from original paper, proves optimal batch complexity remains Θ_d(log log T) even when dz is unknown.
