Conclusion: The dependency on zooming dimension dz in ACE sequences can be eliminated, creating a fully adaptive algorithm that maintains optimal regret rates and batch complexity without requiring prior knowledge of smoothness parameters.

Abstract: The Appropriately Combined Edge-length (ACE) sequence in A-BLiN depends on the zooming dimension $d_z$. This note removes that dependence. The next edge length is selected from the number of cubes that survive the preceding elimination. The resulting Count-Adaptive BLiN algorithm does not use $d_z$ or the zooming constant $C_z$, yet it attains $\widetilde{\mathcal O}_d(T^{(d_z+1)/(d_z+2)})$ regret with $\mathcal O_d(\log\log T)$ batches. Together with the adaptive-grid lower bound in Theorem 10 of the original paper, the optimal batch complexity remains $Θ_d(\log\log T)$ when $d_z$ is unknown.

</details>


### [175] [FedDRAW: Federated Dual Reputation Annealing Weighting for Heterogeneous Multi-Institutional Chest Radiograph Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05223)
*Maryam Moradpour,Anne-Christin Hauschild*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedDRAW is a novel federated learning aggregation method that dynamically adjusts client weights based on both data size and model similarity, outperforming traditional methods on medical imaging tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional federated averaging (FedAvg) assigns aggregation weights based solely on local sample counts, which can disadvantage small but informative hospitals and allow large but less informative clients to dominate the global model. This bias is problematic in medical diagnostics where data is distributed across privacy-conscious institutions.

Method: FedDRAW uses a dual annealing schedule: 1) inner schedule shifts client reputation from data-size prior towards cosine similarity between client and global parameters, 2) outer deferred annealing schedule on softmax inverse temperature keeps weighting selective in early/middle rounds and relaxes to uniformity at convergence.

Result: FedDRAW achieved the highest average rank among 8 federated methods across 12 client-partition scenarios on CheXpert and ChestMNIST datasets, with statistically significant improvements in both AUC and geometric mean of sensitivity/specificity (confirmed by Friedman test with Nemenyi post-hoc analysis).

Conclusion: Scheduling two signals (data size and model similarity) rather than fixing weights by sample count alone enables less biased diagnostic models in federated learning settings, making it particularly valuable for medical applications where data distribution and informativeness vary across institutions.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence models are promising for medical diagnosis, but they require large numbers of unbiased data, which in medicine are distributed across hospitals and cannot be centralized to protect patient privacy. Federated Learning (FL) addresses this, since hospitals train one shared diagnostic model while patient data remain local. Training proceeds in communication rounds, in which each hospital trains the shared model locally and returns it to the server for merging by weighted average. This aggregation weight determines whose institutional knowledge shapes the result. Federated averaging (FedAvg) sets it in proportion to local sample count, so a small but informative hospital is permanently assigned a small influence, andl argest clients could dominate the global model even when they are less informative. We propose Federated Dual Reputation Annealing Weighting (FedDRAW), a server-side aggregation method that combines a data-size prior with the cosine similarity between client and global parameters under two coupled annealing schedules. An inner schedule shifts client reputation from the size prior towards similarity. An outer, deferred annealing schedule on the softmax inverse temperature keeps the weighting selective in the early and middle rounds and relaxes it to uniformity at convergence. We evaluate FedDRAW on 12 simulated client-partition scenarios of two chest radiograph datasets (CheXpert and ChestMNIST), against seven federated baselines under identical local training settings. FedDRAW achieved the highest average rank among all eight methods under both AUC and the geometric mean (GM) of sensitivity and specificity, which a Friedman test with Nemenyi post-hoc analysis confirmed to be a statistically significant difference between the methods. Scheduling two signals, rather than fixing the weights by sample count alone, could enable less biased diagnostic models.

</details>


### [176] [Hessian-based molecular conformation augmentation for a scalable and efficient strategy of machine learning interatomic potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05233)
*Bumju Kwak,Jeonghee Jo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes two Hessian-derived data augmentation methods (UniAug and ModeAug) to improve machine-learning interatomic potentials without architectural changes or computational overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MLIPs are trained only on energy and forces, ignoring Hessian information crucial for applications like vibrational analysis and transition state search. Existing Hessian-aware methods require architectural modifications and incur high computational/memory costs from higher-order backpropagation.

Method: Two Hessian-derived data augmentation schemes: 1) Isotropic Gaussian displacement (UniAug) and 2) Normal mode-weighted displacement (ModeAug). Both use simple Taylor expansions to augment training data without modifying training objectives or extending the autograd graph, enabling plug-and-play integration with existing architectures.

Result: Comprehensive evaluations across non-equilibrium and equilibrium datasets show enhanced model accuracy. The methods provide practical, task-specific guidelines for effective augmentation.

Conclusion: The proposed Hessian-derived data augmentation methods successfully improve MLIP performance for Hessian-reliant applications while maintaining compatibility with existing architectures and avoiding computational overhead.

Abstract: While machine-learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) have successfully learned potential energy surfaces (PES) and atomic forces, many practical applications, such as vibrational analysis and transition state search, rely heavily on the PES Hessian. Yet, standard MLIPs tend to be trained on energy and forces alone, leaving Hessian information largely unexploited. Meanwhile, existing methods that explicitly incorporate the Hessian into training objectives require architectural modifications and introduce significant computational and memory overheads due to higher-order backpropagation. To address these limitations, we propose two Hessian-derived data augmentation schemes: isotropic Gaussian displacement (\textbf{UniAug}) and normal mode-weighted displacement (\textbf{ModeAug}). Both methods utilize simple Taylor expansions, achieving effective augmentation without altering training objectives or extending the autograd graph. This allows seamless, plug-and-play integration with existing architectures and training pipelines. Comprehensive evaluations across non-equilibrium and equilibrium datasets demonstrate that our approach enhances model accuracy while providing practical, task-specific guidelines.

</details>


### [177] [PRICE: A Systematic Study of LLM Adaptation Choices for Bitcoin Price Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05235)
*Maryam Fakhari,Mehran Safayani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PRICE adapts LLMs for Bitcoin price forecasting with LoRA, recursive inference, integer rounding, CTF prompting, and zero-temperature decoding, achieving competitive results despite using text-pretrained models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cryptocurrency markets have extreme volatility and non-stationary dynamics that challenge conventional forecasting methods, and while LLMs show promise for time series forecasting, the combined effects of adaptation choices remain unexplored in financial settings.

Method: PRICE uses a 4-bit quantized LLaMA-3 8B model with five key adaptations: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning with LoRA for hardware efficiency, Recursive multi-step inference for accuracy, Integer-rounded numerical representation to reduce errors, Context-Task-Format (CTF) prompting (which outperforms Chain-of-Thought alternatives), and Exact zero-temperature decoding for stability.

Result: PRICE achieves lowest forecasting errors on validation/test sets compared to eight transformer-based and time-series foundation models, maintains robust performance across evaluation periods, and shows competitive/superior performance to specialized foundation models despite using a text-pretrained model.

Conclusion: Adaptation choices critically determine the accuracy and robustness of LLMs for numerical time-series forecasting, demonstrating that properly adapted text-based LLMs can compete with specialized models in financial forecasting tasks.

Abstract: Cryptocurrency markets exhibit extreme volatility and non-stationary dynamics that challenge conventional forecasting methods. Although Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promise for time series forecasting, the combined effects of adaptation choices remain largely unexplored in financial settings. This study introduces PRICE, a structured approach for adapting LLMs to short-term Bitcoin price forecasting. Built on a 4-bit quantized LLaMA-3 8B model, PRICE investigates how fine-tuning, numerical representation, prompting, inference, and decoding jointly influence forecasting performance. PRICE integrates Parameter-efficient fine-tuning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), Recursive multi-step inference, Integer-rounded numerical representation, Context-Task-Format (CTF) prompting, and Exact zero-temperature decoding. Ablation studies show that each component contributes to forecasting accuracy and reliability. LoRA enables efficient training on limited hardware, recursive inference improves accuracy, integer-rounded values reduce errors, CTF prompting outperforms Chain-of-Thought, Implicit Chain-of-Thought (iCoT), and few-shot prompting, and zero-temperature decoding improves stability during recursive forecasting. Comparative evaluation against eight transformer-based and time-series foundation models shows that PRICE achieves the lowest forecasting errors on both validation and test sets while maintaining robust performance across evaluation periods. Despite being based on a model primarily pretrained on text rather than time-series data, PRICE achieves competitive or superior performance relative to specialized foundation models. These findings demonstrate that adaptation choices critically determine the accuracy and robustness of LLMs for numerical time-series forecasting.

</details>


### [178] [GLASS: Graph-Language Alignment with Spherical Scoring for Transferable Graph-Level Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05253)
*Xudong Wang,Chris Ding,Tongxin Li,Jicong Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GLASS is a graph-level anomaly detection framework that achieves cross-domain transferability by aligning graph and language representations on a hypersphere through multi-scale consistency and probabilistic scoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current graph-level anomaly detection methods lack robust cross-domain transferability and struggle with domain adaptation, especially in few-shot or zero-shot scenarios where target domain training data is limited or unavailable.

Method: The method involves: 1) Building a unified representation space by aligning structure-aware graph encoders with instruction-aware text embeddings via multi-slice soft cosine objective; 2) Serializing graph properties into compact Graph Descriptor Prompts (GraphDP); 3) Enforcing multi-scale consistency through Matryoshka representation slices; 4) Formulating anomaly detection as density estimation on the aligned hypersphere using Spherical Multi-Modal Scoring with von Mises-Fisher kernel density estimators.

Result: GLASS achieves state-of-the-art performance across twelve benchmarks and three meta-domains, obtaining the best average AUROC and rank compared to recent advanced GLAD baselines, and enables effective cross-domain transfer in zero-shot and few-shot settings.

Conclusion: The GLASS framework successfully bridges the gap between graph structure and language semantics through hypersphere alignment, enabling robust cross-domain graph anomaly detection without requiring extensive target-domain training data, with applications to zero-shot and few-shot adaptation scenarios.

Abstract: We introduce GLASS, a framework for graph-level anomaly detection (GLAD) that achieves robust cross-domain transferability through graph-language alignment on the unit hypersphere. GLASS builds a unified representation space by aligning a structure-aware graph encoder with an instruction-aware text embedding via a multi-slice soft cosine objective. Our framework serializes local, global, and semantic graph properties into a compact Graph Descriptor Prompt (GraphDP), creating a text bridge that enables domain-agnostic anomaly scoring. By enforcing multi-scale consistency through Matryoshka representation slices, the model captures anomalous deviations at multiple levels of granularity. For scoring, we formulate anomaly detection as density estimation on the aligned hypersphere and introduce Spherical Multi-Modal Scoring (SMS), which instantiates von Mises-Fisher kernel density estimators in both graph and text embedding spaces. This probabilistic formulation recovers angular k-nearest-neighbor scoring as a high-concentration limiting case and provides a principled fusion of structural and semantic anomaly signals. The shared text embedding space further serves as a cross-domain bridge: by encoding a target domain's GraphDP without target-domain training data, GLASS performs zero-shot anomaly detection, and with only a handful of normal examples, few-shot adaptation via reference-set calibration. Across twelve benchmarks and three meta-domains, GLASS obtains the best average AUROC and rank compared with recent advanced GLAD baselines and enables effective cross-domain transfer.

</details>


### [179] [How to Speculate about Uncertainty in Agentic Coding? A Draft-Model Gate Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05274)
*Konstantin Grotov,Valentin Malykh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SU is a method to predict failure in LLM agents by analyzing output tokens with a small draft model, enabling preventive actions before costly execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents in software engineering fail expensively - they act confidently wrong and failures are only recognized after costly execution. There's a need for predictive failure signals to prevent these expensive errors.

Method: SU inverts speculative decoding: a small open-weight draft model scores the agent's already-generated trajectory in a single forward pass. It extracts phase-aware features by separating reasoning and action spans, then calibrates them against verifiable objectives to produce a failure-likelihood score.

Result: SU reduces execution error rate by 6-8 percentage points and token cost by 14-19% in deployment on Qwen3-Coder-480B and Claude 3.5 Sonnet. The method transfers to out-of-distribution benchmarks without retraining and generalizes across agent models.

Conclusion: SU provides an actionable failure-likelihood signal that enables downstream policies (routing, human intervention, extra compute) to prevent costly agent failures before execution, making LLM agent deployment more reliable and cost-effective.

Abstract: LLM agents deployed for software engineering fail expensively: they act confidently wrong, and bad actions are recognized only after costly execution and retry. We present Speculative Uncertainty (SU), a method that recovers a predictive failure signal for a black-box agent from its output tokens alone, with no access to logits, weights, activations, or repeated sampling. Inverting speculative decoding, a small open-weight draft model scores the agent's already-generated trajectory in a single forward pass. From these speculative cross-likelihoods we extract phase-aware features by separating the reasoning and action spans, and calibrate them against a verifiable objective. SU produces a failure-likelihood score that any downstream policy, such as routing, human intervention, or extra test-time compute, can consume directly. To show the signal is actionable, we instantiate one such policy, a pre-execution veto gate, on software engineering agents Qwen3-Coder-480B and closed-source Claude 3.5 Sonnet, cutting execution error rate by 6-8 percentage points and token cost by 14-19% in deployment, transferring to out-of-distribution benchmarks without retraining, and generalizing across agent models.

</details>


### [180] [Learning from VAE Errors to support ECG-based Differential Diagnosis of Myocardial Scar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05294)
*Shayan Sharifi,Riccardo Treu,Ilaria Gandin,Federico Garoia,Marco Merlo,Giulia Cisotto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper evaluates two VAE-derived ECG representations for detecting LGE+ (scar) in cardiomyopathic patients, finding ECGx.AI outperforms a shallower β-VAE, with DTW reconstruction errors showing promise as scar markers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Late Gadolinium Enhancement (LGE) on cardiac MRI is an important marker for myocardial scar but has limited accessibility, motivating the need for more accessible ECG-based screening methods to identify patients with scarring.

Method: The study evaluated 300 subjects from a local cohort, comparing 32-dimensional features from the foundation ECGx.AI model with a shallower β-VAE trained on normal PTB-XL ECGs. They assessed downstream classification using Random Forest and Gradient Boosting, and analyzed Dynamic Time Warping (DTW)-based reconstruction errors with Logistic Regression.

Result: ECGx.AI achieved AUC-ROC of 0.686 with Random Forest, while the proposed β-VAE reached 0.577 with Gradient Boosting (sensitivity 0.775). DTW-reconstruction errors showed significant differences between LGE+ and LGE- groups in 10 out of 12 leads (Mann-Whitney U test), achieving AUC-ROC of 0.643 with Logistic Regression.

Conclusion: VAE-derived ECG representations show potential for detecting myocardial scar, with ECGx.AI performing better overall, but DTW reconstruction errors from β-VAE provide complementary value as markers of scar-related ECG alterations for accessible screening.

Abstract: Late Gadolinium Enhancement (LGE) on cardiac magnetic resonance is a key marker of myocardial scar, but its limited accessibility motivates routine ECG-based screening. We evaluated whether $β$-variational autoencoder (VAE)-derived ECG representations can discriminate LGE+ from LGE- cardiomyopathic patients in a local cohort of 300 subjects. We compared 32-dimensional features from the foundation ECGx.AI model with those from a shallower $β$-VAE trained on normal PTB-XL ECGs, evaluating downstream classification and Dynamic Time Warping (DTW)-based reconstruction errors. ECGx.AI reached an area under ROC of 0.686 with Random Forest, while the proposed $β$-VAE reached 0.577 with sensitivity of 0.775 with Gradient Boosting. Notably, DTW-reconstruction errors significantly differed between classes in 10 out of 12 leads according to Mann-Whitney U test and help in classification, leading to an area under ROC of 0.643 with Logistic Regression, supporting their potential as markers of scar-related ECG alterations.

</details>


### [181] [How Does mHC Use Its Residual Streams? Selective Routing and Near-Identity Mixing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05309)
*Pengxiang Zhao,Xing Li,Xianzhi Yu,Wei Guo,Zhenhua Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how DeepSeek-V4-Flash uses its four-stream Hyper-Connections, finding that blocks rarely use all four streams, routing is concentrated to about two streams per site, late residual mixing provides minimal benefit, and early mixing is more important but has stable patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how trained models utilize the capacity of Hyper-Connections (and their manifold-constrained variant mHC) that widen residual pathways from one stream to n streams, specifically examining: 1) how broadly blocks read and write across streams, 2) how strongly residual pathways mix streams, and 3) whether streams carry distinct representations.

Method: Examination using three metrics: effective stream counts, cross-stream residual weights, and inter-stream cosine similarity on the four-stream residual pathway of DeepSeek-V4-Flash. Also conducted targeted interventions including replacing mixers with identity, fixing mixers to diagnostic means, and limiting routing to largest weights.

Result: 1) Read/write routing is concentrated to about two streams per attention/FFN site; 2) Residual mixing is modest, primarily in early layers (22-42 mostly carry streams separately); 3) Late mixer replacement only increases perplexity by 1.9% while early replacement increases by 41%; 4) Fixing early mixers to means increases perplexity only 0.2%; 5) Limiting to three largest weights per token increases perplexity at most 2.7%.

Conclusion: The model uses only part of the flexibility offered by four-stream mHC: individual blocks rarely require all four streams, representations remain directionally distinct, late residual mixing provides little measured benefit, and the model could potentially be simplified without significant performance loss on evaluated metrics.

Abstract: Hyper-Connections and their manifold-constrained variant mHC widen a residual pathway from one stream to n, yet how trained models use this capacity remains unclear: how broadly blocks read and write, how strongly the residual pathway mixes streams, and whether the streams carry distinct representations. We examine these properties in the four-stream residual pathway of DeepSeek-V4-Flash using effective stream counts, cross-stream residual weights, and inter-stream cosine similarity. Read/write routing is concentrated but varies across depth: a typical attention or FFN site effectively uses about two streams, while the dominant stream changes across layers and the representations remain directionally distinct. Residual mixing is modest and occurs primarily in early layers; in layers 22-42, the pathway mostly carries each stream forward separately. Targeted interventions establish the functional significance of these patterns. Replacing the late mixers by identity increases C4 perplexity by only 1.9% and preserves the six-task average score, whereas replacing the early mixers increases perplexity by 41%. Fixing each early mixer to its C4 diagnostic mean increases perplexity by only 0.2% and reduces the average score by 0.25 percentage points, showing that its site-specific structure matters more than its token-wise variation on the evaluated metrics. Likewise, retaining the three largest routing weights per token at every site increases perplexity by at most 2.7% and changes the average score by at most 0.4 points. Thus, the studied model realizes only part of the flexibility afforded by four-stream mHC: individual blocks rarely require all four streams, and late residual mixing provides little measured benefit.

</details>


### [182] [Optimal Rates for Agentic Networked Information Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05318)
*MohammadHossein Bateni,Zahra Hadizadeh,MohammadTaghi Hajiaghayi,Mahdi JafariRaviz,Shayan Taherijam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper closes the gap in understanding information aggregation in networked learning by determining the exact Θ(M²/D) rate of excess mean squared error for linear regression in M-covered DAGs, extending results to logistic classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research builds on prior work by Kearns, Roth, and Ryu to study information aggregation in networked learning models that capture patterns in agentic AI, where each agent only sees partial data and passes conclusions forward. The motivation is to understand the precise error rates in these distributed learning systems.

Method: The authors provide a sharper analysis of existing cyclic instances, construct new M-covered path instances for different depths, prove geometric error contraction for fixed distributions, and extend results to logistic classification via logit-passing model while relating it to linear regression through rescaling arguments.

Result: The paper closes the gap between previous bounds, showing the correct rate is constant up to depth M² and Θ(M²/D) beyond it. They provide improved lower bounds (Ω(√(M/D)) for D<M² and Ω(M²/D) for D≥M²), show geometric contraction for fixed distributions, and prove the same optimal rate holds for logistic classification.

Conclusion: The paper establishes optimal error rates for information aggregation in networked learning models, settling open questions from prior work and demonstrating the fundamental limitations and capabilities of distributed learning systems where agents see only partial features and pass predictions forward.

Abstract: Building on the pioneering paper of Kearns, Roth, and Ryu (SODA'26), we study information aggregation in a networked learning model. The model captures a central pattern in agentic AI: each agent sees only part of the data and passes on only its own conclusion. Their model considers a linear regression problem with the mean squared error (MSE) loss. Agents sit in a DAG and each sees only a subset of the features and its parents' predictions, fits a linear predictor, and passes only its prediction forward. The benchmark is the full-feature learner that sees all raw features. A path of depth $D$ is $M$-covered if every block of $M$ consecutive agents collectively sees all raw features. Kearns, Roth, and Ryu proved that the excess mean squared error of the last agent on such a path is $O(M/\sqrt D)$, and gave a cyclic instance with excess error $Ω(M/D)$ for $D<M^2$.
  We close this gap: the correct rate is constant up to depth $M^2$, and $Θ(M^2/D)$ beyond it. We first give a sharper analysis of the cyclic instance and improve its lower bound to $Ω(\sqrt{M/D})$ for $D<M^2$. We then construct, for every depth $D\ge M^2$, an $M$-covered path of depth $D$ with excess error $Ω(M^2/D)$. The same instance gives the constant lower bound for all $D < M^2$. We also show that for any fixed distribution the excess error contracts geometrically along the path, ruling out any single instance that witnesses any polynomial lower bound at every depth.
  Finally, we prove the same optimal rate for logistic classification in the logit-passing model of Bateni et al., which considers the binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss. The same improved upper bound of $O(M^2/D)$ holds, and we transfer all the regression lower bounds by showing that on those examples the logistic path follows the least-squares path up to rescaling.

</details>


### [183] [Embedded Graph Flows for Categorical Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05328)
*Ethan Ma,Zihan Wang,Chris Siu Yeung Chow,Xinguo Feng,Qingqing Li,Rui Jiang,Naipeng Dong,Guangdong Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EGF learns continuous embeddings for graph categories and generates graphs via noise transport using permutation-equivariant transformers, achieving state-of-the-art performance on molecular benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph generators using fixed one-hot category encodings impose artificial geometry where categories are equidistant, failing to capture meaningful relationships between different node and edge types.

Method: Embedded Graph Flows (EGF) learns continuous embeddings for node and unordered-edge categories, transports Gaussian noise toward these learned endpoints using permutation-equivariant graph transformers, and uses a terminal readout to map embeddings back to discrete categories.

Result: On QM9, EGF achieved best results on all four reported metrics among three methods (FCD: 0.150 vs. 0.717 for DiGress and 0.812 for GruM). On ZINC250k, EGF retained lowest MMD using NSPDK kernel, indicating close agreement with local substructures of reference molecules.

Conclusion: EGF effectively learns meaningful continuous embeddings for graph categories and generates coherent molecular structures with state-of-the-art performance, demonstrating advantages over categorical diffusion and bridge-based baselines.

Abstract: Generating categorical graphs requires choosing node and edge types that form a coherent structure without depending on node order. Many graph generators encode categories as fixed one-hot vectors, which can impose an artificial geometry in which categories are equidistant. We propose Embedded Graph Flows (EGF), a generative model that learns continuous embeddings for node and unordered-edge categories and transports Gaussian noise towards these learnt endpoints using a permutation-equivariant graph transformer. A terminal readout maps the embeddings back to discrete graph categories. Across molecular benchmarks, EGF achieved competitive performance. On QM9, EGF gives the best result on all four reported metrics among the three methods, including a Fréchet ChemNet Distance (FCD) of 0.150, compared with 0.717 for the categorical-diffusion baseline DiGress and 0.812 for the bridge-based baseline GruM. When applied to larger molecules in ZINC250k, EGF retains the lowest maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) using the neighbourhood subgraph pairwise distance kernel (NSPDK), indicating close agreement with the local substructures of the reference molecules. Our code is available at https://github.com/Trusted-System-Lab/EGF.

</details>


### [184] [Variational Continuation for Double Pendulum Periodic Orbits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05337)
*Leo Yao,Ziming Liu,Max Tegmark*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hessian-based automated continuation method for periodic orbits using Fourier series parameterization and automatic differentiation, eliminating need for hand-derived Jacobians.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for continuing periodic orbits in dynamical systems require hand-derived Jacobians, which is cumbersome and manual. There's a need for more automated, efficient approaches that can handle complex dynamics like double pendulum oscillations and capture orbit behaviors that may be missing in literature.

Method: 1. Parametrize periodic orbits as Fourier series. 2. Define loss function based on deviation from physical differential equations. 3. Use automatic differentiation (machine learning technique) instead of hand-derived Jacobians. 4. Determine continuation direction using flat directions (zero eigenvalues) of loss landscape. 5. Method is integrator-free and can precisely initialize oscillations around unstable fixed points.

Result: Successfully performed full continuations of periodic double pendulum oscillations from fixed points. Showed bifurcations along orbit families and categorized branches of periodic orbits. Discovered novel periodic orbits where both pendulum masses are never simultaneously at rest, which appears to be new to the literature.

Conclusion: The Hessian-based approach with automatic differentiation provides an efficient, automated method for periodic orbit continuation that eliminates manual Jacobian derivation. The method can precisely handle unstable dynamics, detect orbit intersections and bifurcations, and reveals previously undocumented periodic behaviors in complex systems like the double pendulum.

Abstract: We present a Hessian-based approach to numerically continue periodic orbits in dynamical systems. A loop (periodic orbit candidate) is parametrized as a Fourier series; a loss function is defined based on the deviation of the loop from the physical differential equations. Unlike previous work relying on hand-derived Jacobians, our method automates the process by leveraging automatic differentiation, a common machine learning technique. The continuation direction can be determined by the flat directions of the loss landscapes (directions with zero eigenvalues), making the search of periodic orbits efficient and guided. Our method is integrator-free, precisely initializes oscillations around unstable fixed points, and efficiently detects orbit family intersections and subharmonic bifurcations. As a demonstration, we present full continuations of periodic double pendulum oscillations from fixed points, showing bifurcations along orbit families and categorizing branches of periodic orbits. In particular, we find periodic orbits where both pendulum masses are never simultaneously at rest, which to our knowledge has been missing in the literature.

</details>


### [185] [Distill Globally, Adapt Locally: Reasoning Distillation and Product-Type Test-Time Training for Scalable Trade-Up Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05363)
*Siliang Liu,Mohammad Ghasemi,Sapan Patel,Amin Banitalebi-Dehkordi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a two-level LLM distillation framework for efficient trade-up recommendation that distills teacher LLM reasoning into a compact non-generative student model, achieving near-expert performance with dramatically reduced computational cost and latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs can effectively reason about trade-up recommendations (identifying higher-quality alternatives preserving purchase intent), directly applying them to hundreds of millions of product pairs is operationally impractical due to high computational costs, latency, and inference expense.

Method: A two-level framework: Level 1 uses a retrieval-augmented few-shot LLM teacher to generate structured labels and rationales that supervise a compact embedding-pair classifier through alignment and contrastive objectives. Level 2 employs product-type test-time training (PT-TTT) that optimizes lightweight category-specific adapters over the frozen student using few-shot demonstrations.

Result: The 15.5M-parameter distilled student achieves AUC 0.924 on 8,352 human-annotated pairs, outperforming label-only training (AUC 0.912). PT-TTT further improves AUC to 0.941 and average precision to 0.940. The system is ~5,000x faster and ~10,000x lower cost than direct LLM inference on a 100K-pair catalog.

Conclusion: The proposed distillation framework enables efficient, high-quality trade-up recommendation by capturing LLM reasoning in a lightweight model, making industrial-scale deployment practical while maintaining near-expert performance through product-type adaptation.

Abstract: Trade-up recommendation identifies higher-quality alternatives that preserve a customer's purchase intent while offering upgraded benefits. Large language models (LLMs) can reason about such distinctions, but applying them directly to hundreds of millions of product pairs is operationally impractical. We introduce a two-level framework that distills LLM reasoning into an efficient non-generative student and adapts its decision boundary to product-type-specific trade-up criteria. At Level 1, a retrieval-augmented few-shot LLM teacher generates structured relation labels and natural-language rationales. These rationales supervise a compact embedding-pair classifier through alignment and contrastive objectives; at inference, the student uses only two precomputed 768-dimensional product embeddings, with no LLM calls or text generation. On a fixed human-annotated benchmark of 8,352 pairs, a 15.5M-parameter four-class reasoning-distilled student achieves AUC 0.924 (95% CI [0.918, 0.929]), compared with 0.912 for the four-class label-only student. At Level 2, product-type test-time training (PT-TTT) uses few-shot demonstrations to optimize lightweight category-specific adapters over the frozen student. PT-TTT improves AUC from 0.924 to 0.941 and average precision from 0.920 to 0.940. On a 100K-pair proxy catalog, the distilled student on a single eight-GPU machine is approximately 5,000x faster and 10,000x lower in estimated cost than direct LLM inference.

</details>


### [186] [RegionFed: Federated Learning for Personalized Query Understanding in Heterogeneous Retail Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05403)
*Quoc H. Nguyen,Ali Lafzi,Abhijeet Phatak,Siddharth Pratap Singh,Rohit Upadhyay,Yogananda Domlur Seetharama,Chittaranjan Tripathy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RegionFed is a gradient-level federated learning framework that addresses regional heterogeneity in retail search by avoiding catastrophic failures of parameter-level methods on transformers, while maintaining privacy and achieving near-centralized performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retail search systems face geographic heterogeneity in query patterns, vocabularies, and preferences, creating challenges for privacy-preserving training and model personalization. Federated learning addresses privacy but standard FL produces global models that sacrifice regional performance, while existing personalized FL methods catastrophically fail on modern transformers due to parameter-level conflicts with tied embeddings and LayerNorm.

Method: RegionFed operates at the gradient level (not parameter level) and uses L2 conflict between regional and global gradients as a unified signal to: (1) diagnose heterogeneity, (2) route regions to cheapest sufficient personalization strategy, and (3) adaptively control personalization strength. It treats models as differentiable black boxes, enabling zero-code deployment across various architectures (T5, RoBERTa, CNN).

Result: RegionFed-Meta achieves 92.27% accuracy across three public datasets (Amazon ESCI, Amazon Reviews, LEAF-FEMNIST) and four architectures, closing the gap to the privacy-violating centralized upper bound (92.04%, Δ=0.23pp). It works where parameter-level methods collapse (<10% accuracy on T5), provides (ε≈0.60)-differential privacy, and guarantees O(1/√T) convergence.

Conclusion: RegionFed demonstrates that gradient-level personalization is architecture-robust and overcomes the catastrophic failures of parameter-level approaches on transformers. It enables effective personalized federated learning for retail search systems while maintaining privacy guarantees and achieving near-centralized performance.

Abstract: Retail search systems serve diverse geographic regions with distinct query patterns, vocabularies, and product preferences, creating significant data heterogeneity that challenges both privacy-preserving training and model personalization. Federated learning offers a natural solution for privacy, but standard FL methods produce global models that sacrifice regional performance, while existing personalized FL approaches operate at the parameter level and catastrophically collapse on modern transformers (below 10\% accuracy on T5) due to tied embeddings and LayerNorm interactions. We introduce RegionFed, an \textit{architecture-robust} federated learning framework that sidesteps this failure by operating entirely at the gradient level. RegionFed uses the $\ell_2$ conflict between regional and global gradients as a unified signal that (i) diagnoses heterogeneity, (ii) routes each region to the cheapest sufficient personalization strategy, and (iii) adaptively controls personalization strength. Because it treats models as differentiable black boxes, RegionFed deploys on T5-Small, T5-3B, RoBERTa, and CNN with zero code changes, providing large gains on transformers (where parameter-level methods collapse) and consistent improvements on CNNs. Across three public datasets (Amazon ESCI, Amazon Reviews, LEAF-FEMNIST) and four architectures, RegionFed-Meta achieves 92.27\%, closing the gap to the privacy-violating centralized upper bound (Centralized + Regional Weighting: 92.04\%, $Δ$=0.23pp, within 1$σ$) while providing $(ε{\approx}0.60)$-differential privacy and $\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{T})$ convergence.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [187] [At Equal Inference Cost, Multi-Agent Structure Does Not Beat a Single Frozen Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04217)
*David Dylan,Aoife Brennan,Cian Murphy,Niamh O'Sullivan,Conor Kelly,Saoirse Walsh*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Multi-agent LLM teams like Planner-Executor-Critic often outperform single agents but at higher inference cost. This study shows that when controlling for total model calls, evolving multi-agent teams doesn't provide clear benefits over evolving single agents, with gains mainly coming from executor evolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous research on multi-agent LLM pipelines (Planner-Executor-Critic teams) shows performance gains but at higher inference costs. Existing methods compare teams against single agents at equal environment rollouts, giving teams extra compute. The authors want to examine whether multi-agent teams still beat single agents when controlling for total inference budget (model calls).

Method: The authors introduce MA-Evolve, which represents a Planner-Executor-Critic team as three evolvable role prompts and optimizes them by per-role coordinate ascent over a shared frozen 7B backbone. They compare performance under fixed total language-model calls budget. They conduct experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop environments, analyzing the contributions of each role through leave-one-in analysis.

Result: On ALFWorld, evolving a single executor significantly improves over unevolved agent, while the full team achieves slightly higher mean (0.769 vs 0.754) but not statistically better (p=0.80) despite using 1.8x more evaluation calls. Leave-one-in analysis shows realized value comes entirely from executor; planner and critic evolve to empty/low-impact prompts. With 2-3x free compute, team only matches single agent. On WebShop, evolution is null while team trends worse.

Conclusion: Under equal inference cost constraints, multi-agent structure adds computational overhead without clear performance benefits. Most gains come from executor evolution alone, suggesting simpler single-agent optimization may be more cost-effective than complex multi-agent teams when inference budget is limited.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM pipelines, such as Planner-Executor-Critic teams, often report gains over single agents, but these gains usually come with higher inference cost because the team makes multiple model calls per environment step. Existing automated methods search over roles, topologies, and prompts, but typically compare teams against single agents at equal environment rollouts, giving the team extra compute. We instead fix the total number of language-model calls and ask whether evolving a multi-agent team still beats evolving a single agent under the same budget.
  We introduce MA-Evolve, which represents a Planner-Executor-Critic team as three evolvable role prompts and optimizes them by per-role coordinate ascent over a shared frozen 7B backbone. On ALFWorld, evolving a single executor significantly improves over the unevolved agent, while the full team achieves the highest mean but is not statistically better than the single agent: 0.769 versus 0.754, p = 0.80, despite using 1.8 times more evaluation calls. Leave-one-in analysis shows that the realized value comes entirely from the executor; the planner and critic evolve to empty or low-impact prompts and rarely change the executor's action. With 2-3 times free compute, the team only matches the single agent, and on WebShop evolution is null while the team trends worse. Under equal inference cost, multi-agent structure adds cost without clear benefit.

</details>


### [188] [Multi-dimensional Bias in Modeling Multi-dimensional Preferences: Evaluating the Ability of Synthetic Agents to Replace Human Participants in Conjoint Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04243)
*Ho Ting Hung,Nachiket Midha,Victor Y. Wu,Yiwen Zhang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: LLMs show mixed results as synthetic participants in conjoint survey experiments - they can reproduce some aggregate patterns but not fully replace human respondents due to inconsistent performance across different validation dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's growing interest in using LLMs to improve robustness or reduce costs in survey experiments, but their efficacy in conjoint design - a popular method in political science - remains underexplored. The paper addresses this gap by investigating whether synthetic agents can reproduce the multi-dimensional human preference patterns that conjoint experiments are designed to capture.

Method: The paper replicates published conjoint studies and compares results generated by synthetic agents with original human data along three dimensions: representational correspondence, inferential correspondence, and procedural stability. It evaluates alignment of choice distributions, statistical and substantive similarity of estimates.

Result: Results are uneven across these dimensions and replicated studies. Synthetic agents can sometimes reproduce figures or show strong sign agreement, but they don't consistently achieve the full range of validation criteria needed to replace human respondents. Performance varies across different studies and validation metrics.

Conclusion: The validity of synthetic participants should be considered claim-dependent and hierarchical. Reproducing aggregate outputs is insufficient to support replacing human respondents. The discipline must first map LLMs' boundaries across various levels before considering synthetic agents as robust substitutes for human samples in conjoint experiments.

Abstract: Despite growing interest in using LLMs to add robustness or reduce data-collection costs in survey experiments, their efficacy in conjoint design---an increasingly popular method in political science---remains underexplored. This paper addresses that gap by investigating whether synthetic agents can reproduce the multi-dimensional human preference patterns that conjoint is designed to capture. It replicates published conjoint studies and compares the results generated by synthetic agents with original human data along three dimensions: representational correspondence, inferential correspondence, and procedural stability. Our analysis evaluates the alignment of choice distributions as well as the statistical and substantive similarity of estimates, and the results are uneven across these dimensions and studies replicated. This implies that the validity of synthetic participants should be considered claim-dependent and hierarchical. Reproducing a figure or obtaining strong sign agreement is evidence of similar aggregate outputs, but not enough to support replacing human respondents. Our results suggest that the discipline as a whole must first map this innovation's boundaries across various levels before considering synthetic agents a robust substitute for human samples.

</details>


### [189] [EVOHARNESSBENCH: Can Your Agents Keep Pace with an Evolving Harness?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04280)
*Zixuan Ke,Vaidehi Patil,Haizhou Shi,Yang Li,Ye Liu,Sarath Shekkizhar,Anurag Koul,Jiayu Wang,Xuan Phi Nguyen,Semih Yavuz,Mohit Bansal,Shafiq Joty*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: EVOHARNESSBENCH is a new benchmark for evaluating AI agents as their tool/skill/agent harness evolves, revealing issues like harness-induced forgetting and inconsistent adaptation across capability axes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern LLM-based agents rely on evolving harnesses of tools, skills, and specialist agents, but current benchmarks only handle non-stationarity in tasks, not in the harness itself. There's a need to evaluate agents under controlled harness evolution to understand how they adapt to changing capabilities.

Method: Created EVOHARNESSBENCH with 17 deterministic multi-stage harness streams constructed from verifier-based benchmarks, containing 802 tasks, 520 tools, 42 skills, and 62 agents. Evaluated two settings: deployment evaluation (retention as harness expands) and self-evolving adaptation evaluation (usefulness of accumulated experience with new capabilities).

Result: Revealed three persistent gaps: 1) Harness expansion alone can degrade performance on previously solved tasks (harness-induced forgetting), 2) Gains from self-evolving adaptation remain inconsistent across evolution stages, capability axes, and environments, 3) Retention and adaptation can conflict - preserving earlier competence doesn't necessarily improve adaptation to new capabilities, and vice versa.

Conclusion: Harness evolution represents a distinct challenge for building agents that can keep pace with an evolving harness while preserving previously effective behavior, requiring new approaches beyond existing continual learning benchmarks.

Abstract: Modern LLM-based agents operate through a harness of tools, reusable skills, and specialist agents that shapes what they observe and what they can do. In practice, this harness continually evolves as new capabilities are added. We introduce EVOHARNESSBENCH, a benchmark for evaluating agents under controlled harness evolution across three axes (tools, skills, and agents). Unlike existing continual-learning benchmarks for agents, which typically place non-stationarity (i.e., what changes over time) in the task stream while keeping the harness fixed, EVOHARNESSBENCH places non-stationarity in the externally supplied harness itself. It contains 17 multi-stage harness streams constructed deterministically from verifier-based benchmarks, comprising 802 tasks, 520 tools, 42 skills, and 62 agents. We evaluate two complementary settings corresponding to the central challenges of harness evolution: deployment evaluation, which isolates retention of previously accessible competence as the harness expands, and self-evolving adaptation evaluation, which tests whether accumulated experience remains useful as new capabilities are introduced. Our results reveal three persistent gaps. First, harness expansion alone can degrade performance on previously solved tasks, producing harness-induced forgetting. Second, gains from self-evolving adaptation remain inconsistent across stages of harness evolution, capability axes, and environments. Third, retention and adaptation can pull in different directions: preserving earlier competence does not necessarily improve adaptation to newly introduced capabilities, and vice versa. These results establish harness evolution as a distinct challenge for building agents that can keep pace with an evolving harness while preserving previously effective behavior.

</details>


### [190] [Abstraction Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04303)
*Boning Li,Longbo Huang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A zero-shot pipeline using LLMs to automatically create strategic abstractions for imperfect-information games without domain-specific evaluators, reducing exploitability and transferring across various games.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional game abstraction methods require domain-specific evaluators (like hand-strength calculators) that need expert knowledge and engineering effort, making them unavailable for less-studied games.

Method: A four-phase pipeline: 1) LLM discovers continuous strategic features from natural-language game descriptions using calibration anchors, 2) scores private states on these features, 3) correlation-based feature selection, 4) k-means clustering for abstraction buckets - all without game-specific evaluators, training data, or game-tree traversal.

Result: Reduces lifted-strategy exploitability by up to 62% relative to expected-hand-strength baseline on HUNL turn endgames; beats scalar rank baseline on ROVER Trials (original game); transfers successfully to PLO, HUNL preflop/flop, and Riichi Mahjong with discovered features tracking recognized strategic concepts.

Conclusion: Demonstrates structured knowledge elicitation: converting implicit strategic knowledge in LLMs into explicit numerical features for algorithmic computation, enabling automatic abstraction construction for diverse imperfect-information games without expert knowledge.

Abstract: Information abstraction, which groups strategically similar private states into a tractable number of buckets, is essential for scaling game-solving algorithms to large imperfect-information games. Constructing effective abstractions, however, has traditionally required domain-specific evaluators such as hand-strength calculators or equity estimators, which demand expert knowledge and engineering effort and are unavailable for most less-studied games. We propose the Abstraction Agent, a zero-shot pipeline that uses a large language model (LLM) to discover continuous strategic features from a natural-language game description, score private states on these features, and cluster them into abstraction buckets, without any game-specific evaluator, training data, or game-tree traversal during abstraction construction. The pipeline runs in four phases: feature discovery with calibration anchors, batched private-state scoring, correlation-based feature selection, and $k$-means clustering. The resulting abstractions reduce lifted-strategy exploitability by up to 62% relative to an expected-hand-strength baseline on heads-up no-limit Texas hold'em (HUNL) turn endgames, and beat a scalar rank baseline at every granularity on ROVER Trials, an original game absent from any pretraining corpus. Beyond these quantitative benchmarks, the pipeline transfers with unchanged prompts to four-card Pot-Limit Omaha, HUNL preflop and flop, and Riichi Mahjong, where the discovered features track each game's recognized strategic concepts. This is structured knowledge elicitation: converting implicit strategic knowledge in LLM parameters into explicit numerical features for downstream algorithmic computation. The code is available at https://github.com/lbn187/AbstractionAgent.

</details>


### [191] [Online Change-point Detection for Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05298)
*Fatemeh Saberi Khomami,Julita Vassileva*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: PPR is a lightweight, reward-based change-point detection algorithm for cooperative multi-agent RL that identifies environmental or objective changes during training by smoothing agents' reward streams and applying statistical drift detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperative MARL systems need to recognize when environment or task objectives change during training, as past experience becomes unreliable. Current methods lack efficient online change-point detection capabilities.

Method: PPR (Patterns of Past Rewards) - an algorithm-agnostic detector that smooths agents' return streams, highlights recent changes, and applies statistical drift detection to flag significant shifts.

Result: PPR shows balanced performance between detection speed and alarm stability. It limits redundant detections while successfully identifying controlled shifts, outperforming both raw-return detection (which often misses shifts) and smoothed-return baselines (which detect early but produce many repeated alarms).

Conclusion: PPR is an effective lightweight, reward-based monitoring tool that enables cooperative MARL systems to reliably identify major changes during training, offering a practical solution for online change-point detection.

Abstract: Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) systems rely on past experience for learning coordinated behaviour, but this experience may become unreliable if the environment or task objective changes during training. In such cases, agents first need a way to recognize that the situation has changed before deciding how to adapt. This paper studies online change-point detection for cooperative MARL using reward-derived signals. We propose \emph{Patterns of Past Rewards} (PPR), a lightweight algorithm-agnostic detector that smooths agents' return streams, highlights recent changes, and applies a statistical drift detector to flag significant shifts. We evaluate PPR in a custom Speaker-Listener environment based on the Multi-Agent Particle Environment under two controlled non-stationarity scenarios. Our results show a trade-off between detection speed and alarm stability. A smoothed-return baseline detects earlier but produces many repeated alarms. In contrast, applying the detector directly to raw returns often misses the shift. PPR offers a more balanced approach by limiting redundant detections while still identifying the controlled shifts. These findings highlight PPR as a lightweight, reward-based monitoring tool that enables cooperative MARL systems to reliably identify major changes during training.

</details>


### [192] [Trust-Aware Adaptive Disclosure for Inference Privacy Preservation in Multi-Agent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05340)
*Puspanjali Ghoshal,Tobias J. Oechtering*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A Trust-Aware Privacy Control framework for multi-agent systems that balances consensus performance with privacy preservation against goal inference attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent systems are increasingly used in information-critical domains like healthcare and smart grids, where agents have latent goals that need protection from adversarial inference attacks. There's a need to maintain privacy while still achieving system consensus.

Method: Proposed a Trust-Aware Privacy Control framework that adaptively controls message disclosure based on dynamic trust relationships between agents. Uses a trust-dependent stochastic policy to manage information release, creating a tradeoff between consensus performance and privacy preservation.

Result: Experiments show the method reduces adversarial goal inference accuracy compared to baseline methods while maintaining competitive consensus utility, demonstrating effective privacy preservation.

Conclusion: Trust-aware mechanisms are effective for privacy preservation in multi-agent systems, enabling agents to protect their latent goals from inference attacks while still achieving system consensus objectives.

Abstract: Agent based systems are increasingly deployed in information critical systems including healthcare management systems, and smart grids. In this paper, we consider a multi-agent system where each agent has a latent goal that needs to be kept hidden from observing adversaries. More specifically, this paper studies privacy-preserving consensus in networked multi-agent systems under goal inference attacks. We propose a Trust-Aware Privacy Control framework that adapts message disclosure based on the dynamic trust relationships between agents. The proposed method controls information release using a trust-dependent stochastic policy. This enables a tradeoff between consensus performance and privacy preservation. Experiments demonstrate that the proposed method reduces adversarial goal inference accuracy compared to representative baselines, while maintaining competitive consensus utility, thereby highlighting the effectiveness of trust-aware mechanisms in privacy preservation of the agents in multi-agent systems.

</details>
